	• The object of philosophy is not just God, but also religion
	• God is not just a sterile abstraction, but a living being, spirit (Geist)

â€¢ ••Understanding God as Spirit••
	• God can only be genuinely understood in the mode of his being as spirit
	• This means thinking of God as present in the community of faith

â€¢ ••Reconciliation of Natural and Revealed Theology••
	• Hegel aims to reconcile natural and revealed theology
	• Christianity as the revelatory religion embraces both reason and revelation

â€¢ ••Hegel's Approach to Theology••
	• Hegel believes that philosophy can deal with religion more impartially and fruitfully
	• He aims to establish a more positive relationship between philosophy and theology

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God and Religion are Relational Concepts••
	• God is God only in relation to human knowledge and faith
	• Religion is the relationship between God and human consciousness

â€¢ ••Philosophy of Religion's Task••
	• Not to produce religion, but to comprehend and interpret it
	• Religion is intrinsic to human beings and distinguishes them from animals

â€¢ ••Relationship between Religion and Human Worldview••
	• Philosophy of religion investigates the relationship between religion and human consciousness, cognition, purposes, and interests

â€¢ ••Discord of Modernity••
	• Philosophy of religion encounters the characteristic discord of modernity in its investigation of this relationship

â€¢ ••Philosophy of Religion as a Subdiscipline••
	• A subdiscipline of philosophy as a whole
	• Thematizes the very heart of philosophy

â€¢ ••Philosophy's Object••
	• God is the only proper object of philosophy
	• Philosophy is a cognitive knowledge of the non-worldly, eternal, and divine

â€¢ ••Hegel's Idealism••
	• Not antithetical to realism, but understands ideality as the truth of the finite
	• Ideality overreaches and embraces the real

â€¢ ••Philosophy and Theology••
	• Hegel rejects the distinction between philosophy and theology as worldly and divine wisdom
	• The proper object of philosophy and religion is the same, but with different names (absolute idea vs. God)

â€¢ ••God as Spirit••
	• God is not only the essence that maintains itself in thought, but also the essence that appears and endows itself with revelation and objectivity
	• The name of God as spirit is both conceptual and representational, philosophical and religious

â€¢ ••Philosophy of Religion's Task••
	• To bring about a reconciliation of philosophy and religion by thematizing the spirituality of God
	• To focus on the absolute in itself and its process of production as spirit

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God as the foundation of philosophy••
	• God is both the beginning and end of philosophy
	• The whole of philosophy is a proof of God
	• Philosophy of religion makes God the starting point and focuses on the appearing of God in specific religions

â€¢ ••The philosophy of religion project••
	• The project aims to understand God as the utterly concrete idea
	• It is the last of the philosophical sciences
	• It focuses on the appearing of God in specific religions

â€¢ ••The modern doctrine of unknowing God••
	• The modern doctrine that humans can know nothing of God undercuts the philosophy of religion project
	• This doctrine has become a settled prejudice in our time
	• It cuts out the very heart of philosophy itself

â€¢ ••The contraction of knowledge of God••
	• The more knowledge of finite things has expanded, the more the sphere of the knowledge of God has contracted
	• Our age has renounced the need to know God
	• We are done with the efforts to satisfy this need

â€¢ ••The secular age••
	• Our age is essentially secular
	• The religious injunction "You shall know God" is accounted mere folly
	• The wisdom of our time has made of God an infinite phantom far removed from our consciousness

â€¢ ••The opposition between religious and secular consciousness••
	• There is a gulf between a secular world and a religious world
	• Sunday is set off from the rest of the days of the week
	• This opposition is even more pronounced in modern natural science

â€¢ ••The isolation of religion from the rest of life••
	• No such isolation is possible in true piety
	• The fact that such an isolation has occurred is the discord of our times
	• This theme is returned to at the end of the lecture manuscript

â€¢ ••The plague of the present age••
	• The contention that reason cannot know God is a plague of the present age
	• This is also the contention that reason cannot in the strict sense know any truth at all

â€¢ ••The doctrine of double truth••
	• This doctrine is a traditional form of the modern discord
	• It is philosophically and religiously impossible
	• There cannot be two kinds of reason, a divine and a human

â€¢ ••The impossibility of two kinds of reason••
	• Human reason is reason general
	• There cannot be two kinds of spirit
	• The doctrine of double truth is a direct contradiction to the whole nature of the Christian religion

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of God••
	• God is present and omnipresent in the world as spirit
	• God is a living, effective, and active presence in the world

â€¢ ••Religion as a Product of the Divine Spirit••
	• Religion is a begetting of the divine spirit, not an invention of human beings
	• God rules the world as reason

â€¢ ••The Catholic Church's Approach to Reason••
	• The Catholic Church binds reason strictly to the authority of the church
	• This approach avoids the cleavage between faith and reason

â€¢ ••The Protestant Church's Approach to Reason••
	• The Protestant Church accepted the doctrine of double truth
	• This cleavage between faith and reason led to the development of modern agnosticism and atheism

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of Previous Theologies••
	• Hegel judges that modern theology lacks the resources to provide a rational articulation and defense of central Christian doctrines
	• Theology has acceded to the discordant presuppositions of modernity

â€¢ ••Metaphysical Theology••
	• Metaphysical theology assumes that objects simply are as they are brought before consciousness
	• It conceives God as an other-worldly supreme being, an abstract essence of the understanding

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of Metaphysical Theology••
	• Hegel accepts Kant's critique of precritical ontotheology
	• He applies this critique to Protestant scholasticism and natural theologies

â€¢ ••Rational Theology••
	• Rational theology is based on the disjunctive categories of the understanding
	• It uses the method of petty argumentation rather than holistic conceptual thinking

â€¢ ••The Enlightenment's Influence on Theology••
	• The Enlightenment's theology turns against the content of specific dogmas
	• It is based on the disjunctive categories of the understanding

â€¢ ••Hegel's Alternative Approach••
	• Hegel's proposed alternative does not entail a return to traditional metaphysical theology
	• It involves a holistic, dialectical thinking of reason

Here are 10 bullets summarizing the text with sub-bullets:

â€¢ ••Hegel's views on the proofs of God••
	• He rejects the cosmological and teleological proofs
	• He accepts the ontological proof, but only when properly interpreted
	• He disagrees with Kant's turn to practical reason as a ground of belief in God

â€¢ ••Hegel's critique of Kant's teleological arguments••
	• Kant's arguments reduce God to a trivial, contingent, and finite being
	• Hegel argues that this is a result of using Verstand (understanding) rather than Vernunft (reason)

â€¢ ••Hegel's discussion of theological rationalism••
	• He attends to the exegesis and dogmatics of theological rationalism
	• He critiques the rationalistic arguments of theologians such as J.F. Ro hr and H.E.G. Paulus

â€¢ ••Rational exegesis••
	• Professes to promote an understanding of the Word of God in scripture
	• In reality, introduces the interpreter's own thoughts and prejudices

â€¢ ••The limitations of rational theology••
	• Reason can have no cognition of God, a presupposition at variance with the biblical writers
	• Rational theology exhibits a generally negative tendency towards any content at all in regard to the nature of God

â€¢ ••The nature of God in rational theology••
	• God is reduced to an empty and dead supreme being
	• This is in contrast to the concrete living spirit of church theology, as expressed in the doctrine of the Trinity

â€¢ ••Hegel's critique of rational exegesis••
	• Rational exegesis frequently allows the literal historical sense of scripture to stand
	• It remains oblivious to the presence of myth, symbol, ritual, and constructive theological imagination in religious writings

â€¢ ••The role of myth and symbol in scripture••
	• Rational exegesis neglects the presence of myth and symbol in scripture
	• This is exemplified by David Friedrich Strauss's critique of rational exegesis

â€¢ ••The limitations of abstract categories in theology••
	• Abstract categories are incapable of grasping dialectical distinctions and relations
	• This is why theological rationalism has neglected or rejected the doctrine of the Trinity

â€¢ ••Hegel's approach to theology••
	• He seeks to understand the nature of God as a concrete living spirit
	• He argues that this requires a dialectical approach, rather than a rationalistic one.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of Rational Theology••
	• Hegel criticizes rational theologians for neglecting the doctrine of the Trinity and designating God as a supreme being.
	• This criticism is directed particularly at Schleiermacher, but also at deistic literature and Neopietist theologians.

â€¢ ••Hegel's Quip on Rational Theologians••
	• Hegel compares rational theologians to an Englishman who doesn't know he's speaking prose.
	• This quip suggests that rational theologians are unaware of their own active thinking and its implications for understanding God.

â€¢ ••Historical Theology••
	• Hegel criticizes theologians who focus solely on the historical development of doctrines.
	• This approach is seen as a form of theological rationalism that neglects the present, existential significance of Christian dogmas.

â€¢ ••The Problem with Historical Theology••
	• Hegel argues that this approach trivializes the doctrine of Christ and the Trinity.
	• It reduces the doctrine of Christ to a merely human figure and the Trinity to mere decorative timbering.

â€¢ ••Hegel's Targets••
	• Hegel's targets include rationalist historians of dogma, such as J. L. Mosheim and J. S. Semler.
	• He also targets Neopietist theologians, such as F. A. G. Tholuck, who track trinitarian doctrine to pagan sources.

â€¢ ••Rationalism and Pietism••
	• Rationalism and pietism are able to make common cause in their fear and suspicion of speculative theology.
	• This shared suspicion is seen as a weakness in both approaches.

â€¢ ••Theological Agnosticism••
	• Theological agnosticism is a view that God cannot be known cognitively.
	• This view is associated with Kant, who argues that theoretical reason is limited to objects of sense experience.

â€¢ ••Ethicotheology••
	• Kant's alternative to ontotheology, cosmotheology, and physicotheology is ethicotheology.
	• Ethicotheology provides the only valid proof of God, namely that God is a necessary postulate of action in accord with moral ends.

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of Ethicotheology••
	• Hegel argues that ethicotheology reduces God to an abstraction, a supreme being, a vacuum, or a beyond.
	• This approach has no means of comprehending God as a living spirit.

â€¢ ••Hegel's Alternative••
	• Hegel's alternative to rationalism, pietism, and ethicotheology is speculative theology.
	• This approach seeks to comprehend God as a living spirit, rather than reducing God to an abstraction.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of Nonknowing••
	• Hegel rejects the philosophy of nonknowing, which claims that reason cannot cognize God or any truth.
	• He argues that this view is a plague on the present age.

â€¢ ••Hegel's View on Religion and Ethics••
	• Hegel believes that religion cannot be reduced to a means to an end other than itself.
	• He argues that religion is its own end, and that it cannot be demonstrated to be necessary for ethical action.

â€¢ ••Kantian Agnosticism and Atheism••
	• Hegel believes that the logical outcome of Kantian agnosticism and ethically based theism is thoroughgoing atheism.
	• He argues that a divine guarantor of the kingdom of ends is not needed.

â€¢ ••Critique of Ethicotheology••
	• Hegel's critique of ethicotheology is especially pressing in light of the turn to ethics by Emmanuel Levinas and other critics of Hegelian ontotheology.
	• A close connection exists between religion and ethics, as Hegel himself recognizes.

â€¢ ••Religion as the Substantial Foundation of Ethical Life••
	• Hegel believes that religion serves as the substantial foundation of ethical life.
	• Divine redemption is at work in social and political institutions as well as in individual hearts.

â€¢ ••The Element of Mystery in Speculative Knowledge••
	• Hegel recognizes the element of mystery or non-knowing in speculative knowledge of God.
	• This element drives thinking beyond understanding to reason.

â€¢ ••Apophasis and Kataphasis••
	• The apophatic moment prevents kataphatic theology from becoming dogmatic and authoritarian.
	• The kataphatic moment prevents apophatic theology from dissolving into silence or ethical postulates.

â€¢ ••Preservation of the Apophatic Element••
	• It can be argued that the apophatic element is preserved in Hegel's distinctive way of moving through and beyond Kant's critique of theoretical reason.
	• Hegel radicalizes the doctrine of antinomies, which gives rise to the dialectical nature of thinking.

â€¢ ••Theology of Feeling••
	• Hegel develops his critique of the theology of feeling, which emphasizes the importance of subjective experience and feeling in religious experience.
	• This theology is often associated with Friedrich Schleiermacher.

â€¢ ••Hegel's Considered View••
	• Hegel's considered view is that religion is not a means to non-religious ends, but rather serves as the substantial foundation of ethical life.
	• Divine redemption is at work in social and political institutions as well as in individual hearts.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Hegel's Critique of Schleiermacher's Theology of Feeling••
	• Hegel argues that Schleiermacher's theology reduces God to a hollow abstraction
	• This is because Schleiermacher claims that we can have no cognition of God
	• God has no inward determinations, making God a supreme being without attributes
• ••Schleiermacher's View of Feeling••
	• For Schleiermacher, feeling is the source of religion
	• Consciousness of God is sought only in the form of feeling
	• The relationship between the human spirit and God is confined to the sphere of feeling
• ••Hegel's Dismissive Reading of Schleiermacher••
	• Hegel does not provide a close reading of Schleiermacher's Christian Faith
	• Hegel fails to acknowledge the distinction between ordinary feeling and religious feeling
• ••Schleiermacher's Distinction between Ordinary and Religious Feeling••
	• Schleiermacher distinguishes between ordinary feeling and religious feeling
	• Religious feeling is a feeling of absolute or utter dependence on God
	• This feeling is the condition of possibility for feelings of relative dependence and freedom in the world
• ••Hegel's Critique of Schleiermacher's View of Feeling••
	• Hegel argues that Schleiermacher's view of feeling is too broad, including animal feelings
	• Feeling is not just a physical sensation or emotion, but a form of cognition
	• This form of cognition is prereflective and grounds the human way of being in the world
• ••Schleiermacher's View of Knowledge of God••
	• Schleiermacher claims that we can know only our relation to God, not what God is
	• Our relation to God points to religion, but does not reveal God's nature and attributes
• ••Hegel's View of God as Spirit••
	• Hegel argues that God is inseparable from God's relations
	• The being of God is not separable from the religious relationship to God
	• This is what it means to conceive God as spirit: spirit is always for spirit and thus intrinsically relational
• ••Hegel's Critique of Schleiermacher's Theology••
	• Hegel argues that Schleiermacher's theology does not attain to the cognition of God as spirit
	• As a consequence, Schleiermacher's theology de-emphasizes the doctrine of the Trinity
• ••Schleiermacher's System of Divine Attributes••
	• Schleiermacher develops a system of divine attributes, but these do not describe the divine nature
	• These attributes are aspects of our relationship to God, not God's nature
• ••Hegel's Attention to Jacobi in 1827 Lectures••
	• Hegel's attention turns to Jacobi, who emphasizes that religion entails immediacy
	• God is revealed immediately in the consciousness of human beings, and this immediate knowing is called faith

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Faith••
	• Faith is qualitatively distinct from other forms of knowledge
	• It entails an immediate certainty that God is, but cannot judge what God is
	• Theology must move beyond faith to a mediated knowledge of God's objective being

â€¢ ••Subjectivity in Religion••
	• Subjectivity is an important advance of our age
	• However, subjective consciousness must not become disconnected from its object
	• The risk of modernity is that subjectivity will become one-sided and devoid of content

â€¢ ••Atheism and Pantheism••
	• Atheism is the logical outcome of modern theological tendencies
	• Rationalist, historicist, agnostic, and feeling-based theologies are vulnerable to atheism and/or pantheism
	• These theologies surrender the conviction that God can be known cognitively and that God is spiritually present in the world

â€¢ ••Critique of Theology of Feeling••
	• If God is only known through feeling, then objectivity is lost
	• Materialistic views have been more consistent in denying objectivity to God
	• The result is atheism, where God is seen as a product of feeling and sensation

â€¢ ••Materialistic Views••
	• Materialistic views reduce spirit and thought to feeling and sensation
	• They deny objectivity to God and see God as a product of human needs and emotions
	• Examples of materialistic views include those of Ludwig Feuerbach, Bruno Bauer, Max Stirner, and Karl Marx

â€¢ ••Hegel's Response to Materialistic Views••
	• Hegel insists that God must be understood to be what is independent in and for itself
	• God is not simply rooted in feeling, but is a separate and objective reality

â€¢ ••The Roman Empire and Modernity••
	• Hegel compares the spiritless subjectivity of the Roman Empire to the spiritless subjectivity of modernity
	• Both are characterized by a disconnection from the object and a focus on subjective experience

â€¢ ••The Role of Reason••
	• Reason must not become disconnected from its object
	• The inseparability of subjectivity and the object exists in the subject
	• The risk of modernity is that reason will become one-sided and devoid of content

â€¢ ••The Importance of Objectivity••
	• Objectivity is essential for knowledge and understanding
	• Without objectivity, knowledge becomes subjective and arbitrary
	• Theology must strive to understand God as an objective reality, rather than a product of human emotions and needs

â€¢ ••The Need for Mediated Knowledge••
	• Theology must move beyond faith and immediacy to a mediated knowledge of God's objective being
	• This requires a dialectical approach that takes into account both the subjective and objective aspects of reality

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Hegel's Theology and Materialism••
	• Hegel critiques the theologies of feeling of materialism
	• He argues that they are vulnerable to the reversal of theology into anthropology and idealism into naturalism
• ••Hegel's References to Pantheism••
	• Hegel's references to pantheism are explicit
	• He notes that speculative philosophy had been accused of pantheism by theologians who cannot conceive of God as spirit
• ••The Pantheism Controversy••
	• The controversy centered around an exchange of letters between Jacobi and Moses Mendelssohn
	• It was triggered by G. E. Lessing's alleged deathbed confession
• ••What is Pantheism?••
	• Pantheism is the view that all things simply are God
	• It can also be described as the empiricist, historicist, and naturalistic views
• ••Hegel's Critique of Pantheism••
	• Hegel argues that the usual representation of pantheism focuses on abstract unity rather than spiritual unity
	• This leads to the disappearance of individual things and their actuality
• ••Spinoza's Position••
	• According to Hegel, Spinoza's position is properly described as acosmism, not atheism or pantheism
	• It is not God who is absorbed into the world, but the world into God
• ••Hegel's Definition of God••
	• Hegel defines God as spirit, which preserves distinctions rather than annihilating them
	• This is more accurately described as panentheism, but Hegel does not use this label
• ••Hegel's Approach to Theology••
	• Hegel offers his own approach as an alternative to discredited theologies
	• His approach is characterized as a speculative philosophy of religion and theology
• ••Speculative Philosophy of Religion and Theology••
	• This approach involves a relationship of double mirroring between consciousness and object, thought and being
	• It is a speculative philosophy that consummates in God as absolute spirit
• ••The Problem of Spirit••
	• Neither theists nor atheists have a true comprehension of what spirit means
	• This is a problem that Hegel's speculative philosophy of religion and theology seeks to address

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••God is essentially rational••
	• God is rationality that is alive and self-relating spirit
	• Reason is a dynamic life-force, the heart and soul of the universe
• ••Reason is the medium of the flow of meaning••
	• Meaning flows between subject and object, and from object to subject
	• Thought is the medium of this flow
• ••God is knowable through reason••
	• When we engage in cognitive acts, we are implicitly knowing God
	• Investigating cognition is investigating God
• ••Rationality is the ultimate object of reason••
	• Reason has rationality as its ultimate object
	• The means and end of knowing are one and the same
• ••Rationality is a mystery••
	• Rationality cannot be fully understood by finite categories of the understanding
	• It is the highest mystery, demanding reverent silence and rigorous philosophical speculation
• ••The speculative idea is opposed to the sensible and understandable••
	• The speculative idea is a secret or mystery
	• It is opposed to what is understandable, and therefore requires apophatic moments
• ••God is absolute reason and absolute spirit••
	• God is absolute reason, which is absolute spirit
	• Absolute spirit is knowledge, the determinate rational knowledge of its own self
• ••Religion entails passing over into the region of the absolute••
	• Finite categories of thought are no longer appropriate for absolute truth
	• Representational forms of doctrine must be superseded by conceptual thinking
• ••Conceptual thinking is necessary for understanding absolute truth••
	• The concept grasps the inseparability of distinguished categories and elements
	• The concept requires a deconstruction of representational forms and a reconstruction in conceptual language
• ••Theology must be speculative and philosophical••
	• Theology must be grounded in rigorous philosophical speculation
	• It must be opposed to the sensible and understandable, and require apophatic moments

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Unity of Distinctions••
	• Hegel argues that truth is the unity of all distinctions, including:
		- Immediate and mediated knowledge
		- Subject and object
		- Thought and being
		- Representation and concept
	• These distinctions are not extinguished, but sublated (preserved and transformed)

â€¢ ••The Reconciliation of Reason and Religion••
	• Hegel believes that the antinomy between reason and religion is false
	• Neither rational cognition nor the absolute content of religion should give up anything
	• Christianity is the most advanced in reconciling reason and faith, but still falls short

â€¢ ••Speculative Philosophy of Religion••
	• Hegel's speculative philosophy of religion is closer to positive Christian doctrine than rational theology
	• It preserves more of dogmatics than contemporary dogmatics
	• Classical dogmatics presupposed the rationality of faith and gave its doctrines intellectual formulation

â€¢ ••The Role of Philosophy in Theology••
	• Today, philosophy is pre-eminently what is essentially orthodox, maintaining and preserving the basic truths of Christianity
	• Philosophy protects the dogmas of church doctrine from theologians

â€¢ ••The Importance of Theological Cognition••
	• The only way to learn to think theologically is to engage in acts of theological cognition
	• We must first investigate reason generally, the cognitive capacity or conceptual thought

â€¢ ••The Critique of the Cognitive Capacity••
	• The critique of the cognitive capacity is a stance taken by Kant's philosophy and by our time
	• It was believed that a great discovery had been made, but this was a mistake
	• Reason must be investigated rationally, through rational thinking and cognition

â€¢ ••The Analogy of Going into the Water••
	• The demand to investigate the preconditions of doing philosophy or theology is like wanting to go into the water without learning to swim
	• To learn to swim, one must go into the water (i.e., engage in theological cognition)

â€¢ ••The Fear of Going into the Water••
	• Theology of Hegel's time is afraid to go into the water, preoccupying itself with epistemological prolegomena
	• It fails to provide a satisfactory contemporary alternative to classical metaphysical theology

â€¢ ••The Courage of Conviction and Experimentation••
	• Hegel calls for the courage of conviction and the courage of experimentation in theology
	• This means the determination not to surrender the content of faith and the willingness to experiment with new ideas

â€¢ ••The Nature of Thinking••
	• Thinking is not simply an instrument by which to grasp the truth, but the truth itself in all its beauty and mystery
	• It is the ultimate goal of theological cognition to grasp this truth.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Philosophy of Religion••
	• Divided into three main parts: The Concept of Religion, Determinate Religion, and Consummate or Revelatory Religion
	• This division remains constant across all lecture series

â€¢ ••The Concept of Religion••
	• Represents a representational and scientific presupposition
	• First moment in the philosophical treatment of religion
	• Concerned with the concept of religion as an idea

â€¢ ••Determinate Religion••
	• Posits the concept in determinate reality
	• Elevation and perfection of the concept into the idea as the unity of concept and reality
	• Stages of transition for the concept of religion on the way to its consummation

â€¢ ••Consummate or Revelatory Religion••
	• Consummation of the determinations of the concept
	• Return of the concept into itself, which means the absolute idea (God as spirit in the form of truth and revealedness) is now fully an object of consciousness
	• Occurs in the Christian religion

â€¢ ••Hegel's Logic••
	• Defines the idea as the true in and for itself, the absolute unity of concept and objectivity
	• The idea forms a bridge between the science of logic and the philosophy of nature
	• The idea resolves to let the moment of its particularity go forth freely from itself as nature

â€¢ ••The Idea as a Bridge••
	• Transitions from logic (categories of being, essence, and concept) to reality (nature and spirit)
	• Corresponds theologically to God's creation of the world

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Truth and Method••
	• Truth and method are ultimately one
	• Scientific procedure follows from the movement of the subject matter itself

â€¢ ••The Importance of Critical Thinking••
	• Religion has nothing to fear from bold thought dedicated to the truth
	• The courage of experimentation means recognizing that all our thoughts are really thought-experiments

â€¢ ••The Gift of Thinking••
	• Knowing that our thinking is a construction that reflects an aspect of the inexhaustible mystery
	• Liberates us to think and negotiate with many other constructions

â€¢ ••The Postmodern Version of the Speculative Insight••
	• Recognizes that our thinking is a construction that reflects an aspect of the inexhaustible mystery
	• Liberates us to think and negotiate with many other constructions

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Concept of Religion••
	• The concept of religion is a determinate form that emerges from the concept itself.
	• The concept is like a seed that unfolds into a tree, with the determinate forms being enveloped in a spiritual manner.

â€¢ ••Development of the Concept••
	• The concept develops through its determinate forms, which are initially finite and typified.
	• The concept comes to itself out of its determinateness, becoming the infinite, true concept.

â€¢ ••Return of the Concept to Itself••
	• The return of the concept to itself involves a transcendence of historical determinacy.
	• This return is difficult to understand, as it seems to contradict the historical existence of a religion like Christianity.

â€¢ ••Tension between Historical Forms and Speculative Description••
	• There is a tension between the historical forms of Christianity and the speculative description of it in Hegel's lectures.
	• Hegel privileges the Christian religion as most adequate to the concept, but this is not entirely clear.

â€¢ ••The Concept of God and Humanity••
	• The concept of religion is essentially the concept of God, or of God and humanity together.
	• This togetherness is what Hegel calls spirit, which is active throughout the concept.

â€¢ ••Spirit as Activity••
	• Spirit is active throughout, both in the concept and in its realization in history.
	• Spirit is characterized by self-manifesting, which involves becoming for an other and entering into antithesis.

â€¢ ••Moments of the Life of God••
	• The three main parts of the philosophy of religion correspond to the moments in the life of God as absolute spirit.
	• The same pattern repeats itself in diverse ways within each of the parts.

â€¢ ••The Three Moments of Spirit••
	• The three moments of spirit are:
		- The first moment: spirit that manifests itself, determines itself, and enters into existence.
		- The second moment: spirit that gives itself finitude and becomes the second moment.
		- The third moment: spirit that manifests itself according to its concept, taking its former manifestation back into itself.

â€¢ ••The Rhythm of Spirit••
	• The rhythm of spirit is the eternal life of spirit itself, which involves becoming and being explicitly the way it is implicitly.
	• This rhythm repeats itself in diverse ways within each of the parts of the philosophy of religion.

â€¢ ••The Concept of Religion as a Whole••
	• The concept of religion is a complex and multifaceted concept that involves the development of the concept, the return of the concept to itself, and the moments of the life of God.
	• The concept of religion is essentially the concept of God, or of God and humanity together, and this togetherness is what Hegel calls spirit.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Approach to the Concept of Religion••
	• Hegel's method follows the movement of the subject matter (absolute spirit)
	• His task is to define the concept of religion and examine Christianity as the consummate religion

â€¢ ••The Concept of Religion••
	• Comprises the concept of God (abstract being, knowledge, and worship of God)
	• Will be addressed in Chapter 5

â€¢ ••The Empirical Approach to Religion••
	• Starts with immediate experience or feeling
	• Defines religion as a modification of feeling
	• Limits itself to finite categories of consciousness, unable to arrive at the distinctively religious relationship between finite and infinite spirit

â€¢ ••Limitations of the Empirical Approach••
	• Can never get off the ground, as it cannot reconcile the antithesis between finite and infinite spirit
	• Leads to an impasse, unable to explain how a relationship between the finite and infinite is possible

â€¢ ••Two Options of the Empirical Approach••
	• Either God remains totally other and beyond, or finitude is what is exhaustively real and good
	• Finitude attempts to bridge the gulf between finite and infinite, but fails

â€¢ ••Negative and Affirmative Relationships to God••
	• Negative relationship: feeling of utter or absolute dependence (Schleiermacher)
	• Affirmative relationship: basis of modern atheism (Kant and Fichte)

â€¢ ••Speculative Insight••
	• The infinite constitutes the passage from finite to infinite
	• This is already the speculative insight, which shifts the perspective from finite consciousness to the infinite self-mediation of spirit

â€¢ ••Phenomenology and Speculative Philosophy••
	• Phenomenology is already implicitly speculative
	• Will be examined in the context of the speculative concept of religion

â€¢ ••Speculative Philosophy••
	• The speculative for Hegel is a way of thinking that overcomes the limitations of the empirical approach
	• Will be explored in more detail in the context of the speculative concept of religion

â€¢ ••The Speculative Concept of Religion••
	• Will be examined in the context of Hegel's philosophy
	• Will provide a deeper understanding of the concept of religion and its relationship to Christianity

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Relationship between consciousness and object••
	• Consciousness and object are in a relationship of double mirroring
	• Reality is a mirror of consciousness, and consciousness is a mirror of ultimate reality

â€¢ ••Reversal of meaning••
	• Meaning flows from object to subject and from subject to object
	• This is in contrast to naive realism and naive idealism

â€¢ ••Mediation of knowledge••
	• Knowledge is mediated through categories of thought and the interests of the knower
	• This is a critique and radicalization of Kant's epistemology

â€¢ ••Objective knowledge••
	• Objective knowledge is possible, but it is reflectedly, not directly
	• This is a key aspect of speculative philosophy

â€¢ ••Connection between subject and object••
	• The connection between subject and object is not one of identity
	• Speculative philosophy is a study of unity in its concrete determinations

â€¢ ••Unity and determinacy••
	• Unity is always present, but it is continually becoming more determinate
	• The main thing is the distinction of these determinations of unity

â€¢ ••Diversity of ways••
	• Unity becomes determinate in a great diversity of ways across culture and history
	• This is a key aspect of speculative philosophy

â€¢ ••Absolute knowledge••
	• Absolute knowledge is the knowledge of all these determinacies
	• It is through these alone that absolute knowledge attains a knowledge of unity

â€¢ ••Mystery and reason••
	• Knowledge has an aspect of mystery that is impenetrable from the point of view of understanding
	• Reason may penetrate the mystery but not exhaust it

â€¢ ••Speculative philosophy and the mystical••
	• Speculative philosophy and the mystical are similar concepts
	• Mystery demands a closing of eyes and an opening of ears and minds to self-manifesting truth

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Mystical Posture••
	• Helps to check the excesses of reason in speculative philosophy
	• Maintains the absolute unity of God, which gives itself through the determinacies of history

â€¢ ••The Speculative Concept of Religion••
	• Holds together religious consciousness and its object, which is the true in and for itself (God)
	• Knows that religious consciousness entails a rising above and passing over to the absolute

â€¢ ••The Unity of Opposites••
	• Concrete actuality is grasped as the unity of determinate oppositions (e.g. God and humanity, love and death)
	• The infinite idea of the incarnation of God constitutes the speculative midpoint of philosophy and religion

â€¢ ••The Nature of Religion••
	• Is not merely our consciousness of the absolute, but the self-consciousness of absolute spirit
	• Is mediated in and through finite consciousness

â€¢ ••The Divine Process••
	• Absolute spirit is itself that which connects itself with what we have put on the other side to distinguish it
	• Religion is a divine process, not merely a human one

â€¢ ••The Self-Consciousness of Absolute Spirit••
	• Is the highest determination of the absolute idea itself
	• Falls within its self-consciousness, which includes its consciousness as relationship

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Subjective and Objective Spirit••
	• Subjective spirit is not separate from the object (absolute spirit)
	• The object, or absolute spirit, is knowing itself, it is what knows the object of its knowing

â€¢ ••The Speculative Nature of Human Knowing••
	• Human knowing is a speculum of the divine knowing
	• What transpires in human knowing is not a human projection, but the self-knowing of divine spirit through the mediation of finite spirit

â€¢ ••The Co-constitutive Moments of Religion••
	• Includes both the object that is in religion (God) and religious consciousness itself
	• Neither can be reduced to the other, and each is necessary for the other

â€¢ ••The Struggle between the Object and Consciousness••
	• The two sides struggle against each other, and their initial separation and alienation must be overcome
	• This struggle is overcome in the individual heart and through the process that constitutes the history of religion

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Self-Description••
	• Hegel describes himself as the conflict and conciliation between opposing forces within himself.
	• He is the union and conflict that exist in himself and for himself.

â€¢ ••Religion as a Process••
	• Religion is the process of passing over from consciousness to object.
	• It involves the transition from the finite to the infinite, and from the immediate to the intelligible world.

â€¢ ••The Condition of Possibility••
	• The condition of possibility for the passing over from finite to infinite is the passing over of the infinite to the finite.
	• This involves the incarnation of the divine, where the divine becomes human.

â€¢ ••The Role of Consciousness••
	• Religion is also consciousness, and has finite consciousness within it.
	• However, this finite consciousness is sublated as finite because absolute spirit is itself the other that it knows.

â€¢ ••The Mediation of Finite Spirit••
	• Absolute spirit is mediated through finite spirit, which finitizes itself in order to come to know itself.
	• This finitization is necessary for the divine to become human and to know itself.

â€¢ ••The Singularity of Human Being••
	• The singularity of human being is sublated in the process of the divine becoming human.
	• This means that the single human being is not the primary actor in this process.

â€¢ ••The Empirical Approach to Religion••
	• Empirical approaches to religion never attain to the double passing over to the actual religious relationship.
	• This relationship is the unity of absolute universality and absolute singularity.

â€¢ ••The Role of Phenomenology••
	• Phenomenology is the science of the experience of consciousness.
	• It involves tracing the immanent teleological movement of nature and finite spirit to their goal and foundation in the absolute idea.

â€¢ ••The Speculative Concept of Religion••
	• The speculative concept of religion follows from all that precedes in philosophy.
	• It is the culmination of the philosophical sciences, and provides a summary of the whole sphere of philosophy.

â€¢ ••The Phenomenology of Spirit••
	• The concept of religion is summarized in the Phenomenology of Spirit.
	• The rise of finite consciousness to the absolute is at the same time the return of absolute spirit to itself.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Concept of Religion••
	• The concept of religion is the concept of God
	• God is the abstract being or substance that creates and consumes all things
	• The concept of religion is realized in Christianity

â€¢ ••The Phenomenology of Nature and Spirit••
	• The logical idea unlocks itself in the resolve to become nature
	• Nature passes over into centred consciousness or spirit
	• Spirit moves from its natural immediacy to its ethical and rational ground

â€¢ ••The Absolute Spirit••
	• Absolute spirit is exhibited as necessary and the truth of all things
	• It is the absolute plenitude in which everything is but a moment
	• The one-sidedness of the process is abolished in the result

â€¢ ••The Speculative Reversal••
	• The result casts off its position as result and develops a counterthrust
	• The counterthrust is the speculative reversal, the stream flowing in both directions
	• In the final result, absolute spirit is conscious of itself as the first and alone true

â€¢ ••God as Alpha and Omega••
	• God is the first and last, creating and consuming all things
	• The process of creation and consumption is the activity of God
	• This process shows itself outside and within religion

â€¢ ••The Concept of God••
	• The concept of God is the abstract being or substance that creates and consumes all things
	• The concept of God is realized in Christianity
	• The 1827 lectures start with the speculative insight into the nature of God and religion

â€¢ ••Christianity as the Consummate Religion••
	• Christianity is the religion in which the concept of religion and of God are most fully realized
	• The inner structure or elements of the Christian religion correspond to moments in the life of the triune God

â€¢ ••Attributes of Christianity••
	• Christianity is the consummate or absolute religion
	• Christianity is the revelatory and revealed religion
	• Christianity is the religion of truth, freedom, and reconciliation

â€¢ ••The Three Elements or Kingdoms••
	• Hegel's treatment of the Christian religion is structured into two triads, one within the other
	• The outer triad is an analytic framework applied to each of the determinate religions

â€¢ ••The Structure of the Christian Trinity••
	• The Christian Trinity is prefigured in the structure of the concept of God and religion
	• The Christian Trinity is the consummation of the relationship between God and humanity

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Analysis of Religion••
	• Considers three aspects of a religion: abstract concept of God, concrete representation of God, and cultus (practical relationship with God)
	• Applies this framework to Christianity in the 1821 lectures

â€¢ ••The Concept of Religion••
	• Is absolute spirit in its self-mediation
	• Reflects the development of absolute spirit in three moments: substantial self-unity, self-differentiation, and self-reunification

â€¢ ••The Inner Triad of the 1821 Lectures••
	• A subset of section B of the outer triad
	• Composed of:
		- Idea of God in and for itself (immanent Trinity)
		- Idea in diremption or differentiation (creation and preservation of the natural world)
		- Appearance of the idea in finite spirit (history of estrangement, redemption, and reconciliation)

â€¢ ••Tension in Hegel's Thought••
	• Expected a trinitarian structure for the concrete representation of God in Christianity
	• Instead, a philosophical triad is used, drawn from the three branches of philosophy: logic, nature, and spirit

â€¢ ••The Philosophical Triad••
	• Recapitulates the triad found in the Encyclopedia of Philosophical Sciences
	• Has the peculiar result that the Son (anthropology and christology) occupies the third moment of the triad rather than the second

â€¢ ••The Son's Role in the Triad••
	• Distributed across two moments: the eternal Son or Logos and the finite Son
	• Creates a dilemma for Hegel, as there seems to be no place in the system for religion and the relationship to absolute spirit

â€¢ ••Hegel's Early System Outlines••
	• Envisioned a fourfold division: logic, philosophy of nature, philosophy of ethical life, and religion
	• Later converted to a triadic plan: logic, nature, spirit, with the third part including both finite and infinite spirit

â€¢ ••The Triad Applied to Christianity and the Trinity••
	• Must accommodate four themes: the immanent Trinity, creation and preservation of the natural world, the history of estrangement, redemption, and reconciliation, and the relationship between God and humanity

â€¢ ••Hegel's Experimentation with Arrangements••
	• Experiments with both a more christological and a more pneumatological arrangement
	• The dilemma faced by Hegel arises partly from the fact that the third moment of the philosophical triad is limited to finite spirit

â€¢ ••Implications for Hegel's Theology••
	• Raises questions about the relationship between God and humanity in Christianity
	• Highlights the tension between Hegel's philosophical and theological commitments

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Idea of God••
	• The idea of God is composed of three moments: the Father, the Son, and the Spirit
	• The Father represents the idea of God in and for itself
	• The Son represents the appearance of the idea in finite spirit (humanity, Christ)

â€¢ ••The Trinitarian Moment••
	• The second and third themes together comprise the second trinitarian moment
	• In the philosophical triad, finite and infinite spirit are combined in the third moment
	• In the theological trinity, nature and finite spirit are combined in the second moment

â€¢ ••The Christian Idea of God••
	• To have an adequate account of the Christian idea of God, we need to combine the second and third moments of the philosophical triad
	• We need to incorporate the third moment of the outer triad (community, cultus) into the third moment of the trinitarian mediation

â€¢ ••The Philosophical Triad••
	• The philosophical triad is grounded in the dialectic of thought itself
	• The three logical moments of the syllogism are: universality, particularity, and individuality or subjectivity

â€¢ ••The Modification of the Philosophical Triad••
	• The moments of the philosophical triad are modified to become: abstract unity, differentiation, and return
	• This modification is a concrete application of the dialectical structure to the Christian idea of God

â€¢ ••The Inner Triad••
	• The inner triad is composed of three sections: abstract unity, differentiation, and return
	• In the 1824 and 1827 lectures, the first section becomes the kingdom of the Father
	• In the 1831 lectures, the first section becomes the kingdom of the Father, the second section becomes the kingdom of the Son, and the third section becomes the kingdom of the Spirit

â€¢ ••The Outer Triad••
	• The outer triad is composed of three sections: abstract concept, concrete representation, and community, cultus
	• The original analytic scheme is broken apart, and the inner triad is converted into trinitarian moments

â€¢ ••The Second Moment of the Trinitarian Dialectic••
	• The second moment of the trinitarian dialectic is the kingdom of the Son
	• This moment is composed of two poles: the phase of differentiation and the phase of reconciliation

â€¢ ••The Phase of Differentiation••
	• The phase of differentiation includes the creation of the natural world and the fall of humanity into estrangement and evil

â€¢ ••The Phase of Reconciliation••
	• The phase of reconciliation begins with the appearance of the idea of divine human unity in a single individual

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Shift in Perspective••
	• The turning point is no longer the creation of the first Adam, but the incarnation and crucifixion of the second Adam (Christ)
	• This shift marks a move towards a more pneumatological Trinitarianism

â€¢ ••The Role of the Spirit••
	• The Spirit is the consummation of the whole process
	• The Spirit constitutes the third moment, sublating the first two moments (the eternal Logos and the incarnate Christ)

â€¢ ••Subordination vs. Supremacy of the Spirit••
	• The first pattern (1821) suggests a subordination of the Spirit to Christ
	• The second pattern (1824) suggests a supremacy of the Spirit over Christ

â€¢ ••The Nature of Evil••
	• Evil is not present in nature, but is a product of human actions
	• The primal division or creative scission that brings nature into being provides the condition of possibility for good and evil

â€¢ ••The Turning Point••
	• The turning point is not the creation of the first Adam, but the crucifixion of Christ
	• This marks the beginning of the history of redemption and reconciliation

â€¢ ••The Elements or Kingdoms••
	• The elements or kingdoms are not to be construed literally in temporal and spatial terms
	• They are constitutive aspects of the activity and eternal life of absolute spirit

â€¢ ••The Modes of Thought••
	• The elements or kingdoms can be thought of as God in the three modes of thought: Denken (thought), Vorstellung (representation), and SubjektivitÃ¤t (subjectivity)

â€¢ ••The Objective Referents••
	• The second and third moments have objective referents in the spatiotemporal world: the life and death of Christ and the formation and duration of the community of the Spirit

â€¢ ••The Representation of God••
	• The representation of God is not simply a human way of thinking about God, but God's real setting-forth of godself in the world

â€¢ ••The Consummate, Absolute Religion••
	• Hegel's philosophical naming of the Christian religion means that its significance is not to be identified principally from its historical aspect
	• Hegel employs several concepts and moves dialectically among them to grasp the disparate aspects of the whole.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Definition of Christianity••
	• Hegel considers Christianity to be the consummate and revelatory religion.
	• This means that Christianity is the religion in which the concept of religion has returned to itself and become objective to itself.

â€¢ ••The Concept of Religion••
	• The concept of religion is the relationship between finite consciousness and its absolute object.
	• This relationship is based on the absolute's self-mediation or self-consciousness.

â€¢ ••The Consummate and Revelatory Religion••
	• Christianity is the consummate and revelatory religion because it makes the relationship between finite and infinite consciousness fully manifest.
	• This is achieved through the incarnation of God, which unifies the extremes of finite and infinite consciousness.

â€¢ ••The Absolute Religion••
	• Christianity is also considered the absolute religion.
	• This means that in Christianity, the concept of religion is fully accomplished and consummated.

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Absolute••
	• For Hegel, the absolute is a dynamic and relational concept.
	• The absolute is utterly connected with everything and is nothing but relationality.

â€¢ ••God's Freedom and the World••
	• God is absolutely free within Godself and releases the world to exist as a free and independent being.
	• This release is not a limitation, but rather an all-encompassing relationship between God and the world.

â€¢ ••The Verb "Entlassen"••
	• The verb "entlassen" means to release or let go.
	• This verb is associated with the concept of the absolute, showing that the absolute has a different meaning than its conventional usage.

â€¢ ••Pseudo-Dionysius' Concept of Absolution••
	• Pseudo-Dionysius speaks of the God beyond being as absolutely absolved from all and beyond the whole.
	• However, this absolution is also accompanied by a double ecstasis, from humanity to divinity and from divinity to humanity.

â€¢ ••The Two-Way Movement of Absolution••
	• God is both absolved and absolving, utterly self-contained and utterly releasing.
	• The two-way movement of absolution between divinity and humanity constitutes religion.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Religion••
	• Religion is the awareness of the absolute identity between universal and singular spirit, infinite and finite spirit.
	• This absolute identity is relationality, and as such, it is the whole and the absolute.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's concept of the absolute••
	• Does not refer to God as an external object, but rather to the unity of God with the subject (the individual)
	• Involves the infinite form of substance, which enables it to become its own object

â€¢ ••Characteristics of the absolute••
	• Has the character of both subjectivity and substance
	• Is infinite and elastic, allowing it to become its own object

â€¢ ••Hegel's preferred name for Christianity••
	• Is not "absolute religion", but rather "revelatory religion" (offenbar)
	• This name is more palatable to postmodern sensibilities

â€¢ ••The concept of revelation••
	• Is central to Christianity, which is the religion of revelation
	• Revelation is the manifestation of God's being for consciousness

â€¢ ••God's nature••
	• Is manifest in Christianity, not just in historical or similar fashions
	• Is known as one who particularizes himself and becomes objective

â€¢ ••The relationship between God and the individual••
	• God becomes objective and particularized in the mode of finitude
	• This is a key aspect of the revelatory nature of Christianity

â€¢ ••The absolute and transcendence••
	• The absolute is not transcendent, immutable, or disconnected
	• It is, rather, a unity of God with the subject

â€¢ ••The concept of religion••
	• Is consummated and God becomes absolutely relational only in and through the entire history of religion
	• This history is inexhaustibly rich and still an open history

â€¢ ••Postmodern perspectives on Hegel's concept of the absolute••
	• Accept the historical relativity of religions and affirm religious pluralism
	• See the concept of religion as an ideal that is never fully accomplished in history

â€¢ ••Implications of Hegel's concept of the absolute••
	• Challenges traditional notions of God as an external object
	• Emphasizes the importance of the individual's relationship with God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Revelation and Spirit••
	• For Hegel, revelation refers to the primal division of infinite subjectivity or infinite form
	• It means determining oneself to be for an other, i.e. to reveal oneself to another
	• This is the essence of spirit itself, and a spirit that is not revelatory is not spirit

â€¢ ••God as Spirit••
	• God is intrinsically revelatory and self-manifesting
	• God is not jealous and does not withhold or conceal Godself
	• God's revelatoriness is essentially what God is, and this is what it means to say that God is spirit

â€¢ ••Christianity as Revealed Religion••
	• Christianity is also the revealed (geoffenbart) religion
	• This term suggests something that has been given in a historical, positive fashion through a direct epiphany, inspired writing, or authoritative tradition

â€¢ ••The Meaning of Revelation••
	• What is revealed is not truths or information about God, but rather God's revelatoriness and self-communication
	• Revelation has no object or content other than itself
	• The coincidence of offenbar and geoffenbart means that reason and truth make themselves open and manifest

â€¢ ••The Debate over Reason vs. Revelation••
	• The debate over reason versus revelation was misplaced, according to Hegel
	• What is revealed is precisely the process in which reason and truth make themselves open and manifest

â€¢ ••The Positivity of Christianity••
	• Christianity is a positive religion, whose truth is mediated in sensible, historical fashion through external authorities
	• This is the case because everything that is for consciousness is objective to consciousness

â€¢ ••The Importance of Historical Mediation••
	• The revelatoriness of God requires a historical mediation
	• Even God must come to humanity from without, giving Godself in external, sensuous, historical forms

â€¢ ••The Role of Education and Objective Truths••
	• The truth may be within, but it must be awakened and brought forth by an outward educational process in the form of objective truths, doctrines, laws, persons, and practices
	• This is necessary due to the sensible and sinful nature of human beings

â€¢ ••The Rational Truth of Christianity••
	• Despite the necessity of historical mediation, the essentially rational truth revealed in Christianity derives from its spirit
	• This truth is not just a product of human reason, but is rooted in the revelatoriness of God

â€¢ ••The Relationship between God and Humanity••
	• God must come to humanity from without, giving Godself in external, sensuous, historical forms
	• This is necessary for human beings to come to know and understand God's revelatoriness and self-communication.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Witness of the Spirit••
	• The witness of the Spirit is the only authentic witness to truth
	• It cannot be verified by historical proofs, miracles, or testimonies
	• It can only be verified by the witness of the Spirit itself

â€¢ ••Ambivalence of the Witness of the Spirit••
	• The witness of the Spirit can refer to the Holy Spirit or the Spirit of God
	• It can also refer to the witness of our spirit to spiritual truth
	• The two meanings are two aspects of a single truth

â€¢ ••The Spirit of God and Human Spirit••
	• The Spirit of God witnesses only in and through our spirits
	• There is no divine witness apart from the activity of human spirit
	• The human spirit is not an autonomous, singular activity, but the inner working of the one holy and universal Spirit

â€¢ ••The Witness of Spirit in History••
	• In history, all that is noble, lofty, and divine speaks to us internally
	• Our spirit bears witness to this truth
	• The witness can range from a general resonance or sympathy to explicit faith, insight, and thought

â€¢ ••Philosophy as the Highest Form of Witness••
	• Hegel thinks that the highest form of the witness of spirit is found in philosophy
	• Philosophy provides a deeper understanding of the witness of spirit
	• However, many people need to base their trust on external authority and sensible experience

â€¢ ••The Role of the Bible••
	• The Bible is the fundamental basis for Christians
	• It must resonate within us subjectively for us to be touched by and appropriate its teachings
	• The Bible gives firmness to our conviction, but we must interpret and think about its contents

â€¢ ••The Importance of Interpretation••
	• Anyone can quote the Bible, but that does not make them a theologian
	• Interpretation, exegesis, and explanation are necessary to determine the meaning of the Bible
	• We must bring in the right spirit to give life to the positive

â€¢ ••The Spirit that Gives Life••
	• It is a question of which spirit we bring in to give life to the positive
	• Is it the human spirit, or is it the witnessing of our spirit to the divine Spirit?
	• The Bible itself points to the importance of the Spirit in giving life

â€¢ ••The Approach to the Philosophy of Religion••
	• Hegel's approach will be conceptual, not historical
	• He will not start with external matters, but rather proceed conceptually
	• The lectures on the philosophy of religion will offer a conceptual re-description of Christianity in terms appropriate to philosophical science

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Lectures••
	• The purpose of the lectures is to provide a conceptual understanding of Christianity
	• This understanding will be based on the witness of the Spirit
	• It will provide a deeper understanding of the nature of Christianity and its relationship to the human spirit.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The True Work of Theology••
	• Does not deny the historicality of Christianity
	• Grasps the true, actual, rational, and spiritual within this historicality
	• Uses a speculative method rather than an empirical one

â€¢ ••The Religion of Truth••
	• Christianity is the religion of truth
	• Truth means relating to something objective without it being alien
	• Spirit is the eternal intuition of itself as both subject and object

â€¢ ••The Mutual Adequacy of Subject and Object••
	• Truth is the mutual adequacy of subject and object
	• This is the same definition as freedom, except with a focus on reconciliation

â€¢ ••The Religion of Freedom••
	• Christianity is the religion of freedom
	• Freedom means relating oneself to something objective without it being alien
	• Emphasizes the negation of difference or otherness

â€¢ ••Reconciliation and Liberation••
	• Difference assumes the form of alienation, which calls for reconciliation
	• Christianity takes on the aspect of a liberatory religion
	• Includes liberation from slavery and oppression

â€¢ ••The Recognition of Human Freedom••
	• Christianity recognizes all human beings as free
	• Not just one or some, but all are recognized as free

â€¢ ••The Completion of the Religion of Nature and Finite Spirit••
	• Christianity completes the religion of nature and finite spirit
	• Includes both the objectivity of spirit and the freedom of self-possession

â€¢ ••Self-Possession and Presence to Others••
	• Self-possession or presence to oneself is the essential mark of freedom
	• Achieved in and through presence to others
	• Free institutions, not autonomous individuals, play a key role in realizing freedom

â€¢ ••The Church as a Community of Freedom••
	• The church assumes the form of a community of freedom
	• Realizes freedom through presence to others

â€¢ ••The Emergence of Absolute Freedom••
	• Christianity is depicted as the religion of absolute freedom
	• Emerges over against the rupture and alienation of religious consciousness
	• Traced back to Persian, Jewish, Egyptian, and Greek religion

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Christianity as the religion of reconciliation••
	• Reconciliation is the central theme of Christianity
	• It involves the reconciliation of the world with God
	• The world has fallen away from God and become finite consciousness

â€¢ ••The nature of reconciliation••
	• Reconciliation is the negation of separation and division
	• It involves each entity recognizing itself in the other
	• Reconciliation is freedom and activity, not something quiescent

â€¢ ••The role of God in reconciliation••
	• God's movement into the world is necessary for reconciliation
	• God undergoes the anguish of estrangement and death in the figure of Christ
	• The crucified Christ is central to the process of reconciliation

â€¢ ••Human participation in reconciliation••
	• Human beings must be caught up in the movement of reconciliation
	• They must work for the removal of estrangement
	• Reconciliation assumes the form of liberation

â€¢ ••The universal process of Christianity••
	• Truth, freedom, and reconciliation are all part of a universal process
	• This process cannot be expressed in a simple proposition
	• It requires a complex interplay of propositions

â€¢ ••Hegel's dialectical procedure••
	• Hegel's method involves a speculative redescription of the Christian metanarrative
	• He provides a philosophical comprehension of Christianity, not a historical description
	• His procedure involves a complex interplay of propositions

â€¢ ••The Christian metanarrative••
	• The Christian metanarrative is the narrative of the triune God
	• It involves the three elements of unity, differentiation, and reconciliation
	• It is read onto the whole of the history of religions

â€¢ ••The triadic structure of the metanarrative••
	• The triadic structure is a fundamental logic of things
	• It transcends every determinate articulation of it
	• It manifests itself in a variety of forms throughout history

â€¢ ••The relationship between the Christian metanarrative and other religions••
	• The Christian metanarrative is read onto the whole of the history of religions
	• Other religions are made to subserve the Christian story
	• A less distinctively Christian version of the metanarrative may be more appropriate

â€¢ ••The significance of Hegel's philosophical comprehension of Christianity••
	• Hegel's procedure provides a new understanding of Christianity
	• It involves a speculative redescription of the Christian metanarrative
	• It has implications for our understanding of the history of religions.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Approach to Christianity••
	• Hegel offers a speculative redescription of the Christian metanarrative
	• He seeks to introduce narrativity into the structure of the divine life
	• The immanent Trinity is not an Archimedean foundation, but an element within the revelatory dynamic as a whole

â€¢ ••The Christian Metanarrative••
	• The classical version of the metanarrative is dualistic and mythological
	• It has a sequential view of space/time and representational language
	• This view locks God and the world into ontologically incommensurate realms

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of the Classical Metanarrative••
	• The classical metanarrative requires God to intervene supernaturally and episodically in the world
	• Hegel seeks to articulate an alternative that responds to the critiques of modernity

â€¢ ••The Role of Narrative in Hegel's Thought••
	• Narrative relations are converted into logical relations
	• This allows for a demythologized perdurance of narrative

â€¢ ••Hegel's Demythologization of the Christian Metanarrative••
	• Hegel's friendly demythologization preserves the theological referent and deep insight of the metanarrative
	• It subverts hostile demythologizations that were already under way in his time

â€¢ ••Risks Involved in Hegel's Strategy••
	• Some critics believe that Hegel has thrown out the baby with the bath water
	• Hegel himself remarks that the difficult thing is to separate out what pertains only to representation

â€¢ ••Interpretative Questions••
	• Is a friendly demythologization the most effective way of defending Christian faith against hostile ones?
	• Does Hegel's heterodox version of the Christian metanarrative come closer to the dynamic God of biblical faith?

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Theology and Philosophy••
	• Hegel seeks to translate representational language into speculative concepts
	• This raises questions about the relationship between theology and philosophy

â€¢ ••The Significance of the Triad••
	• The triad (universality, particularity, individuality; or idea, nature, spirit) may represent a more primordial and universal structure
	• This is a question to be addressed in Chapter 10

â€¢ ••The Implications of Hegel's Thought••
	• Hegel's thought has implications for our understanding of the Christian metanarrative and its relationship to philosophy
	• It raises questions about the nature of God, the world, and the relationship between them.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Concept of God••
	• Central to his philosophical enterprise
	• Announced as the beginning and end of all things
	• The one and only object of philosophy

â€¢ ••Evolution of Hegel's Treatment of The Concept of Religion••
	• 1821 manuscript focused on scientific conception of the religious standpoint
	• 1824 lectures moved from empirical observation to speculative concept of religion
	• 1827 lectures bypassed preliminary considerations and moved directly to concept of God

â€¢ ••The Concept of God in Hegel's Lectures••
	• Present in the concept of religion, but not explicitly developed in 1821 lectures
	• Comprises three moments: abstract concept of God, knowledge of God, and worship of God

â€¢ ••The Three Moments of the Concept of God••
	• Abstract concept of God: substantial self-unity
	• Knowledge of God: self-differentiation
	• Worship of God: self-return

â€¢ ••Theoretical and Practical Religious Relationships to God••
	• Comprise the second and third moments of the concept of God
	• Further analyzed in 1824 lectures

â€¢ ••Proofs of the Existence of God••
	• Associated with specific religions
	• Deferred to parts 2 and 3 of the lectures

â€¢ ••The 1827 Lectures••
	• Move immediately into an exposition of the concept of God
	• Unfold in three steps: abstract concept of God, knowledge of God, and worship of God

â€¢ ••The 1831 Lectures••
	• Follow the 1827 structure, but with some variations
	• Focus on the concept of God and its relationship to the concept of religion

â€¢ ••Hegel's Treatment of Gnosticism••
	• Incorporates elements from Gnosticism, Neoplatonism, and mysticism
	• Subverts a properly Christian understanding of God and the world

â€¢ ••Criticism of Hegel's Concept of God••
	• Expressed by William Desmond and others
	• Further discussed in Chapter 11

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Concept of God••
	• God is not just a general substance, but a self-knowing substance or subject.
	• God is both knower and known, subject and object of divine knowing.
	• God knows and loves Godself in humanity, and humans know themselves in God.

â€¢ ••The Concept of Religion••
	• The concept of religion is that God knows himself in spirit and spirit knows itself in God.
	• This concept is the foundation of the relationship between God and humanity.

â€¢ ••Forms of Religion••
	• There are three main forms of religion: feeling, representation, and faith.
	• Faith is now considered the highest form of religious knowledge, replacing thought.

â€¢ ••Faith••
	• Faith is valued for its grounds or reasons, rather than its basis in authority.
	• This valorization of faith may be a response to Schleiermacher's Glaubenslehre.

â€¢ ••Cultus••
	• The cultus is treated similarly to 1827, with a focus on its role in worship and ritual.
	• The 1831 lectures add a new section on the relationship of religion to the state.

â€¢ ••Relationship of Religion to the State••
	• Hegel's renewed concern for this topic is a result of recent political events.
	• This section is a new addition to the 1831 lectures.

â€¢ ••Abstract Concept of God••
	• This concept appears only in the 1827 lectures, but is used as a vehicle to present proofs of the existence of God.
	• The proofs are discussed in the determinate religions and the Christian religion.

â€¢ ••Proofs of the Existence of God••
	• The proofs are gathered together in a lengthy subsection in the 1827 lectures.
	• The first section of part 1, The Concept of God, needed to be filled with new content.

â€¢ ••Philosophical Analysis of God••
	• Hegel argues that God is the absolute truth, the truth of all things.
	• This is the result of philosophy, and is supported by religious consciousness.

â€¢ ••Definition of God••
	• God is defined as the universal truth of all things, which is confirmed by philosophical analysis.
	• This definition is abstract, but is based on the ordinary meaning and use of the word God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God as Unity••
	• God is a unity with itself, not an abstract universality outside of particulars
	• This unity is not a static concept, but a dynamic and concrete one

â€¢ ••God as Substance••
	• God is the absolute substance, the only true actuality
	• Everything else depends on God for its existence

â€¢ ••Avoiding Pantheism••
	• Clinging to the abstract concept of God as substance can lead to Spinozism or pantheism
	• However, Hegel argues that God is not just a substance, but also a subject with spirit

â€¢ ••God as Spirit••
	• Substance is an attribute of God's absolute being-with-self and abiding-with-self
	• When substance is grasped as internally concrete, it becomes spirit

â€¢ ••The Immanent Trinity••
	• God is an abiding unity in which all distinctions remain enclosed
	• This is the meaning of the immanent Trinity as an inexhaustible generative matrix

â€¢ ••God as the Universal••
	• God is the abundant universal, not an inert, abstract universal
	• God is the infinite fountainhead out of which everything emerges, into which everything returns, and in which it is eternally maintained

â€¢ ••God as Thought••
	• We have God not primarily in the mode of feeling and sensation, but in the mode of thought
	• Thought is the soil for this content, the activity of the universal

â€¢ ••The Role of Thought in Religion••
	• Thought is the inmost seat of religion
	• Human beings think, and they alone have religion

â€¢ ••The Abstract Concept of God••
	• This concept of God emerges from speculative philosophy
	• It is the abstract concept of God that Hegel spells out for the first time in the 1827 lectures

â€¢ ••Pantheism and Identity-Philosophy••
	• Some people want to designate this idea of God as pantheism
	• Hegel's philosophy is associated with identity-philosophy, a term used by Schelling to characterize his main writings of 1801-4

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Refutation of Pantheism••
	• Pantheism is often misunderstood as meaning everything is God
	• However, authentic pantheism identifies God with the essence or substance that is in all things, not with things as such

â€¢ ••Spinoza's Philosophy••
	• Spinoza's philosophy tends towards acosmism rather than atheism
	• It affirms the actuality of God rather than the actuality of the world

â€¢ ••Hegel's Clarification of Pantheism••
	• The term "pantheism" can be ambiguous, with two possible meanings:
		- Everything is God
		- God is the All or the whole in which everything finite participates

â€¢ ••Panentheism vs. Pantheism••
	• Hegel notes that pantheism is a poor expression because it can be taken as a collective totality rather than universality
	• Panentheism is a more accurate term, but Hegel lacks it

â€¢ ••The All (das All)••
	• The All is not a totalizing collectivity, but rather gives finite things their free and independent existence
	• It is a unity that generates and emerges from determinacy and difference

â€¢ ••Identity-Philosophy••
	• The term identity-philosophy is ambiguous, obscuring the crucial question of whether unity is defined as abstract substance or concrete spirit

â€¢ ••The Concept of God••
	• The abstract concept of God corresponds to the first moment of the divine life, God's primordial self-unity or the immanent Trinity

â€¢ ••The Creation of the World••
	• The world comes into being as the created other of God, through a primal division or judgement (Urteil)

â€¢ ••Absolute Spirit••
	• Absolute spirit is not a totalizing substance, but a unity that generates and emerges from determinacy and difference

â€¢ ••The Study of Philosophy••
	• Philosophy is a study of how unity through its successive forms becomes continually more determinate

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God and Consciousness••
	• God and consciousness are two elements that interact with each other
	• God becomes an object of consciousness
	• God's nature is to reveal itself, to be manifest, and to be known

â€¢ ••Relationship between God and Consciousness••
	• Can be viewed from two perspectives: divine action and human experience
	• From the divine perspective, God's creation of the world is its manifestation
	• From the human perspective, the relationship yields four basic forms of knowledge of God

â€¢ ••Four Basic Forms of Knowledge of God••
	• Immediate knowledge
	• Feeling
	• Representation
	• Thought

â€¢ ••Immediate Knowledge••
	• Immediate certainty that God is and has being in and for itself
	• Faith is the first and most basic form of this certainty
	• Faith is not to be placed in opposition to knowledge, but is a form of knowledge

â€¢ ••Faith••
	• Faith is holding something to be true without direct empirical evidence or intellectual intuition
	• Grounds for faith derive partly from the testimony of others and partly from the witness of the Spirit
	• The witness of the Spirit is the witness of one's own spirit that the content conforms to the nature of one's spirit

â€¢ ••Witness of the Spirit••
	• The witness of the Spirit is not just the Holy Spirit, but the witness of one's own spirit
	• This witness verifies the congruence between one's existence and the content of faith

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Faith••
	• Faith is an immediate knowledge and verification of an objective content
	• Faith is a judgement that a congruence exists between one's existence and the content

â€¢ ••Modalities of Faith••
	• Feeling: illuminates the subject and provides a sense of the content
	• Other modalities: representation and thought

â€¢ ••Certainty and Faith••
	• Certainty is the immediate relation of the content of faith and oneself
	• Faith is a form of certainty that is not based on direct empirical evidence or intellectual intuition

â€¢ ••God as a Mystery••
	• God is a mystery, but not a secret
	• God's mystery is a rational mystery that makes itself known and is not jealous of communicating itself

â€¢ ••God's Nature••
	• God's nature is to reveal itself, to be manifest, and to be known
	• God loses nothing when it communicates itself, just like a lamp loses no light when its light is passed on to another lamp.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Feeling (GefÃ¼hl)••
	• Hegel uses the term "GefÃ¼hl" (feeling) to describe the subjectivity of faith in his 1824 and 1827 lectures
	• Replaces the earlier term "Empfindung" (sensibility, sensation)
• ••Characteristics of Feeling••
	• Feeling is an activity of the self that integrates sensations into a reflected totality
	• Belongs to the realm of ideality and subjectivity
	• Can express a relationship of immediacy to nonsensible objects, such as God
• ••Relationship to God••
	• When I say I feel God, I mean that God is within my being
	• God is not a product of my feeling, but exists independently of me as the ground of my being
• ••Aspects of Feeling••
	• Feeling can be seen as an immediate contact with an object
	• Analogies can be drawn from sense experience (e.g. feeling something hard)
• ••Importance of Feeling••
	• Feeling is presently in vogue because it involves personal and subjective involvement
	• We become physically identified with what we believe and act accordingly
• ••Limitations of Feeling••
	• Feeling is indeterminate and lacks the capacity for making judgements
	• Every content is capable of being in feeling, including religion, right, ethics, and crime
• ••Role of Thought••
	• Thought is necessary to purify and cultivate feelings
	• Thought is required to make judgements about the validity of feelings
• ••Schleiermacher's Influence••
	• Hegel's understanding of feeling was influenced by Friedrich Schleiermacher's Der christliche Glaube
	• Schleiermacher introduced the term "GefÃ¼hl" as a major theological category
• ••Feeling and Worship••
	• Feeling plays a role in the partaking of the eucharistic elements
	• We become physically identified with what we believe and act accordingly
• ••Feeling and Knowledge••
