	• Feeling is not the only form of knowledge of God
	• Faith is a higher form of knowledge that issues from feeling and representation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of Feeling••
	• Feeling is a lower form of consciousness that is common to humans and animals.
	• When a content like right, ethical life, or God is posited in feeling, it is the worst way it can be posited.
	• Feeling is limited to sensing one's finitude and dependence on the world.

â€¢ ••Schleiermacher's View of Feeling••
	• Feeling is a prereflective awareness of the whence and whither of all existence.
	• It is not a sense-based experience, but rather a fundamental aspect of human consciousness.
	• It bears a resemblance to the natural attitude or Urglaube.

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Thought••
	• Thought is the soil in which content like God is apprehended and engendered.
	• Thought is the rational will that determines the content of consciousness.
	• Thought is the means by which humans transcend their finite limits.

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Feeling and Thought••
	• Feeling is a limited form of knowledge, whereas thought is the means by which we know and understand the world.
	• Feeling is a mode of apprehension independent of knowing and doing, but it is also the ground of both.
	• Thought is the means by which we cultivate and bring our feelings into the heart.

â€¢ ••Representation and Feeling••
	• Representation (Vorstellung) is the means by which we attend to the objective aspect of our experiences.
	• Representation relies on the understanding (Verstand), which places images and sense impressions under concepts.
	• Representation is prior to feeling when it comes to the matter of truth.

â€¢ ••The Priority of Representation••
	• Feeling is indeterminate and can contain any possible content.
	• Feeling cannot serve as the justifying criterion for its content.
	• Representation is necessary to determine the truth of our experiences.

â€¢ ••The Role of Doctrine and Teaching••
	• Religious instruction begins with representation.
	• Doctrine and teaching cultivate and bring our feelings into the heart.
	• Representation is the means by which we know and understand the world.

â€¢ ••The Limitations of Feeling••
	• Feeling is limited to sensing one's finitude and dependence on the world.
	• Feeling is not a means of knowing or understanding the world.
	• Feeling is a lower form of consciousness that is common to humans and animals.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Thought••
	• Thought is the means by which we transcend our finite limits.
	• Thought is the rational will that determines the content of consciousness.
	• Thought is the means by which we know and understand the world.

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Hegel and Schleiermacher••
	• Hegel and Schleiermacher had different views of feeling and thought.
	• They did not adequately understand each other's positions.
	• Their mutual polemics were often misdirected.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Representation as Consciousness••
	• Representation is a consciousness of something objective
	• It occurs when we think of something as being before us
	• This is how people ordinarily think

â€¢ ••Philosophy and Representation••
	• Philosophy transforms representations into concepts
	• The content remains the same, but the form changes
	• Philosophy is not about removing content, but transforming it

â€¢ ••Forms of Representation••
	• There are two basic forms: sensible and nonsensible
	• Sensible forms are called images (Bilder)
	• Images have a double signification: immediate and inner meaning

â€¢ ••Sensible Representation••
	• Includes figurative forms such as symbolic, allegorical, and metaphorical
	• Examples: God has begotten a son (not meant literally, but symbolically)
	• Historical narratives can also be representational, such as the story of Jesus

â€¢ ••Nonsensible Representation••
	• Includes spiritual contents, activities, and relationships
	• Examples: God created the world (terms have a spiritual meaning)
	• Nonsensible configurations do not depend on sensible images, but may have originated from them

â€¢ ••Representation as Metaphysical and Epistemological Category••
	• Representation is a metaphysical category, dealing with the nature of reality
	• It is also an epistemological category, dealing with how we know reality

â€¢ ••The Role of Imagination in Thought••
	• Hegel's own thinking is replete with images, metaphors, and analogies
	• Imagination plays a crucial role in thought, but is not always explicit

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Representation and Concept••
	• Representation is transformed into concepts through philosophy
	• The content remains the same, but the form changes
	• Concepts become dry and abstract without images

â€¢ ••The Significance of Representation in Religion••
	• Religion is the consciousness of absolute truth
	• Representation is a key aspect of religious thought and experience
	• Philosophy is not about overthrowing religion, but transforming its content

â€¢ ••The Importance of Understanding Representation••
	• Understanding representation is crucial for understanding philosophy and religion
	• Representation is a complex and multifaceted concept
	• It requires careful analysis and consideration of its various forms and meanings.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Representation vs. Thought••
	• Representation apprehends contents in their determinate isolation
	• Thought seeks relationships and universality, dissolving the simple form of representation

â€¢ ••The Role of Thought••
	• Thought raises representational configurations to conceptual form
	• Concepts grasp and hold together disparate elements in simple representations

â€¢ ••Conceptual Thinking••
	• Does not invent new terms or convert everything into logic
	• Develops arguments and hypotheses to elucidate the unity of representational features

â€¢ ••The Nature of Thought••
	• Is a way of thinking dialectically
	• Continues to be fructified by imagistic material from representation

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Representation and Thought••
	• A dialectic between representation and thought is constantly taking place
	• Thought is not possible without representation, and vice versa

â€¢ ••Attributes of God••
	• Attributes like graciousness and justice, or omnipotence and wisdom, appear to contradict each other in representation
	• Dialectical thinking shows that these qualities are mutually and necessarily connected in God

â€¢ ••Necessity and Mediation••
	• Necessity entails mediation, which is essential for thought
	• True thought or comprehension unites immediate and mediated knowledge

â€¢ ••Immediate and Mediated Knowledge••
	• Immediacy is the principle of representation, while mediation is essential for thought
	• Immediate knowledge is empirical, while mediated knowledge is speculative in its highest form

â€¢ ••The Goal of Thought••
	• The goal of thought is necessity, which is achieved through mediation
	• Necessity is the unity of distinct determinations within a simple reality

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Thought and Knowledge••
	• Thought is not a new conceptual apparatus, but a way of thinking dialectically
	• Thought continues to be influenced by representation, and vice versa.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Immediacy and Mediation••
	• Immediacy is an abstraction, and everything is connected
	• Pure immediacy does not exist, only pure relationality
	• Immediacy and mediation are intertwined

â€¢ ••Knowledge and Mediation••
	• Knowledge is always mediated, involving both subjective and objective elements
	• The connection between immediacy and mediation is consummated when thought knows itself in objects of thought
	• This is absolute knowing, a goal rather than an achievement

â€¢ ••Religious Knowledge••
	• Religious knowledge is both mediated and immediate
	• Positive revelation and education cannot bring about religion from outside
	• Mediated knowledge stimulates a recollection of something within ourselves

â€¢ ••The Role of Thought••
	• Thought is what brings spiritual content to consciousness
	• Without mediation, spiritual content would slumber in immediacy
	• Thought is what arouses us from our slumber

â€¢ ••Proofs of God's Existence••
	• Mediated knowledge takes the form of proofs for God's existence
	• The mediation between two terms in a syllogism is provided by the third term, which has the character of a proof

â€¢ ••The Nature of Religion••
	• Religion is an act of mediation, a movement from finite to infinite being
	• The passage from finite to infinite being corresponds to the cosmological and teleological proofs
	• The passage from subjective to objective infinitude corresponds to the ontological proof

â€¢ ••The Forms of Knowledge of God••
	• The knowledge of God takes the form of the proofs of God's existence
	• The proofs are the concrete forms that the knowledge of God assumes
	• The proofs are a twofold passage, from finite to infinite being and from subjective to objective infinitude

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Philosophy and Religion••
	• Philosophy is on the way to the goal, not at the goal
	• The goal is the beatific vision and God's self-knowing
	• Philosophy is concerned with the relationship between philosophy and religion

â€¢ ••The Doctrine of the Triple Syllogism••
	• The doctrine of the triple syllogism is discussed in the Encyclopedia
	• The doctrine is concerned with the relationship between philosophy and religion
	• The doctrine is summarized in the concluding section of the Encyclopedia

â€¢ ••The Conclusion••
	• The Phenomenology of Spirit concludes with a brief discussion of absolute knowing
	• Absolute knowing is a goal rather than an achievement
	• The Phenomenology of Spirit is concerned with the journey to the goal, not the goal itself

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's treatment of proofs in his lectures••
	• In most lectures, Hegel treats proofs in relation to specific religions
	• In the 1827 lectures, he gathers all proofs into one section in "The Concept of Religion"

â€¢ ••Concerns about atheism and secularism••
	• Hegel may have been responding to charges of atheism against his philosophy
	• He may have been trying to ward off the threat of atheism in the modern secular-bourgeois world

â€¢ ••Lectures on the proofs of God's existence••
	• Hegel gave separate lectures on the proofs in the summer of 1829
	• He was planning to publish them when he died in 1831

â€¢ ••Distortion in the proofs••
	• There are two sorts of distortion in the attempt to prove God's existence
	• The first distortion is the suggestion that God's existence can be said to exist in the same way as finite being

â€¢ ••First distortion: existence and determinate being••
	• Existence (Dasein) refers to determinate, finite being
	• God's being is not limited and cannot be said to exist in the same way

â€¢ ••Second distortion: demonstration from finite being••
	• The notion that it is possible to prove or demonstrate God's being from finite being is a distortion
	• This would make God a result or consequence dependent on the being of the finite

â€¢ ••Authentic form of the proofs••
	• The religious elevation to the infinite is not based on an affirmation of the finite
	• It is based on the negation of the finite, its self-cancelling or sublation

â€¢ ••The finite and the infinite••
	• The finite becomes aware of the infinite not as one aspect of a relationship but as the whole
	• The infinite overreaches both finite and infinite, includes negation within itself, and thus truly is

â€¢ ••The affirmative and the active••
	• What is affirmative or active is not the finite but the infinite
	• The religious relationship and hence the proofs are based on the return of infinite or absolute spirit

â€¢ ••Speculative expression of the proofs••
	• The proofs are based on the return of infinite or absolute spirit
	• This is a speculative expression of the authentic form of the proofs

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Analysis of the Cosmological Proof••
	• Hegel discusses the cosmological proof in his lectures, specifically in 1821, 1824, and 1827.
	• He argues that the proof is not valid, as it attempts to demonstrate necessity from contingency.

â€¢ ••The Cosmological Proof in Different Religions••
	• Hegel distinguishes between three forms of the cosmological proof related to specific religions:
		- Argument from finite to infinite (religions of nature)
		- Argument from the many to the one (Jewish religion)
		- Argument from contingency to necessity (Greek religion)

â€¢ ••The Fundamental Religious Activity••
	• Hegel notes that all religious proofs are based on the fundamental religious activity of human beings:
		- Rising from finite to infinite
		- Transcending singularity to universality
		- Becoming being-in-and-for-itself

â€¢ ••The Limit of the Finite••
	• The limit of the finite is precisely the infinite, and in this sense the finite already belongs to and is its other, the infinite.

â€¢ ••The Self-Negation of the Finite••
	• The self-negation of the finite is the transition to the infinite, but it cannot be the basis for a proof.

â€¢ ••The Proof Based on Divine Unity••
	• This proof utilizes the categories of one and many, but the concept of God is not exhausted by the category of oneness.

â€¢ ••The Proof Based on Contingency and Necessity••
	• This proof is logically similar to the previous one, but the contingent is a richer, more concrete category than the finite.

â€¢ ••The Defect in the Proof••
	• The proof sets up contingent things on one side and necessity on the other, implying that contingent things condition absolute necessity.

â€¢ ••The Authentic Form of the Proof••
	• The authentic form of the proof would start with the process of mediation intrinsic to absolute necessity.

â€¢ ••The Self-Proof of the Infinite••
	• The only genuine proof of the infinite is its self-proof, as it cannot be demonstrated from the finite.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Teleological Proof••
	• Based on the experience of purposiveness or utility (ZweckmÃ¤ÃŸigkeit)
	• Involves positing a third rationally ordering principle to explain harmony in the world
• ••Kant's Critique••
	• Refuted the physicotheological proof in the Critique of Pure Reason
	• Argued that we cannot argue from empirically observed worldly circumstances to the absolute and infinite
• ••Hegel's Response••
	• Does not simply accept Kant's critique, but reworks and expands it
	• Argues that we cannot argue from power to omnipotence, from wisdom to omniscience
• ••The Concept of God••
	• What we want in God is absolute wisdom, power, unity
	• The teleological proof affords only a concept of great wisdom, power, unity, but not absolute
• ••Inner Purposiveness••
	• Introduced by Kant as an alternative to external purposiveness
	• Refers to the concept of organic life (Lebendigkeit), where every living thing is a telos with its means implicit within it
• ••The World-Soul••
	• A universally purposive life, hence to a world-soul or Nous
	• Arrives only at a definition of God as soul or power, not as spirit
• ••Ethicotheology••
	• Introduced by Kant as the attempt to infer the supreme cause and its attributes from the moral end of rational beings in nature
	• Involves arguing that God is a necessary postulate of action in accordance with moral ends
• ••Kant's Programme••
	• Carried out in the Critique of Practical Reason
	• Argued that God is the guarantor of the kingdom of ends and thus of the reality of the highest good
• ••The Limitations of the Teleological Proof••
	• Cannot make judgements as to the world-soul's goodness and wisdom
	• Remains deficient in its definition of God as soul or power, not as spirit
• ••The Leap to the Absolute••
	• From great to absolute, we make a leap that cannot be justified by the teleological proof alone

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Concept of God••
	• Hegel considers God to be the first, highest, most perfect, and most real being
	• He does not discuss God in his lectures, indicating a lack of interest in traditional moral interpretations of religion

â€¢ ••The Ontological Proof••
	• The ontological proof is the only genuine proof of God's existence
	• It was first proposed by Anselm of Canterbury in the 12th century
	• The proof passes from the concept of God to the being or reality of God

â€¢ ••Kant's Critique of the Ontological Proof••
	• Immanuel Kant critiques the ontological proof, arguing that it presupposes traditional ontological attributes
	• Kant's God is static, unchanging, and non-interacting with the world
	• Kant concludes that the existence of such a God cannot be demonstrated by rational theology

â€¢ ••Kant's Alternative: Moral Theology••
	• Kant proposes moral or ethico-theology as an alternative to rational theology
	• The existence of God is a necessary postulate of practical reason
	• This understanding of God is traditional and unattractive to Hegel

â€¢ ••Hegel's Response to Kant••
	• Hegel responds to Kant's critique with a post-Kantian version of the ontological proof
	• His argument is developed in his lectures from 1821 to 1831
	• Hegel's version of the proof is based on his own logic

â€¢ ••Kant's Objection to the Ontological Proof••
	• Kant objects that reality cannot be plucked from the concept of God
	• Being is not a predicate that adds anything to a content

â€¢ ••Hegel's Concept of God vs. Kant's••
	• Hegel's concept of God is more radical than Kant's
	• Hegel's God is dynamic and interacting with the world

â€¢ ••The Cosmological and Physico-Theological Proofs••
	• The cosmological and physico-theological proofs depend on the validity of the ontological proof
	• Kant has more sympathy for the physico-theological proof, which is based on empirical principles

â€¢ ••The Failure of Rational Theology••
	• Kant concludes that rational theology is unable to demonstrate the existence of God
	• This leads to a rejection of traditional rational theology in favor of moral or ethico-theology

â€¢ ••Hegel's Use of Kant••
	• Hegel uses Kant's critique of the ontological proof for his own philosophical agenda
	• Hegel's interpretation of Kant is not unbiased, and he is deconstructing Kant's system

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Concept of the Concept••
	• The concept of a hundred thalers is not the same as the actual thalers
	• The concept is a subjective idea, but the true concept contains objectivity within itself

â€¢ ••The True Concept••
	• Is alive and active, mediating itself with itself
	• Is the movement or process of self-objectifying
	• Sublates its subjectivity, making it real

â€¢ ••The Identity of Concept and Being••
	• Is not a presupposition, but a result of the concept's self-determining movement
	• The concept makes itself reality and becomes the truth, the unity of subject and object

â€¢ ••The Ontological Proof••
	• Is the proof that God exists
	• Is based on the idea that God is the most perfect concept
	• Becomes fully manifest in the Christian religion

â€¢ ••God as the Most Perfect Concept••
	• Is the most perfectly real
	• Is the unity of subject and object

â€¢ ••The Incarnation••
	• God takes on finite, worldly, determinate being (Dasein)
	• Becomes absolute spirit through the process of self-diremption and self-return

â€¢ ••The Proof of Divine Existence••
	• Does not involve an illicit logical trick
	• Is provided by God's self-involvement in the world-process

â€¢ ••The Importance of Religious Experience••
	• There is no way to prove someone's being religious
	• Involvement in the practice of a religious community is required

â€¢ ••The Worship of God••
	• Is the third moment in the Concept of Religion
	• Corresponds to the return to and participation in God of all creatures through the sanctification of the Spirit

â€¢ ••The Cultus (Worship)••
	• Was not discussed until the 1827 lectures
	• Is the most concrete and richest of the moments in the Concept of Religion

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Cultus as Practical Religious Relationship••
	• The cultus is the practical religious relationship between the individual and God.
	• It involves action and the activity of the cultus, which makes the theoretical knowledge of God existentially relevant.

â€¢ ••The Role of Action in the Cultus••
	• Action is what accomplishes the unity between the individual and God.
	• The cultus is the including of the individual within themselves with God.

â€¢ ••The Definitive Cultic Act••
	• The definitive cultic act is the act of enjoyment, partaking, communion, or eucharist.
	• This act is the symbolic ingestion or assimilation of the crucified God.

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Cultus••
	• The cultus does not bring about reconciliation between God and humanity.
	• It rather presupposes reconciliation, which is already implicitly and explicitly accomplished by the grace of God.

â€¢ ••The Importance of the Cultus••
	• The cultus is more or less pushed to one side in modern Protestant churches.
	• The focus is solely on subjective faith, rather than participation in God through ritual practice.

â€¢ ••The Traditional Chapter on Mystical Union••
	• The traditional chapter on mystical union deals with the cultus.
	• This chapter is concerned with the innermost feature of the cultus, which is the feeling of union and reconciliation with God.

â€¢ ••The Relationship between the Mystical and Speculative••
	• The mystical union of the divine and human is at the heart of speculative theology.
	• This union is accomplished by a sensuous ritual action, which is more attuned to God's spiritual presence than hearing.

â€¢ ••The Displacement of the Word of God••
	• The proclamation of the Word of God seems to be displaced as the central liturgical act by the celebration of Communion.
	• This is a significant shift in the focus of worship.

â€¢ ••The Three Basic Forms of the Cultus••
	• The first form of the cultus is devotion (Andacht).
	• Devotion is not mere faith, but is present when faith becomes vivid and the subject prays.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Feeling in the Cultus••
	• Feeling is more attuned to God's spiritual presence than understanding.
	• The gratification of being with God in his grace is the innermost feature of the cultus.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Andacht (Devotion)••
	• A passionate and engaged thinking (Denken) that immerses oneself in the divine
	• Not merely objective, but a personal and emotional experience

â€¢ ••Cultic Forms••
	• Sacraments: reconciliation brought into feeling and present sensible consciousness
	• Sacrifice: the negation of the finite by offering it up to God
	• Repentance: offering one's heart or inmost self to God

â€¢ ••Repentance••
	• The highest form of cultus, where one renounces external things and offers one's heart to God
	• Issues in ethical life, which is the most genuine cultus

â€¢ ••Ethical Life••
	• Represents an extension and realization of the religious cultus
	• Social and political ethics are an extension of the religious cultus

â€¢ ••Philosophy as Cultus••
	• Philosophy is a continual cultus, with the object of the true and the absolute spirit
	• To know the true in its highest shape as absolute spirit, as God, is philosophy

â€¢ ••Philosophy as Theology••
	• Philosophy is theology, and the occupation with philosophy is the service and worship of God
	• Philosophy is the highest form of cultus, where one knows the true in its highest shape

â€¢ ••The Concept of God••
	• Develops in three elements: the idea of God in and for itself, the idea of God in representation and appearance, and the idea of God in community and as the Holy Spirit

â€¢ ••The Idea of God in and for Itself••
	• The immanent trinitarian relations, symbolized by divine persons
	• God subsists in abstract universality

â€¢ ••The Idea of God in Representation and Appearance••
	• The history of the natural and human worlds coming to focus on the figure of Christ
	• The universal sets itself forth or appears as finite, particular, and differentiated

â€¢ ••The Idea of God in Community and as the Holy Spirit••
	• The presence of God in the community of the Spirit
	• Anticipates the redemption and consummation of all things in God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God as Absolute Spirit••
	• Is the unity of spirit and nature
	• Is a process of self-cognition and self-differentiation
	• Is the unity of itself and an other (nature)

â€¢ ••Trinitarian Concept of God••
	• God distinguishes himself, determines himself, and posits an other to himself
	• Sublates the distinction, remaining present to himself
	• Is spirit only through this process of being brought forth

â€¢ ••Divine History••
	• God is in his eternity before the creation of the world
	• Creates the world and posits the separation between God and nature
	• Reconciles with itself what it distinguished from itself in its act of diremption

â€¢ ••Process of Reconciliation••
	• God restores to freedom and to his truth what is alien and has fallen away
	• This is the path and process of reconciliation
	• Results in the Holy Spirit, the Spirit present in its community

â€¢ ••Spirit as Subjectivity••
	• Is the infinite elasticity of substance that enables it to dirempt itself inwardly
	• Makes itself its own object through self-mediation
	• Is infinite substantial subjectivity

â€¢ ••God's Relationship with Nature••
	• Nature is other than spirit but ultimately not foreign or alien to spirit
	• Is encompassed within the divine life
	• Is posited as something within the range of absolute spirit

â€¢ ••Community of Recognition••
	• All of spirit's relationships occur within a divinely constituted community of recognition
	• The other that spirit absolves or releases to be an other is not posited as something beyond the range of absolute spirit

â€¢ ••Self-Cognition of Absolute Spirit••
	• Rises up out of the multiple consciousnesses of finite spirit
	• Is a process of being brought forth, or of bringing itself forth

â€¢ ••Spirit as a Process••
	• Brings itself forth out of nature, out of God's created otherness
	• Entails consciousness or cognition, which is always embodied
	• Is a process of self-differentiation and reconciliation

â€¢ ••Hegel's Speculative Redescription••
	• Sets forth the Christian metanarrative in the third part of the lectures on the philosophy of religion
	• Focuses on the concept of God as absolute spirit, which is intrinsically a trinitarian concept

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's finitude••
	• Hegel argues that God's finitude is a concept that limits God's nature
	• This finitude is brought inside God, making it a spatial way of thinking

â€¢ ••Spatial and temporal imagery••
	• Hegel uses spatial imagery to describe God's relations, but acknowledges it's conceptually inadequate
	• Temporal imagery is also inadequate for grasping the eternal divine history

â€¢ ••Absolute spirit••
	• Absolute spirit incorporates externality, but its relations are internal and moments of an organic intersubjectivity
	• These relations are genuinely other, but not totally other, and cannot sever their connection with God

â€¢ ••The whole (das Ganze)••
	• Hegel's totality is to be construed holistically, not totalistically
	• This means the other is not reduced to the same, but neither are they mutually exclusive atoms

â€¢ ••Hegel's holism••
	• Seeks a middle ground between atomism and monism
	• Aims to preserve the individuality of the other while maintaining their connection to the whole

â€¢ ••The two Trinities••
	• Hegel identifies two Trinities: an immanent and an economic Trinity
	• The immanent Trinity is God in eternity, the idea of God in and for itself

â€¢ ••The immanent Trinity••
	• Symbolized by the figures of Father, Son, and Spirit
	• Represents the inward dialectic of identity, difference, and mediation

â€¢ ••The economic Trinity••
	• Represents the creation of the natural and human worlds and their fall into evil
	• The appearance of God in Christ, the history of reconciliation and redemption

â€¢ ••The inclusive Trinity••
	• A more accurate designation for the economic Trinity
	• Includes the immanent Trinity as the first of its moments

â€¢ ••God's self-realization••
	• God is ideally complete apart from the world, but needs the world for real completion
	• The divine life is an engaged and serious love for others, not just a play of love with itself

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Inclusive Trinity••
	• Incorporates two essential aspects of Hegel's thought
	• Rejects a right-wing dissipation of the world into the logical realm
	• Rejects a left-wing reduction of Idea and Spirit to worldly finitude

â€¢ ••The Preworldly Trinity••
	• Is complete apart from its worldly manifestation
	• Is as real for philosophic comprehension as it is for Christian faith
	• Is wholly real apart from the world and wholly real in it

â€¢ ••The Relationship between the Preworldly and Worldly Trinity••
	• The preworldly reality is complete only because of the worldly manifestation
	• The converse judgement also holds: the worldly manifestation is complete only because of the preworldly reality

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Trinity••
	• Encompasses both the inner, preworldly dialectic of the divine life and the outward mediations by which the world is created and redeemed

â€¢ ••The Elements of the Trinity••
	• Are three elements or spheres of the divine life
	• Are not separable, but distinguishable
	• Are related spirally or concentrically, with each spiral overlapping and encompassing the previous ones

â€¢ ••The Priority of the Elements••
	• The elements cannot be prioritized or ranked
	• Classical theology prioritized the ideal self-relations of God
	• Modern theology prioritized the economic Trinity, but Hegel rejects this division

â€¢ ••The Pattern of the Trinity••
	• Is a trinity within a trinity, of Father within Son within Spirit
	• Each spiral overlaps and encompasses the previous ones
	• The pattern is only fully understood at the final spiral

â€¢ ••The Nature of God••
	• Is defined as universal spirit that particularizes itself
	• Is eternal and omnipresent
	• Participates in temporal and spatial realities

â€¢ ••The Relationship between God and the World••
	• The world is a moment within God
	• The distinction between God and the world is an abstraction
	• The world remains world, not-God within God

â€¢ ••The Inclusive Trinity and Christian Faith••
	• Enables us to see that God's external relations are not something extrinsic to God
	• Incorporates external relations, making them internal to God without annihilating their exteriority

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Trinity as a rational mystery••
	• The Trinity is a mystical concept that is intelligible to a certain way of thinking.
	• This way of thinking comprehends distinctions as logical elements of a living rational process.
• ••The problem with representational language••
	• The Christian doctrine of the Trinity is often couched in representational language that introduces numbers and persons.
	• This language is problematic because it implies a digital, quantitative understanding of the Trinity.
• ••The issue with numbers••
	• The Trinity cannot be understood as a matter of quantity or counting.
	• Counting yields the conclusion that three equals one, which is an unmathematical and paradoxical conclusion.
• ••The limitations of digital thinking••
	• Digital thinking cannot grasp how unity manifests itself in diversity and difference.
	• Reason can employ relationships of the understanding, but only insofar as it destroys the forms of the understanding.
• ••The problem with familial relationships••
	• Introducing familial relationships (e.g. Father and Son) is a childlike and figurative way of understanding the Trinity.
	• These relationships are not comparable to the true nature of the Trinity.
• ••The true nature of the Trinity••
	• The Trinity is the universal working through its process, differentiating itself and bringing forth what is already there from the beginning.
	• The differentiation is an internal process, not an external one.
• ••The role of the Father••
	• The Father is not a specific divine person, but a symbol designating the kingdom of the immanent Trinity.
	• The Father represents the movement by which God is present to godself through a differentiation that has not yet been replicated in the world.
• ••The relationship between the Father and the Son••
	• The abstractness of the Father is given up in the Son, which is a kind of death.
	• The negation of this negation is the unity of Father and Son, which is the Spirit.
• ••The significance of the Spirit••
	• The Spirit is the most concrete and encompassing of the trinitarian symbols.
	• The Spirit is beyond gender distinctions and is the most retrievable of the classical trinitarian figures.
• ••The sublation of the Father and the Son••
	• The Father and the Son are sublated (annulled and preserved) in the Spirit.
	• This sublation minimizes gender-specific language about God.
• ••The implications of Hegel's understanding of the Trinity••
	• Hegel's understanding of the Trinity emphasizes the internal, dialectical process of God's self-differentiation.
	• This understanding has implications for how we think about God and the nature of the Trinity.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's view of God's attributes••
	• Do not describe God's relations to the world or humanity
	• Do not designate different external aspects of God
	• Are not particular characteristics, but rather refer to trinitarian relations

â€¢ ••Predicates and God's nature••
	• Predicates are not appropriate to the nature of God
	• They work only as they relate to the movement by which God is God
	• They proceed from the modes of divine self-differentiation

â€¢ ••Hegel's approach to the Trinity••
	• Does not attend to technicalities of the classical trinitarian debates
	• Does not engage with questions of the homoousian or procession of the Spirit

â€¢ ••Hegel's speculative reconstruction of the Trinity••
	• Accords with an orthodox version of the doctrine
	• Enhances the divinity of God in the second and third moments of the divine life

â€¢ ••Hegel's view of the Spirit••
	• Proceeds of necessity from the second as well as the first moment
	• Links and completes the first and second moments

â€¢ ••Comparison with other models of the Trinity••
	• Is closer to Augustine's trialectical model than to Thomas Aquinas' triangular model
	• Shares similarities with Augustine's model, despite no direct influence

â€¢ ••Hegel's knowledge of theological sources••
	• Was familiar with Gnostic and Neoplatonic sources
	• Had no direct knowledge of Augustine's theology

â€¢ ••The role of the third person in the Trinity••
	• Is logically necessary for Augustine and Hegel
	• Is not necessary for Aquinas, who views the Spirit as a third person dependent on and subordinate to the Father and Son

â€¢ ••Hegel's view of God as absolute spirit••
	• Understands God as infinite personality or infinite subjectivity
	• Constitutes distinctions within itself but remains a single, unified entity

â€¢ ••Implications of Hegel's view of the Trinity••
	• Challenges traditional notions of the Trinity and God's attributes
	• Offers a speculative reconstruction of the doctrine that enhances the divinity of God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's Nature••
	• God is a living, dynamic process
	• God is love, which is a distinguishing of two and the sublation of the distinction
	• God's loving and living are superabundant, overflowing into creation

â€¢ ••Personality and Relationships••
	• Personality is a living play of relationships, formed by love and friendship
	• Relationships are essential to understanding God's nature
	• Love is a distinguishing of two, who are not distinguished for each other

â€¢ ••Speculative Theology••
	• Speculative theology comprehends the rationality of the mystery of God
	• It introduces the analogy of ethical relationships to understand God's nature
	• It describes the nature of personality and relationships

â€¢ ••Contradiction and Unity••
	• Everything concrete and living contains contradiction within itself
	• The contradiction is resolved in the idea, and the resolution is spiritual unity
	• The living thing is an example of what cannot be grasped by the understanding

â€¢ ••God's Consciousness••
	• God is conscious of himself only in the other, in absolute externalization
	• God's consciousness is a spiritual unity in the form of feeling
	• This is similar to the identity of one with the other in relationships of friendship, love, and family

â€¢ ••Life and Dialectic••
	• Life has drives and needs, which create distinction within itself
	• The satisfaction of needs annuls the contradiction, but it emerges again
	• Life maintains itself by entering into differentiation and struggling with particularity

â€¢ ••Trinitarian Dialectic••
	• The trinitarian dialectic is necessary for love and life
	• It involves the distinguishing of two and the sublation of the distinction
	• It is a continuous process of diversification and unification

â€¢ ••God's Relationship with Creation••
	• God's loving and living pour outward into the creation of a finite world
	• God loves, struggles with, suffers in, and saves the world
	• The inward play of love with itself becomes deadly serious in creation

â€¢ ••God's Nature as Love and Life••
	• It is equally true to say that God is love and God is life
	• God's loving and living are superabundant and overflowing into creation
	• God's nature is a dynamic, living process

â€¢ ••Understanding God's Nature••
	• Speculative theology is necessary to comprehend the rationality of the mystery of God
	• It requires the introduction of the analogy of ethical relationships
	• It involves the conversion of logical categories into ethical relationships

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's relationship to the world••
	• The world is not spatially exterior to God, but is encompassed within the divine life.
	• God's act of creation enlarges the divine life.

â€¢ ••Personality in God••
	• Personality is the infinite subjectivity of self-certainty.
	• Personality includes plurality within itself, and is not limited to three persons or centers of consciousness.

â€¢ ••The Trinity as a familial person••
	• God is a familial or corporate person, incorporating sexual distinction within itself.
	• God is not gendered.

â€¢ ••The Trinity as one person••
	• The Trinity is comprised of one person, or personality (PersÃ¶nlichkeit) as such.
	• Personality is accomplished when persons maintain themselves in love and friendship.

â€¢ ••Love and personality••
	• Love means giving up particularity and achieving universality.
	• The truth of personality is found in being immersed in the other.

â€¢ ••The divine personality••
	• God is the utterly concrete, universal personality.
	• An infinity of persons contributes to the divine personality.

â€¢ ••God's family••
	• God's family is the whole earth, the whole realm of spirits.
	• The divine personality is not limited to purely interior relations.

â€¢ ••Traces of the Trinity in other religions••
	• Reminiscences and traces of the Trinity are found in earlier religions such as Hinduism.
	• The triad of Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva is an example of a triad in Hinduism.

â€¢ ••Precedents for the Trinity••
	• The form of triplicity was found in ancient Greek philosophy, such as in Plato and Aristotle.
	• Kant also used the form of triplicity as a schema for thought.

â€¢ ••Hegel's point about precedents••
	• Hegel's point in citing these precedents is that the doctrine of the Trinity is not unique to Christianity.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Triadic Structure••
	• Is deeply ingrained in human consciousness
	• Reflects something basic about the structure of reality itself
• ••Ancient Understanding of the Triadic Structure••
	• The ancients did not fully understand the significance of the triadic structure
	• It contained the absolute consciousness of truth
• ••Hegel's Critique of Enlightenment Theologians••
	• Challenged theologians who neglected or downplayed the doctrine of the Trinity
	• Criticized Neopietist F.A.G. Tholuck for regarding the Trinity as pagan
• ••Hegel's Study of Neoplatonism and Gnosticism••
	• Devoted attention to Neoplatonists and Gnostic systems of Valentinus and the Valentinians
	• Recognized the distinction between the divine One and the self-emerging or self-manifesting of God
• ••Precedents for the Triadic Structure••
	• Found in Aristotle's De caelo, Plato's Timaeus, and Proclus' Platonic Theology
	• Also found in Kant's Critique of Pure Reason, where he claims that the third category in each class arises from the combination of the first two
• ••Hegel's View of the Trinity••
	• Saw the Trinity as a representation of the absolute consciousness of truth
	• Recognized the presence of the Trinity in everything and everywhere
• ••Gnosticism and the Triadic Structure••
	• Criticized Gnosticism for introducing a dualism between the divine One and the natural world
	• Saw the Gnostic view of the Trinity as inadequate and not yet rational
• ••Jacob Boehme's Recognition of the Trinity••
	• Recognized the Trinity as universal and present in everything and everywhere
	• Saw the Trinity as a ferment in the human mind that needed to be developed
• ••Hegel's Agenda••
	• To recognize the presence of the Trinity in everything and everywhere
	• To develop a pure form of thinking that could grasp the totality of the Trinity
• ••Influence of Valentinian Gnosticism on Hegel••
	• Cyril O'Regan discusses the influence of Valentinian Gnosticism on Hegel's thought
	• Hegel's views on the Trinity were influenced by his study of Gnosticism and Neoplatonism.

â€¢ ••The Trinity as the Essential Nature of God••
	• The Trinity is the essential and sole nature of God
	• It is not something above and beyond, but rather the goal of cognition to know the truth in particular things
	• The Trinity is the truth not only about God, but about the whole of reality in all its determinacy and particularity

â€¢ ••The Trinity as the Structure of Reality••
	• The created world images the Creator
	• The structure of the whole is replicated in every part
	• The Trinity is the truth about the whole of reality, including its determinacy and particularity

â€¢ ••Creation as the Kingdom of the Son••
	• The creation of the world is the second element or sphere of the divine life
	• It involves the fall into estrangement and reconciliation through Christ
	• The condition of possibility for creation is the internal differentiation within the divine life

â€¢ ••Internal Differentiation and External Diremption••
	• Internal differentiation within the divine life makes God a subject and not a windowless monad
	• God creates out of God's own internal fecundity or generative power
	• The differentiation is posited as existentially other in the act of creation

â€¢ ••Appearance and Manifestation••
	• The differentiation becomes appearance or manifestation (Erscheinung)
	• It is no longer absolute but finite spirit that is posited
	• The ideal and eternal Son becomes a real flesh-and-blood historical Son

â€¢ ••The Movement from Schein to Erscheinung••
	• The movement from Schein (show) to Erscheinung (appearance) is a key moment in the creation of the world
	• Everything that appears also disappears, and thus the element of Schein lingers in the Erscheinung

â€¢ ••The Absolute Idea and Its Subjective Aspect••
	• The absolute idea is complete in itself
	• However, in its subjective aspect, it is not yet concrete, not yet reflected into itself, and not yet posited as differentiated

â€¢ ••The Two Sides of Appearance••
	• Appearance can be grasped from two sides: the side of the divine idea and the side of the Son
	• From the side of the divine idea, spirit posits itself in the mode of particularity, but remains the eternal idea
	• From the side of the Son, the Son unites the two modalities of universal and particular, identity and difference

â€¢ ••The Modalities of Universal and Particular••
	• The two modalities of universal and particular, identity and difference, are to be posited as distinct
	• However, they are not truly distinct, and the spatio-temporal world is only a passing instant in the divine life

â€¢ ••The Diachronic and Synchronic Aspects of Creation••
	• The creation of the world involves a diachronic (temporal) and synchronic (simultaneous) aspect
	• The spatio-temporal world is only a passing instant in the divine life, and the two modalities of universal and particular are not truly distinct.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Creation as a Process••
	• Hegel describes creation as a process of positing (Setzen) and release (Entlassen)
	• Creation is an intellectual act, where God creates by speaking a word

â€¢ ••Creation as a Primal Division••
	• Creation is a primal division (Urteil) that grants independence to the side of other-being
	• This division is an act of goodness that grants the idea as a whole to the side of other-being in its estrangement

â€¢ ••Two Judgements in Creation••
	• Two judgements or divisions occur: one interior to the divine life and the other positing the world in distinction from God
	• These two judgements are implicitly the same, but representation holds them apart as distinct acts

â€¢ ••God's Differentiation••
	• God's self-differentiation is the ground for the possibility of creating a world of nature and finite spirit
	• This differentiation is the explicitly negative moment of other-being, which has no truth but is only a moment

â€¢ ••The Disappearing Moment of Appearance••
	• From God's point of view, the world is a disappearing instant, called into being by a fleeting word
	• The creative act is non-objectifiable, like a flash of light or a vanishing sound

â€¢ ••The World's Dependence on God••
	• The world is sustained solely by the divine goodness
	• Creation hovers on the brink of non-being and is dependent on God's continued existence

â€¢ ••Creation and Appearance••
	• Without creation and appearance, we have a relationship of God to himself only
	• The act of differentiation is a movement of love with itself, which does not arrive at the seriousness of other-being

â€¢ ••The Distinguished Elements••
	• The distinguished elements are posited as the same, but have not yet come to be defined as distinctly determined
	• This is what must happen for the other within God, the eternal Son, to obtain its determination

â€¢ ••The Relationship between God and the World••
	• The world is a moment of appearance in God's eternity
	• The world's existence is dependent on God's continued existence and creative act

â€¢ ••The Qualitative Difference between God and the World••
	• There is a qualitative difference between the eternity of God and the temporality of creation
	• The world's existence is a radical dependence on God, and its non-being is always a possibility.

â€¢ ••The necessity of otherness••
	• Otherness is necessary for difference to exist
	• What is distinguished must be the otherness of an entity

â€¢ ••The release of the other••

	• This other is the world as such

â€¢ ••The context of release••
	• The absolute idea's freedom is such that it can release the otherness that is intrinsic to its own being
	• An unfree or partially free being would cling to its otherness as essential to its self-constitution

â€¢ ••God's freedom and the world••
	• God's radical freedom is complete in and for itself
	• God creates the world without any threat to its own freedom, which is gratuitous and overflowing

	• The world remains a part of God's life, even in its otherness from God

â€¢ ••The relationship between God and the world••

â€¢ ••Hegel's Concern••
	• Hegel is concerned with explaining the fall into estrangement and evil
	• He rejects the idea of an original, paradisaical state of human existence

â€¢ ••Critique of Romantic Mythology••
	• Hegel criticizes philosophers like Friedrich Schlegel and Friedrich Schelling for idealizing a state of original perfection
	• He rejects the idea that humanity is good by nature, attributing this doctrine to Rousseau rather than Kant

â€¢ ••Implications for Pedagogy••
	• Hegel argues that a view that idealizes natural principles and rejects discipline and acquired knowledge is flawed
	• He believes that education should focus on developing human reason and knowledge

â€¢ ••The Story of the Fall••
	• The Bible describes the transition from innocence to responsibility and guilt as a fall from the divine idea
	• This fall is also a rise into the divine idea and the image of God

â€¢ ••Paradoxes in the Story••
	• Hegel notes the contradictions in the story, such as why God would forbid knowledge that elevates humans into the divine likeness
	• He argues that these contradictions reflect the ambiguities present in consciousness and knowledge

â€¢ ••Speculative Thinking••
	• Hegel believes that speculative thinking is necessary to grasp the ambiguities present in consciousness and knowledge
	• He sees the story of the fall as a deep insight into the eternal history of humanity

â€¢ ••The Significance of the Story••
	• Hegel notes that the story of the fall is not mentioned again in the Hebrew Bible
	• It is Christians who have developed the connection between the first Adam and the second Adam, Christ

â€¢ ••The Second Adam••
	• The second Adam, Christ, represents the likeness of God that is to be regained
	• This promise is the infinite side of knowledge, which slumbered in the Jewish people

	• His 1824 and 1827 lectures add little to the exegesis contained in the manuscript

â€¢ ••The Significance of Labour••
	• Hegel notes that the punishment for disobedience is labour, having to work for a living
	• However, such labouring is also the stamp of humanity's higher spiritual nature

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Story of the Fall••
	• Hegel views the story of the fall as a myth, not history
	• The story is meant to illustrate the human condition, not provide a literal account of events

â€¢ ••The Role of Discipline and Punishment••
	• Hegel argues that discipline and punishment are necessary for human progress
	• Without them, humanity would remain in a state of infancy

â€¢ ••The Paradox of Human Nature••
	• The condition for the possibility of good also includes the possibility of evil
	• This paradox is a fundamental aspect of human nature

â€¢ ••The Knowledge that Wounds••
	• The knowledge that makes a human being also wounds her
	• This paradox is a central theme in Hegel's philosophy

â€¢ ••The Story of the Fall as a Representation••
	• The story of the fall is a representation of two conditions: the immediate, natural state of desire and the state of consciousness
	• Consciousness brings with it the knowledge of the good, but also the possibility of evil

â€¢ ••The Origin of Evil••
	• Evil arises from the separation between consciousness and desire
	• This separation is a fundamental aspect of human existence

â€¢ ••The Role of Cognition in Evil••
	• Cognition (Erkenntnis) posits the antithesis in which evil is found
	• Evil is the consciousness of being-for-myself in opposition to an external nature and the objective truth

â€¢ ••The Nature of Evil••
	• Evil is the singularization of oneself against others
	• This singularization is a free choice for which humans are responsible

â€¢ ••The Divine Principle of Turning••
	• The divine principle of turning, or return to self, is present in cognition
	• This principle gives the wound and heals it, because it is the principle of spirit and truth

â€¢ ••The Source of Reconciliation••
	• The separation that produces evil is also the source of reconciliation
	• This separation is the point where humanity must progress in order to realize its spiritual potential

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Nature of Evil••
	• Evil is closely connected to consciousness and being-for-self
	• It arises from the opposition between the individual and the universal truth
	• This opposition is a necessary step in the progression to being-for-self, but it also leads to evil

â€¢ ••The Tragic Element••
	• The cost of freedom is the misuse of freedom
	• Estrangement and reconciliation have the same source: consciousness and cognition
	• Reconciliation requires a reorientation of consciousness away from self-centredness and singularization

â€¢ ••The Role of Language••
	• Language is used to describe the separation between consciousness and the universal truth
	• This separation is the seat of evil
	• Language is also used to describe the reconciliation between consciousness and the universal truth

â€¢ ••The Connection between Good and Evil••
	• Knowledge brings knowledge of both good and evil
	• We are intrinsically evil because we posit divisions among ourselves, between humans and the world, and between humans and God

â€¢ ••The State of Rupture••
	• We exist in a state of rupture or antithesis vis-a-vis both God and the world
	• This rupture leads to infinite anguish and unhappiness

â€¢ ••Infinite Anguish••
	• Infinite anguish arises from the separation from God
	• It is the anguish of absorption into ourselves and the loss of goodness
	• This was the condition of Judaism in late antiquity

â€¢ ••Unhappiness••
	• Unhappiness arises from the awareness that we are not satisfied in the world
	• We are driven back into ourselves, seeking happiness in inner harmony
	• This was the condition of the Roman world at the beginning of the common era

â€¢ ••The Antithesis••
	• The antithesis between infinite and finite is abstractly one-sided
	• It is characterized by an unbridgeable gap between the infinite and the finite
	• This antithesis is refined in the concept of the preceding religions

â€¢ ••The Appearance of Christ••
	• The appearance of Christ is a transition into the theme of reconciliation
	• It is a proof of the necessary appearance of Christ in a particular time and place
	• This appearance is a response to the enduring and universal marks of religious alienation

â€¢ ••The Application to Modern Times••
	• Jewish anguish reappears in the modern world in the form of Protestant piety
	• This anguish is a particular application of Hegel's rendition of anguish and unhappiness to his own time and place

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Evil••
	• Evil is a distortion and perversion of what is highest in humanity
	• It has a potency that far outstrips natural destruction
	• It is connected to knowledge and reason, but also has irrational aspects

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of Enlightenment Rationalism••
	• Roman unhappiness is reflected in Enlightenment rationalism
	• This rationalism valorizes the finite and is vulnerable to secularism and atheism

â€¢ ••Hegel's Trivialization of Evil?••
	• Some argue that Hegel trivializes the problem of evil by connecting it too closely with knowledge
	• However, Hegel sees evil as a distortion of reason, not its displacement

â€¢ ••The Role of Reason in Overcoming Evil••
	• Reason on its own cannot overcome the capacity for self-deception that evil has
	• A redemption of reason is required to overcome evil

â€¢ ••Hegel's Omission of Institutional Evil••
	• Hegel does not reflect much on the reinforcement and intensification of personal evil in institutional structures and social ideologies
	• He is aware of the tragic and violent character of human history, but does not envision radical evil like the Holocaust

â€¢ ••The Challenge of Postmodernity••
	• The experience of evil in the past century calls for a fundamental rethinking of Hegel's philosophy
	• This challenge helps explain why the 1827 lectures treat themes of knowledge, estrangement, and evil differently

â€¢ ••The Incarnation of God in Christ••
	• The incarnation of God in Christ is a universal redemptive event
	• It occurred under specific religio-cultural conditions in a particular time and place

â€¢ ••The Possibility of Reconciliation••
	• The possibility of reconciliation consists in humanity's coming to consciousness of the universal in and for itself (i.e. God)
	• This involves humanity's recognition of its own essence and infinity, from which it has been estranged by its willing of singular selfishness

â€¢ ••The Necessity of Reconciliation••
	• Reconciliation is necessary because humanity's estrangement from God has led to unhappiness and suffering
	• The incarnation of God in Christ is the necessary response to this estrangement

â€¢ ••The Actuality of Reconciliation••
	• The actuality of reconciliation is established through the philosophical arguments of the 1821 manuscript
	• Hegel reconstructs the concepts of reconciliation and incarnation, as well as other christological categories, in this process

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Goal of Humanity••
	• The absolute truth and goal of the will
	• Recognized as the goal of infinite freedom
	• Elevates humans above all locality, nationality, and condition

â€¢ ••The Condition of Possibility for Reconciliation••
	• Humanity bears within itself the divine idea
	• The idea of the unity of divine and human nature
	• This idea is humanity's substantial, though lost or distorted, nature

â€¢ ••The Vocation of Humanity••
	• To regain its own nature as a divine gift
	• Not through striving or merit, but through divine gift

â€¢ ••The Significance of Incarnation••
	• The becoming-flesh or becoming-human of divinity
	• A moment in the process of the divine nature
	• Happens once in a revelatorily definitive way

â€¢ ••Reconciliation••
	• Has nothing to do with extrinsic payment of a debt or atonement for sin
	• Signifies the overcoming of estrangement and the reunification of God and humanity

â€¢ ••The Meaning of Reconciliation••
	• The unity of the finite and infinite
	• The unity of divine and human nature
	• Spiritual essentiality, which bridges all dichotomies

â€¢ ••The Condition of Possibility for Reconciliation••
	• Unity is the original and final divine-human condition
	• Not something that needs to be brought about by an extrinsic action

â€¢ ••Incarnation and Reconciliation••
	• Closely connected, if not ultimately synonymous terms
	• Both involve the unity of divine and human nature

â€¢ ••The Subject's Role in Reconciliation••
	• The subject cannot produce reconciliation by its own agency
	• Instead, reconciliation is a presupposition that appears to the subject

â€¢ ••The Standpoint of Infinite Flight••
	• The standpoint at which we now find ourselves
	• The truth must appear to the subject as a presupposition at this standpoint

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Reconciliation as the original divine-human condition••
	• Affirming reconciliation does not deny the present human condition of anguish and estrangement
	• The question is how reconciliation can appear and overcome the reality of estrangement

â€¢ ••The 1827 lectures on reconciliation••
	• Implicitly, reconciliation is eternally accomplished and the antithesis between divinity and humanity is overcome in principle
	• The antithesis must be intensified to its greatest extreme for humans to become aware of the need for atonement

â€¢ ••God as the one who accomplishes reconciliation••
	• God is the one who distinguishes himself from himself, positing an other and remaining identical with himself
	• The otherness of God is what eternally posits and sublates itself, making God to be God

â€¢ ••God's relationship to evil••
	• God is on both sides of the antithesis, undergoing the anguish of alienation and overcoming it
	• God incorporates estrangement into the divine life as a negated negative

â€¢ ••Reconciliation as an eternal process••
	• Reconciliation is possible because God is an eternal process of reconciliation
	• The only question is how human beings become aware of this process and participate in it

â€¢ ••The necessity of reconciliation••
	• The knowledge of reconciliation must come to humanity as something empirically certain
	• This knowledge must appear in a wholly temporal, ordinary worldly appearance in a single human being

â€¢ ••The actuality of reconciliation••
	• The idea of divine-human unity must appear as a single human being who is recognized to be the Son of God
	• This human being is the divine idea, not just a teacher or higher being

â€¢ ••The immediate certainty of divinity••
	• Fallen and embodied beings need the immediate certainty and presence of divinity
	• This is the way in which the Is (das Ist) exists for natural consciousness

â€¢ ••God's objectivity in humanity••
	• Gods objectivity is realized in the whole of humanity immediately
	• This is an essential theme in Hegel's thought

â€¢ ••The relationship between God and humanity••
	• God is on both sides of the antithesis, and reconciliation is possible because God is an eternal process of reconciliation
	• Human beings must become aware of this process and participate in it in order to achieve reconciliation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's Incarnate Presence••
	• God's presence is found in all human suffering
	• Reconciliation requires the action of humanity as a whole
	• God's infinitude is worthy of human love, which requires a world's anguish

â€¢ ••Necessity of God's Appearance••
	• God must appear in the world to become a spiritual God
	• Humans need a concrete verification of their redemption
	• God's appearance in the world is necessary for unity with humanity

â€¢ ••Argument from Necessity••
	• The unity of divine and human nature must be known with certainty
	• This requires God to appear in the world in an immediate and external way
	• God's appearance in the world is necessary for humanity to know its redemption

â€¢ ••Form of God's Appearance••
	• God's appearance can take many forms, including a single human being or a multiplicity of incarnations
	• The form of appearance is determined by the actualities of history
	• God's appearance is not predetermined, but is a necessary condition for reconciliation

â€¢ ••Role of Individuality••
	• The universal is present in the concrete subjectivity of individual human beings
	• Subjectivity is the place where the connection occurs between the infinite and the finite
	• Individuality is the necessary condition for the appearance of God's unity with humanity

â€¢ ••Uniqueness of Subjectivity••
	• Each subject exists on its own account and stands out from others
	• There must be only one individual in whom the idea of divine human unity appears in a revelatory definitive way
	• In one individual, all humanity is encompassed

â€¢ ••Abstraction of Divinity••
	• If divinity appears in several individuals, it becomes an abstraction
	• The uniqueness of subjectivity is necessary for the appearance of God's unity with humanity
	• A single individual is necessary for the revelation of divine human unity

â€¢ ••Jesus as the Particular Person••
	• Jesus of Nazareth is the particular person in whom the idea of divine human unity appears
	• Jesus is the individual in whom the universal is present in a concrete and revelatory way
	• Jesus is the necessary condition for the reconciliation of humanity with God

â€¢ ••Reconciliation in and through a Single Human Being••
	• Reconciliation is realized in the form of human individuality
	• The idea of reconciliation is realized in a single human individual
	• Jesus is the particular person in whom the idea of divine human unity appears

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• The argument for actuality runs through three phases: individuality, uniqueness, and Jesus as the particular person
	• The uniqueness of subjectivity is necessary for the appearance of God's unity with humanity
	• A single individual is necessary for the revelation of divine human unity.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's argument for Christ's uniqueness••
	• Christ is the only one who fully realizes the infinite idea of humanity
	• Other divine men, such as those venerated by the Greeks, Romans, and Hindus, are not the same as Christ

â€¢ ••God's appearance in the flesh••
	• God appears in sensible presence and has no other shape than that of the singular human being
	• This is the appearance of God in the flesh, a monstrous reality that entails the conjunction of two natures

â€¢ ••The shape of God in human shape••
	• The shape of God in human shape is a theme already enunciated in The Spirit of Christianity and Its Fate
	• It entails the conjunction of two natures that are thought to be intrinsically different but share a common essence, that of spirit

â€¢ ••The necessity of a singular human being••
	• The unity of divine and human nature must appear in just one human being
	• This is not just a matter of singularity in general, but of concrete, sensible reality

â€¢ ••The certainty of intuiting and sensing••
	• The substantial unity of God and humanity lies beyond immediate consciousness and ordinary knowledge
	• This is why the unity in question must appear for others as a singular human being set apart

â€¢ ••The problem of abstraction••
	• Humanity as a whole is an abstraction
	• It is not clear why divinity should be regarded as any less concrete in several singular incarnations in different religio-cultural settings

â€¢ ••The prejudice of unity over diversity••
	• The insistence on oneness, on once and for all or one and only, reflects a prejudice that privileges unity over diversity
	• This is why Hegel's argument for the uniqueness of Christ does not resonate well with the recognition of a plurality of living world religions

â€¢ ••The mediator of redemption••
	• The mediator of redemption is set apart from other human beings who are in need of what is being mediated
	• This is not a persuasive argument for only one such mediator; there could be several who are set apart and work in a diversity of ways

â€¢ ••The possibility and necessity of reconciliation••
	• Hegel's arguments for the possibility and necessity of reconciliation point in the direction of diversity
	• His insistence on the role of concrete, singular individuals is well-taken, but his claim that one such individual stands above all the others does not resonate well with the recognition of a plurality of living world religions

â€¢ ••The tension between Hegel's arguments and postmodern perspectives••
	• Hegel's arguments for the uniqueness of Christ and the necessity of a singular human being do not resonate well with postmodern perspectives that recognize a plurality of living world religions and the diversity of savior figures.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Argument for Actuality••
	• Hegel's argument for actuality is not required by his own philosophical principles
	• He is bending his argument to accommodate normative Christian doctrine

â€¢ ••The Idea of Reconciliation••
	• The idea of reconciliation is realized in a single individual: Jesus of Nazareth
	• This individual is the Christ, the divine-human unity

â€¢ ••The Singularity of the Christ••
	• Hegel argues that the divine-human unity becomes objective in only one individual, not in several incarnations
	• This singularity is not necessarily implicit in the idea of divine-human unity

â€¢ ••The Focus on Individuality••
	• The consummation of reality in immediate singular individuality is the most beautiful point of the Christian religion
	• This focus on individuality is eloquently stated

â€¢ ••The Exclusivity of the Christ••
	• Hegel argues that there is only one Son, one exclusive of other finite beings
	• This claim is based on metaphorical rather than conceptual categories

â€¢ ••The Problem of Cultural Diversity••
	• The argument is reinforced by a trinitarian claim, but this may not be true given the fact of cultural diversity
	• No single human subject can represent the whole of humanity

â€¢ ••The Evidence for Jesus as the Christ••
	• The claim that Jesus of Nazareth is the Christ is above all a claim of faith
	• However, it is also congruent with historical fact

â€¢ ••The Revelatory Impact of Jesus••
	• Jesus' life, death, and resurrection are revelatory and ontologically adequate to the idea of divine-human unity
	• Reconciliation occurs in actuality when the unity appears, is recognized, and is put into practice

â€¢ ••The Connection between the Revelatory and Ontological••
	• The revelatory impact of Jesus follows from the fullness of God's presence in him
	• The revelatory and ontological are connected

â€¢ ••The Judgement of Faith••
	• The discernment of the time and place of God's appearance in Jesus is based on the inner witness of the Spirit
	• This judgement of faith is not based on miracle or historical proof

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Hegel's View of Christ••
	• Christ is seen as a teacher of humanity and a messenger of God
	• He is regarded as a martyr to the truth, similar to Socrates
	• Hegel identifies two perspectives on Christ: the human, historical perspective and the religious perspective
• ••The Human, Historical Perspective••
	• Christ is viewed as a human being in all external contingencies and temporal exigencies
	• He is born like every other human being and has common human needs
	• He does not share the corruption, passions, and evil inclinations of others
• ••Christ as Teacher••
	• Christ's teaching office is the focus of Hegel's interest
	• His teaching concerns the universal, the kingdom of God, and the eternal as a homeland for spirit
	• The topic of his teaching is not God alone, but the kingdom of God
• ••Comparison with Socrates••
	• Hegel compares Christ with Socrates as a teacher who brought inwardness to consciousness
	• Christ's teaching about the kingdom of heaven and purity of heart contains an infinitely greater depth than Socrates'
	• Christ is even more excellent than Socrates because he was without sin
• ••The Religious Perspective••
	• Christ is identified as the God-man, the one in whom God is definitively present and revealed
	• This perspective depends on the witness of the Spirit
	• It is distinct from the human, historical perspective, but not mutually exclusive
• ••The Relationship between the Two Perspectives••
	• The human, historical perspective confirms or corroborates the spiritual perspective
	• Faith and spiritual discernment are necessary for understanding Christ
	• The two perspectives are not mutually exclusive, but rather complementary
• ••The Significance of Christ's Teaching••
	• Christ's teaching is integrated with his life and destiny
	• His teaching concerns the universal, the kingdom of God, and the eternal as a homeland for spirit
	• It signifies a coherence between what he proclaims and who he is
• ••The Focus of Christ's Teaching••
	• Christ's teaching focuses on inner purity of heart and the kingdom of heaven
	• It is not concerned with external authority or worldly pursuits
	• It is a call to inwardness and spiritual transformation
• ••The Importance of Faith••
	• Faith is necessary for understanding Christ
	• It refers back to the story of his life prior to death
	• It is distinct from unbelief, but not mutually exclusive
• ••The Mythical Nature of the Virgin Birth••
	• Hegel does not discuss the story of the virgin birth
	• He regards it as mythical and not relevant to his understanding of Christ

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Jesus' Teaching••
	• Emphasizes love as the central moral imperative
	• Includes love of neighbors, love among disciples, and love of enemies
• ••Breaking Away from Established Norms••
	• Jesus breaks away from established norms, including Jewish ordinances
	• Proclaims the kingdom of God, which is at hand
• ••Revolutionary Aspect of Teaching••
	• Implores followers to not be anxious about worldly affairs
	• Prepares followers to break familial attachments
• ••Relationship of Christ to God••
	• Jesus' self-references as Son of God or Son of Man
	• Assumes the Gospels provide a reliable account of Jesus' words
• ••Jesus' Life and Teaching••
	• Conforms to his teaching and is adequate to the idea of divine-human unity
	• Brings the kingdom of God to life as a spiritual community
• ••Content of Jesus' Life••
	• The kingdom of God that he proclaims
	• Constitutes his divinity through his teaching
• ••Jesus' Teaching as a New World••
	• Provides a new concept of God
	• Produces a universal soil for the concept of God in the form of the kingdom of God
• ••Revolutionary Doctrine••
	• Leaves standing institutions aside and partly destroys and overthrows them
	• Emphasizes perfect independence of worldly relationships
• ••Love as the Principal Commandment••
	• Includes mutual love of the community
	• Includes particular duties and responsibilities
• ••Jesus' Teaching as a Spiritual Community••
	• Brings the kingdom of God to life as a spiritual community
	• Constitutes the existence of spirit through his teaching

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Jesus' Teaching••
	• Jesus' teaching is a new world, a new consciousness of humanity, and a new religion.
	• It is a state of affairs, the kingdom of God, where God rules in the heart and has dominion.

â€¢ ••Polemical Aspect of Jesus' Teaching••
	• Jesus' teaching is opposed to external conditions and the later doctrine of the church.
	• It demands that one remove oneself from finite things and elevate oneself to an infinite energy.

â€¢ ••Jesus as a Prophet••
	• Jesus speaks not merely as a teacher, but as a prophet.
	• He expresses God's demand immediately, without mediation.

â€¢ ••God's Presence in Jesus••
	• God speaks through Jesus, and Jesus' life is the Spirit in the truth.
	• The divine presence is essentially identical with Jesus' humanity.

â€¢ ••The Incarnation••
	• Hegel's construal of the doctrine of the incarnation is that divinity and humanity fuse in the shape of a human teacher-prophet.
	• God works within humanity without cancelling but rather strengthening human subjectivity and personality.

â€¢ ••Orthodox Christology••
	• Hegel rejects the traditional doctrine of the incarnation, with its two natures and one person.
	• He replaces it with a new philosophical intuition that sees divinity and humanity as connected.

â€¢ ••The Role of History••
	• Hegel regards Jesus' teaching as having being said from one side, the human historical side.
	• History serves as a confirmation or corroboration of the witness of faith.

â€¢ ••Faith and History••
	• Without a congruence between faith and history, faith would be undercut.
	• Faith would have as its object a mythical figure, not a real historical person.

â€¢ ••Jesus' Humanity••
	• Jesus is the Son of Man, a representative human being.
	• His humanity is essential to his role as a teacher-prophet.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Jesus' Speech••
	• Jesus' speech is not an external revelation, but rather God's working in a human being.
	• God speaks through human speech, not in an extrinsic, supernatural way.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Religious Perspective••
	• Emerges after and in light of the death and resurrection of Christ
	• Involves the contemplation of faith, of the Holy Spirit, and the divine view (go ttliche Betrachtung)

â€¢ ••Christ as God-Man••
	• A monstrous compound of divine and human nature
	• Contradicts both representation and understanding
	• Expresses the unity of divine and human nature

â€¢ ••Divine Life in Human Life••
	• The divine life is envisaged in the course of human life
	• Conditions necessary for this include Christ's teaching, self-references, and miracles
	• Miracles possess no independent corroborative power, but suggest divine power in Christ

â€¢ ••Faith and the Holy Spirit••
	• Faith is based on the witness of the Holy Spirit to individuals and the community of faith
	• Only faith can see that God is present in Christ
	• There are reasons for this envisagement, which does not run counter to the historical witness

â€¢ ••The God-Man••
	• Jesus is the God-man, a compound of divine and human nature
	• This compound expresses the recognition that human finitude and weakness are necessary to the unity of divine and human nature

â€¢ ••Divine Self-Emptying••
	• The divine self-emptying or divestment (Enta uerung) is intrinsic to the process of divinity
	• Allows the human contingency of Jesus to stand uncompromised
	• Enables faith to affirm that God is present in his life in such a way as to change all other human lives

â€¢ ••The Death of Christ••
	• Begins the reversal in perspective
	• Can be viewed from two perspectives: historically and from the perspective of faith

â€¢ ••Historical and Faith Perspectives••
	• Historical perspective: the death of Christ as a historical event
	• Faith perspective: the death of Christ as a revelation of the divine life

â€¢ ••Verification of Faith••
	• Sensory verification falls away, including miracles
	• The true miracle is the spirit itself
	• Genuine verification is through power over minds (Geister)

â€¢ ••The Community of Faith••
	• The community of faith is a witness to the power of the Holy Spirit over many centuries
	• This power manifests itself in the community's ability to affirm the divine presence in Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Christ's Death••
	• Consistent with Christ's life and teaching
	• Consequence of his religious radicalism and revolutionary attitude
	• Not unique, as many others have died for their faith

â€¢ ••The Historical Perspective••
	• Christ's death is seen as a shameful, dishonouring death
	• Executed as a common criminal, a torturous death on a cross
	• This death is converted into a badge of honour by Christians

â€¢ ••The Political Function of the Cross••
	• Corresponds to the gallows, a symbol of Roman imperial authority
	• By converting the cross into a badge of honour, Christians rejected Roman authority

â€¢ ••The Death of God••
	• Hegel sees Christ's death as the death of God
	• This is a moment of the divine idea, where finitude is assumed by God

â€¢ ••The Significance of Death••
	• Death is the highest pinnacle of finitude
	• The highest divestment of the divine idea is expressed as God's death

â€¢ ••The Monstrous Picture of God's Death••
	• A fearful picture that brings before the imagination the deepest abyss of cleavage
	• But also a picture of the highest love, where the divine and human are united

â€¢ ••The Unity of the Divine and Human••
	• Love entails a supreme surrender of the divine to the human
	• This unity is achieved in the death of Christ, where the divine and human are one

â€¢ ••The Reversal of Values••
	• The cross is converted from a symbol of dishonour to a badge of honour
	• This reversal is predicated on seeing the death of Christ as the death of God

â€¢ ••The Irony of the Cross••
