	• When Christianity acquired political authority, the cross was converted back into a symbol of power and oppression
	• This is seen in the Crusades, where the Church distorted the principle of Christian freedom

â€¢ ••The Significance of Christ's Death••
	• A moment of the divine idea, where finitude is assumed by God
	• A picture of the highest love, where the divine and human are united in death

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Death of God in Christ••
	• Represents the highest expression of divine love
	• Is the universal identity with other-being, death
	• Is the speculative intuition of love itself

â€¢ ••The Significance of Death••
	• Represents the extreme limit of finitude
	• Sublates natural finitude and immediate existence
	• Overcomes divestment and limitation

â€¢ ••The Nature of Satisfaction••
	• Is not a substitutionary atonement or transaction
	• Is not an extrinsic sacrifice, but rather an eternal accomplishment
	• Is made for us because it has occurred in and for itself

â€¢ ••The Role of Christ••
	• Is not a substitute for us, but rather the revealer of reconciliation
	• Is not the accomplisher of reconciliation, but rather the one who reveals it
	• Is the individual through whom God accomplishes reconciliation

â€¢ ••The Trinitarian Mediation••
	• Is the process by which God reconciles the world and himself
	• Involves the negation of immediacy and the positing of spirituality
	• Results in God's return to himself and the overcoming of negation

â€¢ ••The Meaning of Satisfaction••
	• Is intimately connected with the trinitarian mediation
	• Is the story of God, representing the absolute, self-contained movement of spirit

â€¢ ••The Nature of God••
	• Is spirit, because he is not merely Father, but also Son
	• Becomes the other and sublates this other
	• Is reconciled with himself eternally through death

â€¢ ••The Significance of Christ's Resurrection••
	• Is the moment of negation being overcome
	• Is the negation of negation, a moment of the divine nature
	• Results in God's return to himself and the positing of spirituality

â€¢ ••The Importance of Subjectivity••
	• We must take reconciliation into our own subjectivity
	• We must conform our lives to the eternal history of God
	• We must assume responsibility for our own redemption

â€¢ ••The Nature of Reconciliation••
	• Is not a capricious accident, but rather a true and consummating event
	• Is not the history of a single individual, but rather the history of God
	• Is the eternal accomplishment of reconciliation within the godhead

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Death of Christ••
	• Represents a reversal and transition into a new mode of existence
	• A transition from sensible to spiritual presence
	• Not just a moral teaching or human subjectivity, but an infinite relationship to God
• ••Sacrifice and Satisfaction••
	• Not a substitutionary sacrifice, but a relationship to God
	• Satisfaction is found in the awareness that God is present and is love
	• The death of Christ is the means of salvation and reconciliation
• ••The Resurrection and Ascension••
	• Begins at the point where the history of Christ receives a spiritual interpretation
	• Christ's death assumes the character of a death that constitutes the transition to glory
	• A glorification that is only a restoration of the original glory
• ••God's Involvement in Death••
	• God himself is dead, according to a Lutheran hymn
	• Finitude, negativity, and otherness are moments of the divine
	• Death is the furthest extreme to which humanity is exposed, and God is involved in this
• ••The Triune God••
	• The reconciliation in Christ makes no sense if God is not known as the triune God
	• The triune God distinguishes otherness from God, yet sublates this otherness and returns to Godself as love and the Spirit
• ••The Death of God••
	• Not a single act, but the eternal divine history
	• A moment in the nature of God himself
	• Has taken place in God himself
• ••The Connection between Crucifixion and Resurrection••
	• The process does not come to a halt at the death of God
	• A reversal takes place: God maintains himself in this process
	• The resurrection is something that belongs just as essentially to faith as the crucifixion
• ••The Trinitarian Process••
	• The abstractness of the Father is given up in the Son (death)
	• The negation of this negation is the unity of Father and Son (love, or the Spirit)
• ••The Reality of Death••
	• Death is as real for God as it is for humanity
	• Not just a seeming death, but a real one
• ••The Significance of Christ's Death••
	• A moment in the nature of God himself
	• A moment of reconciliation and salvation
	• A moment of the transition to glory

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's relationship with death••
	• God overpowers death, not the other way around
	• Death is taken into the divine life as a negated moment
	• The death of God signifies the death of death

â€¢ ••The concept of God••
	• God is not just the Father, but also the Son and Spirit
	• The Son is a concrete, world-embracing, suffering, dying God
	• The Spirit is the final and most adequate name of God

â€¢ ••The resurrection of Christ••
	• The resurrection is essential to Christian faith, but not a material event
	• It's more like an interpretative perspective on the crucifixion and life of Christ
	• It's based on the spiritual experience of the community, not historical evidence

â€¢ ••The nature of the resurrection••
	• It's not a literal bodily resurrection, but a transition to spiritual presence
	• Christ's sensible, bodily presence is left behind
	• The resurrection is an exaltation (Erhebung) of Christ

â€¢ ••The images of resurrection••
	• Resurrection is connected to images of raising up, ascent, return, and consummation
	• It's an elevation (Erhebung) to God
	• It's an envisagement or intuition, not a description or report

â€¢ ••The consummation of the divine life••
	• The resurrection and ascension are modes of the consummation and return of the divine life
	• It's a going forth of the divine idea into the uttermost cleavage
	• It's the absolute reconciliation, containing the negation of the negative within itself

â€¢ ••The transition in focus••
	• Resurrection designates a transition in focus from a single human being to the community of the Spirit
	• It's not about the survival and prolongation of individual streams of consciousness
	• It's about the consummation of all things in the life of God

â€¢ ••The 1824 lectures••
	• Virtually no reference to the resurrection, except that it means God's coming back to Godself as Spirit

â€¢ ••The 1827 lectures••
	• The history of the resurrection and ascension of Christ begins at the point where this history receives a spiritual interpretation
	• This history refers to the community's spiritual experience and understanding of the event

â€¢ ••The question of faith••
	• Whether God as Spirit has the power to prevail over death is a question that confronts faith in all ages
	• This question is particularly relevant in the age of postmodernity, where death is widely believed to be the prevailing power

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Story of Christ••
	• The story of Christ is a communal, not individual, event
	• The resurrection of Christ is coterminous with the ongoing faith and life of the spiritual community
	• The church rejects historical investigations and proofs of the appearance of Christ after his death

â€¢ ••Spiritual Interpretation••
	• A spiritual interpretation of the history of Christ before his death prevails in the community of faith
	• The Gospels were written only after the outpouring of the Spirit
	• The resurrection is an occurrence only for faith, not objective history

â€¢ ••Hegel's Approach••
	• Hegel does not push the demythologizing interpretation of resurrection too hard
	• He turns directly to the theme of spiritual community
	• Hegel's awareness of opposition to his philosophical theology is reflected in his introductions to the philosophy of religion lectures

â€¢ ••The Holy Spirit and Community••
	• Hegel's treatment of the Holy Spirit and community is structurally similar in all of the lectures
	• Three topics are addressed: origin, existence, and realization of the community

â€¢ ••Origin of the Community••
	• The origin of the community is the transition from the sensible to the spiritual presence of Christ
	• This transition involves the unique intersubjectivity of the spiritual community

â€¢ ••Affirmation of Sensible Presence••
	• Hegel affirms the moment of sensible presence in Christianity
	• The sensible presence of divinity is a distinctive feature of Christianity as the religion of the Spirit
	• The poet and speculative intuition honor the sensible shape as having spirit within it

â€¢ ••Transition to Spiritual Presence••
	• The divine idea occurs for the Christian religion as a present, immediate individual
	• This individuality is removed from the senses and raised to the right hand of God
	• Humanity is directed inward, and worldly grandeur disappears

â€¢ ••Retreat to Inwardness••
	• The Christian retreat is not a Stoic retreat, but a retreat that comes from infinite anguish
	• The Christian retreat endlessly divests itself of its particularity and self-possession
	• The infinite value of the Christian is contained in infinite anguish and comes from it

â€¢ ••Existence of the Community••
	• The existence of the community takes the form of institutions, teachings, and sacraments
	• The community is a worldly actuality that passes over into faith

â€¢ ••Realization of the Community••
	• The realization of the community involves the process by which faith passes over into worldly actuality
	• The question arises as to whether the community itself passes away

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Transition from Sensible to Spiritual Presence••
	• Occurs after Christ's withdrawal and sensible presence ceases
	• Involves two simultaneous passages: from individual to community, and from externality to inwardness

â€¢ ••Characteristics of the Transition••
	• The sensible element is preserved by letting it pass away
	• It cannot be held on to as a relic or holy image
	• Must be allowed to pass into new forms, remembered, and re-enacted

â€¢ ••The Role of the Holy Spirit••
	• The Holy Spirit can only outpour after Christ's withdrawal
	• The Spirit is a Comforter, turning from externality to the inner realm

â€¢ ••The Turn to the Spirit••
	• A turn to inwardness and subjectivity
	• A turn from truth as externally given to truth as subjectivity
	• Truth is in the subject, filled with the Spirit

â€¢ ••The Community and Faith••
	• The spiritual community arises with the rise of faith
	• Knowledge takes the form of faith in the third realm
	• The community is the cultus, faith, and the soil of spirit

â€¢ ••The Nature of Spirit••
	• The subject itself becomes spirit by traversing the process
	• The process is the divine self-differentiation and self-return
	• The individual subject is caught up in the process, spiritualized

â€¢ ••The Role of the Community in Faith••
	• The community has an objectivity over against the individual
	• The immediate knowledge of faith is mediated by the community's forms and practices

â€¢ ••The Witness of the Spirit••
	• Faith derives from the witness of the Spirit, not proofs based on miracles
	• Christ himself enjoins against seeking signs, proclaiming the kingdom of God is within

â€¢ ••Miracles and the Power of Spirit••
	• Miracles are effects produced by the power of spirit on the natural nexus
	• Spirit is a higher or absolute intervention in the natural process and laws of nature

â€¢ ••The Kingdom of God••
	• The kingdom of God is within the subject, not a return to externality
	• It is a turning to the Spirit and the community, not a literal second coming

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Faith and Spirit••
	• Faith is based on the inner witness of the Spirit of truth
	• Genuine faith rests on the truth of the idea, not external verification
	• The Spirit of truth is poured out upon the departure of Christ (John 16:7, 13)

â€¢ ••Verification of Faith••
	• External verification is contingent and based on superstition
	• Faith is verified through the inner witness of the Spirit of truth
	• The concept of faith is its own verification, not dependent on external evidence

â€¢ ••Sensible History••
	• Sensible history exists only as a past history sublated to the right hand of God
	• Sensible history is past for representation, not present
	• Faith begins with sensible, empirical history, but moves beyond it

â€¢ ••Historical Method••
	• The historical, juridical method is used to verify external facts
	• This method is appropriate for details of the external history of Christ
	• However, faith in Christ's divinity is not verified through this method

â€¢ ••Certainty of Faith••
	• Verification of the sensible is always subject to objections and qualifications
	• True certainty is achieved through the inner witness of the Spirit of truth
	• Faith is not based on empirical evidence, but on the concept of faith itself

â€¢ ••Spiritual Community••
	• The faith of the spiritual community stands on a different soil than sensible history
	• The church has condemned attacks on miracles and the resurrection
	• These events are not what establish Christ's divinity, but rather faith in Christ's divinity is what establishes them

â€¢ ••Philosophy and Faith••
	• Only philosophy can justify the content of faith
	• History is not the basis of faith, but rather spirit is concerned with what is in and for itself
	• The community is the origin of spirit, not something past

â€¢ ••Historicism••
	• Hegel is suspicious of historicism in the 1824 lectures
	• Spirit is not historical in the sense of being something past
	• Spirit is concerned with what is present, not something past

â€¢ ••Spirit and Nature••
	• The influence of spirit on nature is not a proof of faith
	• Such phenomena may occur, but they do not constitute a proof of faith
	• Faith is not based on direct power over nature, but on the inner witness of the Spirit of truth

â€¢ ••Resurrection and Spirit••
	• The resurrection of Jesus entails an annulment of his sensible presence
	• However, his real presence is preserved and transfigured into the modality of spirit
	• The resurrection is not a historical event, but rather a spiritual one.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's Historical Nature••
	• God is geschichtlich, meaning He is involved in a process of self-distinguishing and self-reintegrating.
	• This process is not subject to empirical investigation, but rather is an eternal movement that God Himself is.

â€¢ ••The Witness of Faith••
	• The only witness to spirit is the spirit itself.
	• Faith arises from the divine self-attestation, and is the human mode of participating in this attestation.

â€¢ ••The Community of Faith••
	• The community of faith is a communal or intersubjective reality.
	• It arises from the divine self-attestation and is characterized by infinite love and absolute freedom.

â€¢ ••The Distinction between Geschichte and Historie••
	• Hegel distinguishes between two senses of history: the intrinsically historical (geschichtlich) and the now-past historical (historisch).
	• The intrinsically historical refers to the eternal movement of God, while the now-past historical refers to events in which this process appears.

â€¢ ••The Kingdom of God••
	• The kingdom of God is the realm in which the idea of reconciliation is revealed and actualized.
	• It is the community of the Spirit, in which God is redemptively present.

â€¢ ••The Community of the Spirit••
	• The community of the Spirit is characterized by infinite love and absolute freedom.
	• It is a unique and unsurpassable intersubjectivity, distinguished from all other forms of human love and friendship.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Human Subjectivity••
	• Human subjectivity is renewed and transfigured by the indwelling of the Spirit of God.
	• It becomes a communal subjectivity, giving up its old independence and exclusivity.

â€¢ ••The Kingdom of the Spirit as Infinite Love••
	• The kingdom of the Spirit is the sphere of infinite love.
	• It is the place where the individual subject knows itself to have infinite worth and absolute freedom.

â€¢ ••The Role of Love in the Kingdom of the Spirit••
	• Love equalizes all things, even absolute opposition.
	• It is the source of infinite value and is contained in infinite anguish.

â€¢ ••The Mediation of Value••
	• Value is found in absolute mediation, not in immediacy.
	• The human subject is infinite only through this mediation, not immediately.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Kingdom of Actualized Freedom••
	• Hegel describes the world of spirit as a second nature
	• The kingdom of the Spirit is a kingdom of freedom, and vice versa

â€¢ ••Equality Before God••
	• All human beings are equal before God
	• This equality is the root of truly universal justice and the actualization of freedom

â€¢ ••Speculative Love••
	• Love that arises from infinite anguish is speculative
	• This love is not human (sexual) love or noble friendship, but a third, substantial bond

â€¢ ••The Distinction between Eros, Philia, and Agape••
	• Hegel struggles to express the distinction between these three types of love
	• Eros and philia are fulfilled in agape, which is love in the fullest sense

â€¢ ••Compassion and Suffering••
	• Love in the fullest sense is compassion, suffering with and on behalf of another
	• This love is grounded in the divine compassion

â€¢ ••The Unity of the Spirit••
	• The unity formed by this love is not a sensible, worldly linkage
	• It is a unity simply in the Spirit

â€¢ ••The Holy Spirit••
	• The Holy Spirit is in individuals who comprise the community of faith
	• They are the universal Christian church, the communion of saints

â€¢ ••The Subjectivity of the Spirit••
	• The Spirit is infinite subjectivity, not represented but actual divinity
	• It is the presence of God, not the substantial in-itself of the Father or the Son

â€¢ ••The Community as the Existing Spirit••
	• The community itself is the existing Spirit, the Spirit in its existence
	• God existing as community is the infinitesubjectivity of God as spirit

â€¢ ••The Actualization of Reconciliation••
	• The community of the Spirit is ontologically definitive of divinehuman (and interhuman) unity
	• Actualization requires subjective appropriation, and the community is the existing Spirit.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Christian Religion as the Religion of Spirit••
	• The Christian religion is the unification of the infinite antithesis of God and the world.
	• It is the genuine speculative enjoyment of the nature of God or spirit.
	• This is its content and vision, and it is accessible to the ordinary, uneducated consciousness.

â€¢ ••Sin Against the Holy Spirit••
	• Sin against the Holy Spirit is the only unforgivable sin.
	• It is sin against the consuming process of the infinite anguish of love.
	• Without being consumed by love, we are lost and have no share in eternal life.

â€¢ ••Catholicism and the Representation of the Son••
	• Hegel thinks that Catholicism may advance no further than the representation of the Son and those about him.
	• This is because Catholicism prolongs the physical, sensible presence of God.
	• The second moment has not yet been spiritualized, and the Spirit does not become an object in its own right.

â€¢ ••The Necessity of the Second Moment••
	• The second moment of objectivity and otherness must be sublated in love.
	• This is necessary for the unification of the abstractness of the Father and the objectivity of the Son.
	• The third moment unifies the abstractness of the Father and the objectivity of the Son in the community of the Spirit.

â€¢ ••The Community of the Spirit••
	• The community of the Spirit is not spiritual in an otherworldly sense.
	• It unifies God and the world.
	• It assumes institutional forms, which are inadequate to the divine content.

â€¢ ••The Invisible and Visible Church••
	• The invisible church, the community of the Spirit, is an abstraction without the visible.
	• The visible church without the invisible is a mere mechanism.
	• The community of the Spirit realizes itself in the real community, which is the church.

â€¢ ••The Church and Baptism••
	• The community of the Spirit realizes itself in the church.
	• The church is the institution whereby its subjects come to the truth and appropriate the truth to themselves.
	• The Holy Spirit becomes real, actual, and present within them and has its abode in them.

â€¢ ••The Role of Institutional Forms••
	• Institutional forms follow from the twofold task of coming to the truth and appropriating the truth.
	• The role of baptism, faith, doctrine, repentance, worship, and the sacraments serve the truth.
	• The Holy Spirit has its abode and activity in ecclesial practices that serve the truth.

â€¢ ••Omission of Governance and Ministry••
	• Hegel makes no mention of governance, ordination, ministry, preaching, etc.
	• Perhaps this is because these are not essential to the community of the Spirit.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Innocence in Regard to the Spirit••
	• Innocence in regard to the Spirit is not an acceptable excuse.
	• The community of the Spirit assumes institutional forms, which are inadequate to the divine content.
	• The ideality of infinite love and transfigured intersubjectivity must become actual.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Institutional Forms••
	• Institutional forms are not directly related to the process of coming to and appropriating the truth
	• They are considered instrumental to this process

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of Protestant Clergy••
	• Hegel has a low opinion of Protestant clergy and their vanity
	• He criticizes them for abandoning their teaching responsibility

â€¢ ••The Church as the Kingdom of God••
	• The church is the achieved presence, life, preservation, and enjoyment of the Spirit
	• It is not the kingdom of God in the sense of an eschatological ideal

â€¢ ••Renunciation and the Church••
	• The church is not directly responsible for worldly goods and values
	• It should provide ideals, goals, and frames of reference for the formation of the body politic

â€¢ ••The Role of the Church in the Formation of the Body Politic••
	• The church provides ideals, goals, and frames of reference
	• It has an essential role to play in the formation of the body politic

â€¢ ••The Sacrament of Baptism••
	• Baptism signifies that the individual is already born within the community of the church
	• The community has the responsibility of nurturing children in their spiritual growth

â€¢ ••Faith and Truth••
	• The community has faith, which is a form of certainty
	• Faith is a certainty of absolute truth, of what God is

â€¢ ••The Nature of Faith••
	• Faith rests on reason itself, on the Spirit
	• It is not based on external authority or past occurrences

â€¢ ••The Spirit and Feeling••
	• The Spirit is higher than feeling
	• It conquers and purifies feeling, and is liberated from feeling

â€¢ ••The Truth Mediated by Institutional Forms••
	• The community begins with the fact that the truth is at hand
	• The truth is known, extant truth, and is what God is: the triune God, life, and the process of himself with himself.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Subject and Faith••
	• The subject is related to the faith through the certainty of reconciliation
	• Faith is the certainty that individuals are meant to be children of God and citizens of God's kingdom
	• The difficulty of individual limitations and errors is removed by knowing God looks into the heart

â€¢ ••The Community of the Spirit••
	• The essentialization of the community begins when individuals surrender their individual subjectivity
	• The ecclesial community comes into being with the purpose of mediating God's truth
	• The community employs various means to make God's truth available, including doctrine and teaching

â€¢ ••Doctrine and Teaching••
	• Doctrine and teaching serve to awaken feelings that proceed from the representation of truth
	• Doctrine is deposited in creedal symbols and bound to fixed expressions to protect it from arbitrariness and contingency
	• Tradition is something given, not created from itself, and is cultivated by the Spirit present within the community

â€¢ ••The Role of Tradition••
	• Tradition is the Spirit of the community as a whole that is creative
	• The doctrine of the church is not produced by the church but is cultivated by the Spirit present within it
	• The community is the infinite power and authority by which its own development and teaching are determined

â€¢ ••The Emerging and Subsisting Community••
	• The emerging community is where the Holy Spirit is poured, and truth is present as intuition, faith, and feeling
	• The subsisting community is where the truth is established and extant as doctrine, the doctrine of faith
	• The content of this doctrine is reconciliation, and it is elaborated as something presupposed and settled

â€¢ ••The Development of Doctrine••
	• Feeling precedes rather than proceeds from doctrine in the emerging community
	• Feeling is the fertile matrix of interiority from which doctrine develops, though intermixed with impurities
	• Doctrine is liberated from impurities through an ongoing process of thought

â€¢ ••The Church as a Teaching Church••
	• The church is essentially a teaching church with a teaching office
	• Individuals are born into this teaching and partake of it at first implicitly, as children
	• The truth is external to them, comes to them as presupposed, and has authority

â€¢ ••The Role of Authority••
	• All truth initially comes in the form of authority and thus must be taught and learned
	• The inner truth is known not as an object of sense experience but only as something that is taught

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Community••
	• The purpose of the community is to mediate God's truth and make it available in concrete practices
	• The community employs various means to achieve this purpose, including doctrine and teaching, repentance and penance, and the cultus and sacraments

â€¢ ••Hegel's Ecclesiology••
	• Hegel's ecclesiology displays a rather more Catholic than Protestant sensibility
	• Hegel portrays Catholicism as overly juridical, but his own views on the community and tradition are more in line with Catholic thought

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Spirit is already the absolute possibility of assimilating knowledge••
	• Recognizes the teaching as the truth in which one abides
	• Concern of education is with the process of inwardly assimilating outwardly mediated truth

â€¢ ••Children born into the church are born in and to freedom••
	• Do not have to be converted from evil
	• Evil has already been overcome in principle

â€¢ ••The task of cultivation is to habituate the learner to the good and the true••
	• Discourages evil from re-emerging
	• Focuses on inwardly assimilating truth and renouncing evil

â€¢ ••Spirit has the power to undo evil••
	• Evil is not eliminated, but its doing is reversed
	• God prevails in the struggle with evil by taking it upon and into godself

â€¢ ••Repentance and penitence signify a process of renouncing transgressions and rising to the truth••
	• Acknowledging truth over evil and willing the good
	• Nullifies evil through the power of the Spirit

â€¢ ••The undoing of evil cannot take place in a sensible, external manner••
	• Only takes place in a spiritual manner or inwardly
	• Evil deeds remain, but their damage can be reduced and reversed

â€¢ ••The church promotes the process of undoing evil through habituating and educating the spirit••
	• Focuses on inwardly assimilating truth and renouncing evil
	• Concerned with promoting the process of rebirth and nullifying evil

â€¢ ••Faith is the divine Spirit that works in the subject••
	• The subject is not a passive receptacle
	• The Holy Spirit is equally the subject's spirit to the extent that the subject has faith

â€¢ ••Human beings must be born twice: naturally and spiritually••
	• Like the Brahmans, human beings must be reborn
	• Spirit is not immediate, but only engenders itself from itself

â€¢ ••Rebirth is a human action, but only in so far as the subject gives up its singularized, private being-for-self••
	• The divine Spirit effects rebirth
	• Rebirth is also a free gift of God, for everything divine is free

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Cultus and Sacraments••
	• The cultus and sacraments are the final and most important forms of the church.
	• Hegel emphasizes the place of cultus as the culminating moment of the concept of religion.

â€¢ ••Christian Community of Faith••
	• The Christian community of faith is cultus, an activity of worship and liturgy.
	• The life, passion, and resurrection of Christ are eternally repeated in the members of the church.

â€¢ ••Sacraments••
	• The sacraments attest to the inner certainty of the truth and the implicit unity of divine and human nature.
	• The sacrament of the eucharist or holy communion is the primary sacrament.

â€¢ ••Eucharist••
	• Believers are received into the mystical union of the kingdom through partaking of the body and blood of Christ.
	• Partaking or communion symbolizes the fulfilment of the whole process of reconciliation.

â€¢ ••Immediate Sensuous Aspect••
	• The actual partaking of food and drink is important in the eucharist.
	• Eating and drinking are a way of appropriating something consciously and individually.

â€¢ ••Transubstantiation••
	• The sensible as such must be validated and transformed into the divine substance itself.
	• The two become one in the eucharist.

â€¢ ••Ritual Action••
	• The ritual action is essential for immediate certainty in the eucharist.
	• The Christian eucharist is connected with primitive rituals of sacrifice.

â€¢ ••Mystical Union••
	• The union accomplished through the partaking of the eucharist is a mystical union, not a physical union.
	• Christ is eternally sacrificed and rises again in the heart.

â€¢ ••Education Analogy••
	• Hegel applies the analogy of eating to education as well as to the eucharist.
	• In both cases, the ingestion of something external brings about an internal change.

â€¢ ••Sacraments and Church••
	• Baptism is the only sacrament besides the eucharist that Hegel discusses.
	• Confession is also a sacrament for Catholics, but Hegel does not discuss it.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Process of Self-Divestment••
	• Individuals make themselves their own (Sichzueigenmachen) through the eternal sacrifice
	• This process involves a reconciled relationship with themselves and God
	• The resurrection of Christ takes place within individuals
• ••The Eucharist and Self-Giving••
	• The eucharist extends the self-divestment of God in Christ into the life of the community
	• The community is destined to give itself up for the sake of reconciliation
• ••Confessional Differences••
	• Hegel emphasizes the differences between Christian confessions in their view of the sacrament
	• He believes these differences will pass away as the Christian idea is realized
• ••Catholic View of the Eucharist••
	• Catholics venerate the host as a physical presence of Christ
	• Christ is believed to be present in the host in a sensible, bodily fashion
• ••Lutheran View of the Eucharist••
	• Lutherans believe the host is consecrated only in the faith of each individual
	• Transubstantiation occurs only in the partaking of communion, in a spiritual fashion
• ••Reformed View of the Eucharist••
	• The Reformed view lacks the mystical element of the Lutheran view
	• The communion becomes a memorial, an ordinary psychological relationship
• ••Hegel's Account of Confessional Differences••
	• Hegel believes Lutheranism holds the mean between Catholic supernaturalism and Reformed rationalism
	• His account is open to suspicion of prejudice due to his Lutheran background
• ••Hegel's Eclecticism••
	• Hegel's philosophical theology is transconfessional and eclectic
	• He appropriates both orthodox and heterodox doctrinal traditions
• ••Hegel's Account of Historical Details••
	• Hegel's account of historical details is often imprecise
	• His emphasis lies on conceptual distinctions that appear in history
• ••The Passing Away of the Community••
	• The 1821 manuscript discusses the passing away of the community
	• This section is replaced in the 1824 manuscript

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Shift from "passing away" to "realization"••
	• Hegel's analysis moves from the category of "passing away" (Vergehen) to "realization" (Realisierung)
	• This shift reflects a change in emphasis, but may also indicate an altered interpretation on Hegel's part

â€¢ ••Community's divestment and transition••
	• The community gives up its inward spirituality for the sake of the redemption of the world
	• This transition prolongs the eucharistic motif at the heart of its cultic practice

â€¢ ••Three forms of objective reality••
	• The community of faith realizes itself in three forms: immediate world, abstract thought, and the concept
	• These forms are distinguished in the 1824 lectures, but not in the 1827 lectures

â€¢ ••Transformation of the community's spirituality••
	• The community's spirituality takes on diverse forms of worldly actuality in the 1827 lectures
	• These forms are divided into three stages: real, ideal, and ideal-real

â€¢ ••Three stages of reconciliation••
	• Reconciliation occurs in three stages: heart, church, and ethical life
	• Each stage represents a different level of reconciliation between inner spirituality and worldly actuality

â€¢ ••First stage: Heart••
	• Reconciliation occurs in the realm of immediacy or the heart
	• This reconciliation is abstract and has the world as such over against it

â€¢ ••Second stage: Church••
	• Religiosity and worldliness remain external to each other in the church
	• The church prevails over the unreconciled world, but takes worldliness into itself

â€¢ ••Third stage: Ethical Life••
	• The community's spirituality is transformed into a worldly actuality in the form of ethical life
	• This stage represents a higher level of reconciliation between inner spirituality and worldly actuality

â€¢ ••Monasticism as a transitional phase••
	• The community initially resists the realization of reconciliation in the worldly realm
	• Monasticism prevails as the ecclesial ideal during this transitional phase

â€¢ ••Spirit's development and worldliness••
	• The nature of spirit is to develop itself and differentiate itself into worldliness
	• This development is necessary for the realization of reconciliation in the worldly realm

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Church as Empire••
	• The church becomes a worldly empire, taking on imperial attributes
	• It rules over its subjects with papal absolutism, suppressing human freedom
	• This leads to a condition of servitude, opposite of genuine reconciliation

â€¢ ••The Ethical Realm••
	• The principle of freedom penetrates into the world, becoming concrete and rational
	• Ethical life is the most genuine cultus, extending the cultic practice of reconciliation into the world
	• Institutions of ethical life (law, family, civil society, state) are divine, serving reconciliation and human freedom

â€¢ ••The Modern Protestant World••
	• The modern Protestant secular-bourgeois world succeeds the church as the realm of the kingdom of the Spirit
	• This world is meant to actualize the kingdom of the Spirit, but Hegel has severe questions about its condition

â€¢ ••The Role of the State••
	• The state is the organization where the divine has broken through into actuality
	• The foundation of the worldly realm is the divine will, law, and freedom
	• The state properly governed disciplines worldliness, making it serve divine purposes

â€¢ ••The Alternative to the State••
	• The alternative to the state is anarchy and barbarism
	• The state is necessary to maintain order and discipline in the world

â€¢ ••Hegel's Interest in the State••
	• Recent political events in France and Great Britain (1830-1831) rekindled Hegel's interest in the state and social philosophy
	• He added a section on the relationship of religion to the state to his 1831 lectures

â€¢ ••The Relationship of Religion to the State••
	• The state is the genuine mode of actuality by which the idea of reconciliation is realized in the world
	• Religion is the knowledge of the highest truth, while the state is the actualization of freedom in the world

â€¢ ••The Concept of Freedom••
	• There can be only one concept of freedom that manifests itself in both religion and the state
	• This concept of freedom is the foundation of the state and the telos of religion

â€¢ ••Representing the Relationship of Religion and State••
	• The claim that laws, governmental authority, and political constitutions derive from God is advanced in Protestant states
	• However, this claim needs to be justified, and abuses resulting from the conjunction of religious and political power need to be avoided

â€¢ ••Avoiding Abuses••
	• Hegel questions how abuses such as child labor, low wages, and restriction of the franchise can be avoided in the conjunction of religious and political power
	• He likely sees the need for a more nuanced understanding of the relationship between religion and the state.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Lectures and Writings••
	• Hegel's Heidelberg lectures (1817-1818) were translated as "Lectures on Natural Right and Political Science"
	• His Berlin lectures (1819-1820) were published in two editions
	• His own textbook, "Elements of the Philosophy of Right", was published in 1820 and translated in 1991
	• His essay on "The English Reform Bill" (1831) was his last published writing before his death

â€¢ ••Religion and State••
	• In Catholic states, religion and state can be sundered, with different laws and values
	• This can lead to a realm of holiness and eternity being set up in opposition to right and temporality
	• The separation of church and state can lead to theocratic abuses or secularism

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Religion and State••
	• Hegel does not believe that the church should be superseded by the modern secular world
	• He also does not believe that the state is the principal telos of religion
	• His view is that both religion and state are essential, and neither can be dissolved into or superseded by the other

â€¢ ••The Goal of Reconciliation••
	• The goal is the reconciliation of the world, which requires a state and other ethical institutions as well as religion and a church
	• Religion alone is not enough, and a purely secular state has no orientation other than to human ends

â€¢ ••The Problem of Institutionalizing the Religion-State Relationship••
	• Neither a theocratic nor a secularist state will do
	• Hegel does not seem to offer an alternative

â€¢ ••The Ideal Stage of Reconciliation••
	• The realization of reconciliation in the church and in the institutions of ethical life appears to be ambiguous and incomplete
	• A reaction sets in, with the freedom of reason turning against merely spiritless externality and servitude

â€¢ ••The Enlightenment••
	• The Enlightenment is a modern cultural movement that represents reflective thinking and subjectivity
	• It takes on the form of an abstract universality that is directed not merely against externality, but also against the concepts of reconciliation and liberation

â€¢ ••The Critique of Modern Secularism••
	• Hegel's critique of modern secularism precludes the idea that the church should be superseded by the modern secular world
	• He believes that religion cannot be made subservient to ends other than the knowledge and worship of God

â€¢ ••The Importance of Religion••
	• Religion is the necessary foundation of the state
	• It provides an orientation towards the absolute and the infinite

â€¢ ••The Ambiguity of the Religion-State Relationship••
	• The question of the right relationship between religion and state remains unresolved in the modern world
	• Hegel's view is that both religion and state are essential, and neither can be dissolved into or superseded by the other

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of Enlightenment Rationalism••
	• Hegel argues that Enlightenment rationalism attacks the doctrine of the Trinity
	• This leads to an abstract and empty self-identity, with no specific knowledge of God
	• Hegel sees this as a step towards atheism

â€¢ ••Consequences of Enlightenment Rationalism••
	• The autonomous subject succumbs to a new servitude
	• Hegel may be thinking of the Reign of Terror during the French Revolution

â€¢ ••Islamic Religion as a Form of Abstract Identity••
	• In Islamic religion, subjectivity has no rights of its own
	• Its vocation is to submerge itself in the unity of God, the pure infinite
	• This leads to a fanatical hatred of everything concrete

â€¢ ••Hegel's Introduction of Islam••
	• Islam is introduced as a contemporary rival to Christianity
	• It lacks a place in Hegel's schema of determinate religions
	• Hegel's discussion of Islam seems improvised and lacks a full assessment

â€¢ ••Pietism as a Form of Abstract Idealism••
	• In the 1827 lectures, Pietism replaces Islam as the contrasting form of abstract idealism
	• Pietism represents an extreme form of subjectivity and freedom
	• It renounces objective truth and moves within itself, knowing only its own definitions

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Pietism••
	• Pietism acknowledges no objective truth and opposes itself to dogmas and the content of religion
	• It retains a connection with Christ, but only in the realm of feeling
	• It results in a subjectivity devoid of content

â€¢ ••Hypocrisy and Vanity in Pietism••
	• Pietism can assume the form of hypocrisy and extreme vanity
	• It can also result in peaceful, noble, and pious aspirations

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of Pietism••
	• Hegel sees Pietism as a form of abstract idealism that lacks content
	• It is a subjectivity that is devoid of objective truth and dogma

â€¢ ••Comparison between Enlightenment Rationalism and Pietism••
	• Both forms of abstract idealism lack content and objective truth
	• Both result in a subjectivity that is devoid of meaning and purpose

â€¢ ••Implications of Hegel's Critique••
	• Hegel's critique of Enlightenment rationalism and Pietism highlights the dangers of abstract idealism
	• It suggests that a more nuanced and balanced approach to religion and truth is necessary.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Philosophy and Religion••
	• Hegel's philosophy was increasingly attacked by Neopietists in 1827
	• They targeted his Trinitarianism and accused him of pantheism

â€¢ ••Enlightenment and Religion••
	• Enlightenment reflection invaded the sphere of religion
	• Thinking assumed a hostile attitude toward religion and its concrete content

â€¢ ••Philosophy's Role••
	• Philosophy advances beyond both the church and the Enlightenment
	• It mediates between them and recognizes the necessity of representation

â€¢ ••Philosophy's Goal••
	• The goal of philosophy is the cognition of the truth
	• The truth is the absolute truth, which is God

â€¢ ••Philosophy and the Church••
	• Philosophy is opposed to the church because it conceptualizes
	• However, it recognizes the necessity of representation and preserves its truth-content

â€¢ ••Philosophy and the Enlightenment••
	• Philosophy is opposed to the Enlightenment because it does not share its indifference to content
	• It recognizes the truth of religion and Christianity

â€¢ ••Speculative Philosophy••
	• Speculative philosophy shares with reflective philosophy a recognition of the truth produced by subjective consciousness
	• It also recognizes the truth as not produced, but as subsisting in and for itself

â€¢ ••Reconciliation between Real and Ideal••
	• Philosophy mediates the real and the ideal
	• It knows the objective content of religion in accord with its necessity and reason

â€¢ ••Philosophy as Theology••
	• Philosophy presents the reconciliation of God with himself and with nature
	• It shows that nature is implicitly divine and that finite spirit arrives at reconciliation

â€¢ ••Philosophy's Task••
	• The task of philosophy is to show forth the rational content of religion
	• It is to reconcile reason with religion in its manifold forms

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's reconciliation of religion and philosophy••
	• Hegel sees a reconciliation between religion and philosophy as the "peace of God"
	• This reconciliation is based on reason, which is first known and thought through

â€¢ ••Questionable assumption••
	• Hegel's reconciliation is based on the assumption that religion must take refuge in philosophy
	• This assumption may be questionable, as philosophy may become marginalized in the postmodern world

â€¢ ••Religion should not take flight••
	• Instead of taking refuge in philosophy, religion should stand and resist
	• This would allow religion to criticize and rise above its own representational forms

â€¢ ••Theology should not be limited to philosophy••
	• A theology that draws on philosophical resources, but does not strictly be a philosophical theology
	• This theology would make use of the concept to interpret and reimagine its own primary symbols and practices

â€¢ ••Advantages of Christian theology••
	• Christian theology has an intrinsic advantage over philosophy due to its connection to concrete communities of faith and ethical practices
	• This connection gives Christian theology a deeper understanding of the world and its affairs

â€¢ ••Hegel's concern about the passing away of the community••
	• In his 1821 lectures, Hegel considers the possibility of the passing away of the community of the Spirit
	• This would be a discordant note, as the kingdom of God has been established eternally

â€¢ ••Signs of modern decadence••
	• Hegel enumerates signs of modern decadence, including the profanation of sacred things and the decline of a sense of common life based on religion
	• Rationality takes refuge in private rights and goods, rather than a common life

â€¢ ••Philosophy's isolation from the world••
	• The community of philosophy is in danger of becoming isolated from the affairs of the world
	• This isolation would make philosophy's claims hegemonic and unproductive

â€¢ ••The importance of concrete materiality••
	• The concrete materiality of Christ and the Eucharist is essential to theology
	• Philosophy trades in representations, and should not be seen as superior to the religious

â€¢ ••Hegel's tendency towards philosophical hegemony••
	• Hegel's tendency towards philosophical hegemony may explain why his theological voice was misunderstood or secularized
	• This tendency should be avoided in favor of a more nuanced understanding of the relationship between philosophy and theology.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Fragmented Text••
	• The text is fragmented and reflects Hegel's haste in completing his lecture manuscript.
	• The critical edition of the text provides a more refined and less radical version.

â€¢ ••The Church and Teaching of Christ••
	• The text in Matthew 16:18 refers to the church, not the teaching of Christ.
	• Hegel accepts this passage as a saying of Jesus and uses it as a prooftext.

â€¢ ••The Decline of the Church••
	• The church has disappeared, and the quest for private welfare and enjoyment has taken its place.
	• The knowledge of God has been lost, replaced by historical and technical information.
	• Moralistic views and subjective feelings have replaced true faith.

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Decline••
	• The clergy and theologians have abandoned their calling, leaving ordinary people with no resources.
	• The salt of the gospel has lost its savour, and the teaching of love has been relinquished.
	• The result is finitude turned in upon itself, arrogant barrenness, and lack of content.

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of the Modern World••
	• Hegel does not see the modern world progressing toward a philosophical nirvana or utopian state.
	• He acknowledges that the present day must be left to its own resources to solve its problems.

â€¢ ••The Role of Philosophy••
	• Philosophy forms an isolated order of priests who preserve and interpret truth.
	• Philosophy does not create or struggle for truth, but rather comes on the scene after the action is over.

â€¢ ••The Limitations of Philosophy••
	• Philosophy is not a calling for those who want to be part of the struggle.
	• Hegel's philosophy has had a tremendous impact on the struggles of the modern world, but he did not anticipate this.

â€¢ ••The Possibility of Revisioned Theology••
	• Hegel shuts off the possibility of a revisioned theology and prophetic church confronting challenges with different resources.
	• This is a remarkable conclusion, given the impact of his own philosophy on the modern world.

â€¢ ••The Three Estates of the Kingdom of the Spirit••
	• Hegel suggests that religion, church, and faith are locked into the first estate of the kingdom of the Spirit.
	• Theology is locked into the second estate, that of the understanding.
	• The community of philosophy alone occupies the third estate, that of reason.

â€¢ ••The Better Alternative••
	• The better alternative is for religious communities themselves to acknowledge the challenges and take action.
	• This is a more realistic and hopeful prospect than Hegel's suggestion that the community of philosophy will survive by passing over into the community of philosophy.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Pessimism about Theology••
	• Hegel was pessimistic about the ability of theology to respond to the challenges of modernity
	• He recognized the limits of rationality and the need for a prophetic and critical approach to theology

â€¢ ••The Challenges of Modernity••
	• The challenges of modernity are more daunting than Hegel imagined
	• There are no panaceas, religious or philosophical, for achieving reconciliation in history

â€¢ ••The Consummation of Religion••
	• The only consummation of religion is in God
	• This means that true reconciliation can only be achieved in the afterlife

â€¢ ••Part Two of the Lectures: Determinate Religion••
	• This part of the lectures is the largest and most complex
	• It covers a range of materials, including world religions and their histories

â€¢ ••Hegel's Interpretation of Christianity••
	• Christianity is understood to be the consummation of the concept and history of religions
	• However, this is not a simple or brief statement, and Hegel's treatment of the topic is extensive

â€¢ ••Hegel's Engagement with the History of Religions••
	• Hegel was fascinated by the topic of the history of religions
	• He undertook extensive reading in primary and secondary sources, but was unable to arrive at a satisfactory arrangement of the materials

â€¢ ••Hegel's Sources••
	• Hegel drew on approximately 240 works for his lectures on the philosophy of religion
	• Two-thirds of these works were used primarily or exclusively for Determinate Religion

â€¢ ••The Limitations of Hegel's Sources••
	• Many of Hegel's sources were based on travel and missionary reports
	• They left a great deal to be desired as far as a scientific study of religion is concerned

â€¢ ••Hegel's Focus on Classical Expressions of Religions••
	• Hegel focused on the original or classical expressions of religions
	• He did not attend to their subsequent histories or contemporary living expressions

â€¢ ••The Problem of Fossilized Religions••
	• Hegel's treatment of world religions leaves most of them behind
	• Christianity alone truly lives, surrounded by fossilized forms, and may eventually become a fossil itself

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Organization of Determinate Religion••
	• He experimented with different arrangements of Determinate Religion in four lecture series
	• Each arrangement was distinct, with the 1824 lectures on The Consummate Religion being the most coherent

â€¢ ••Hegel's Treatment of Specific Religions••
	• His discussions of specific religions were packed with insights and showed a high level of sophistication
	• He was seriously engaged with the material, but struggled to produce a unified history of religions

â€¢ ••Omission of Islam••
	• Hegel's omission of Islam is puzzling, given its population and his appreciation for some of its mystical writings
	• Possible reasons for the omission include limited knowledge, lack of fit with his schematism, and European antipathy

â€¢ ••Character of Hegel's Work••
	• His work on the history of religions has more the character of a geography or pluralism of religions
	• He struggled to produce a unified history, instead focusing on the diversity of religions

â€¢ ••Four Renditions of Determinate Religion••
	• Hegel presented four different renditions of Determinate Religion in his lectures
	• These renditions appeared in the 1821, 1824, 1827, and 1831 lectures

â€¢ ••Moments of the Concept: The 1821 Lectures••
	• In the 1821 lectures, Hegel described the concept of religion in its determinate aspects
	• He used a phenomenological and speculative approach to describe the stages of religious consciousness

â€¢ ••Phenomenological and Speculative Approach••
	• Hegel's approach was both phenomenological (attending to concrete stages of religious consciousness) and speculative (interpreting from the perspective of the absolute idea)
	• He used two sets of analytic categories to describe the religions: internal and external

â€¢ ••Internal and External Categories••
	• The internal set identified three aspects of a religion: its abstract concept, its determinate concept, and its absolute concept
	• The external set related the religions to each other, highlighting their differences and similarities

â€¢ ••Hegel's Experimentation with the History of Religions••
	• Hegel struggled to produce a unified history of religions, instead experimenting with different arrangements and approaches
	• His work on the history of religions was characterized by a high level of sophistication and engagement with the material

â€¢ ••Logic of Hegel's Philosophy of Religion••
	• Hegel's philosophy of religion points beyond inclusivism to a genuine religious pluralism
	• In this pluralism, Christianity takes its place as one of the determinate religions of the Concrete Spirit.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Concept of Religion••
	• A tripartite concept consisting of metaphysical concept, concrete representations, and cultus
	• Initially envisioned as a twofold scheme, later developed into a triple division

â€¢ ••External Analysis of Determinate Religion••
	• Arranges Determinate Religion into a triad corresponding to logic's fundamental moments: being, essence, and concept
	• Applies these categories in the mode of determinateness and finitude

â€¢ ••The Three Moments of Determinate Religion••
	• 1. Prereflective immediacy or undifferentiated substance (Oriental religions of nature)
	• 2. Differentiation in the form of particularity (Jewish religion) and necessity (Greek)
	• 3. External purposiveness or expediency (Roman religion)

â€¢ ••Roman Religion••
	• Transitional stage between finite and infinite religions
	• Universal, related to the whole human race, but finite and concerned with earthly circumstances
	• Lacking transcendence and depth, it is prosaic and preoccupied with earthly arrangements

â€¢ ••The Role of Roman Religion••
	• Prepares the transition from finite to infinite religions
	• Negates itself and prepares the way for genuine infinitude

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Concept••
	• Does not exist at the beginning, full-grown on its own account
	• Not immediate, but rather a product of history and becoming

â€¢ ••The Path to the Concept••
	• Considered in the second part of the philosophy of religion
	• Religions are stages of this path, but not the true religion

â€¢ ••The Concept of Religion as a Product of History••
	• Not an a priori idea, but rather a product of history
	• Attained through the stages of determinate religions

â€¢ ••The Leap to Ideality••
	• The concept seems to leap out of history into ideality when fully attained
	• Raises questions about the connection between ideality and reality, rationality and actuality, spirit and history

â€¢ ••Hegel's Approach to the History of Religions••
	• Aims to apprehend the concept of religion, rather than simply compiling a history of religions
	• Seeks to understand the path to the concept and the stages of determinate religions

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Approach to Religion••
	• Recognizes reason in religions, but also acknowledges their flaws
	• Sees human activity in brutal and oppressive practices, but condemns them for destroying human freedom
	• Considers human religiousness in general to be idolatrous

â€¢ ••Critique of Roman Religion••
	• Devotes extensive attention to Roman religion in his lectures
	• Critiques Roman religion as an example of idolatry and a hindrance to human freedom
	• Sees Roman religion as a form of finite purposiveness

â€¢ ••Three Moments of Determinate Religion••
	• Immediate, natural religion (God intuited in the natural unity of the spiritual and the natural)
	• Religions of spiritual individuality (God represented as an individual subject or subjects)
	• Religion of finite purposiveness (Roman religion)

â€¢ ••Revised Classification of Religions••
	• Converts threefold division to a twofold one: immediate or nature religion and religions of spiritual individuality
	• Incorporates Roman religion as a third sub-phase following Jewish and Greek religion
	• Places Christianity as the third moment in a larger dialectic of religions

â€¢ ••Correspondences between Religions••
	• Suggests that the religions of spiritual individuality correspond to three forms of nature religion in reverse order
	• Places Jewish religion corresponding to Persian, Greek religion to Hinduism, and Roman religion to the state religion of China

â€¢ ••Transition to Consummate Religion••
	• Sees the determinate or finite religions as cycling back upon themselves in death and degeneracy
	• Suggests a return to the primitive at a more advanced level of culture

â€¢ ••Judaism and Zoroastrianism••
	• Links Jewish and Persian (Zoroastrian) religion under the category of the good
	• Portrays Zoroastrianism as a religion of light, with Judaism as a pure form of monotheism

â€¢ ••Cosmic Dualism vs. Monotheism••
	• Distinguishes between Zoroastrianism's cosmic dualism and Judaism's pure monotheism

â€¢ ••Christianity's Place in the Dialectic••
	• Places Christianity as virtually another moment in the dialectical structure
	• Resists any monolithic, linear theory of progress

â€¢ ••Hegel's Methodology••
	• Uses a necessary classification that follows objectively from the nature of spirit
	• Is willing to alter his classification in the process of lecturing

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Concept of Determinate Religion••
	• Involves a shift from the Roman cult and pantheon to a more direct consideration of Christianity
	• Christianity is seen as the final phase of determinate religion, completing the movement from nature to finite spirit to infinite spirit

â€¢ ••Structural Experimentation••
	• Hegel experimented with different arrangements of determinate religion in his lectures
	• In 1827 and 1831, he returned to an internal threefold arrangement that excluded Christianity

â€¢ ••Content Enrichment••
	• The 1824 lectures are twice as long as the 1821 lectures
	• The first part, immediate or nature religion, is expanded to include detailed discussions of various religions, including magic, ancient China, Buddhism, Hinduism, Persian religion, and Egyptian religion

â€¢ ••Interpretation of Judaism••
	• Major changes occur in the interpretation of Judaism in the 1824 lectures
	• The exact nature of these changes is not specified in the text

â€¢ ••Philosophical Treatment of Religions••
	• Hegel's treatment of religions is a purely philosophical one, not an empirical history
	• Philosophy grasps the necessity of what unfolds in actuality, but it is not easy to grasp the actual historical course of unfolding

â€¢ ••Diversity of Determinate Forms••
	• The concept of religion appears in various determinate forms, leading to religious pluralism
	• This diversity is a logical deduction of the necessity of religious pluralism

â€¢ ••Typology and Geography••
	• Hegel's lectures offer a typology and geography of religions, rather than a history
	• The actual historical course of unfolding cannot be deduced logically, but is a matter of historical contingency

â€¢ ••Commonality among Religions••
	• What is common to the religions is that spirit is coming to itself in them
	• This commonality is a key aspect of Hegel's philosophical treatment of religions

â€¢ ••Christianity as the Third Form of Religion••
	• Christianity is seen as the third form of religion, following the religion of nature and the religion of finite spirit
	• This movement is one from consciousness to self-consciousness, finitely determined to self-consciousness self-contained

â€¢ ••Distinctive Structure of the 1824 Lectures••
	• The 1824 lectures incorporate Christianity into the horizon of determinate religion as its third form
	• This structure is confirmed by a brief comment at the end of the introduction to The Consummate Religion in 1824

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Hegel's Concept of Determinate Religion••
	• In 1827, Hegel reestablishes the threefold division of determinate religion
	• The categories are influenced by the dialectic of nature and spirit
• ••Three Stages of Determinate Religion••
	• ••Stage 1: Unity of Spiritual and Natural••
		- Religion as the unity of the spiritual and the natural
		- Examples: nature religion, Roman religion
	• ••Stage 2: Elevation of Spiritual Above Natural••
		- The spiritual is elevated above the natural in the form of thought (Jewish religion) or concrete individuality (Greek religion)
		- Examples: Jewish religion, Greek religion
	• ••Stage 3: Annihilation of Spirit••
		- The religion in which the spiritual is effectively annihilated
		- Example: Roman religion
• ••Nature Religion••
	• Not devoid of spirit, but worships it in natural and human shapes
	• Examples: magic, advanced nature religions
• ••Jewish and Greek Religions••
	• In 1827, Hegel reverses the order of Jewish and Greek religions from 1821 and 1824
	• Greek religion is now considered the pinnacle of the determinate religions
	• Judaism is still lacking in its grasp of the concrete mediation of divinity and humanity
• ••Christianity••
	• Emerges in the context of Hellenistic Judaism and fills a void at the heart of Roman culture
	• Overcomes the limitations of Jewish and Greek religions
• ••Hinduism and Buddhism••
	• Recognized as having a spiritual substance, not a natural one
	• Strains in treating these religions under the category of nature religion are becoming apparent
• ••Greek Religion••
	• Considered the religion of beauty
	• Necessary soil for the consummate religion (Christianity) to come to birth
• ••Judaism••
	• Insight into the unity, rationality, and spirituality of God
	• Necessary soil for the consummate religion (Christianity) to come to birth
• ••Roman Religion••
	• An abortive, retrograde, and expedient amalgamation of the religions
	• Proves to be an ineffective and limiting form of religion

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Religion••
	• Religion is not an immediate or static concept, but a living and acting process of mediation with itself.
	• This process involves a cycle of immediacy, development, and recapitulation.

â€¢ ••Historical Development of Religion••
	• Hegel believes that religion is historical and exists as a particular shape of religion.
	• Christianity presents a paradox of being historical and concrete, while also transcending determinacy and finitude.

â€¢ ••Immediacy, Rupture, Reconciliation••
	• In the 1831 lectures, Hegel introduces a new structure of immediacy, rupture, and reconciliation.
	• This approach is more strictly dialectical and descriptive of the life of the concept and the dialectic of consciousness.

â€¢ ••Nature Religion••
	• Nature religion or magic is the first moment of immediacy, which is not yet properly religious.
	• This stage is characterized by a lack of distinction between the natural and the essential.

â€¢ ••Rupture of Consciousness••
	• The rupture of consciousness occurs when it distinguishes its sensuous nature from what is essential and knows the difference between natural power and God.
	• This rupture permits an elevation of the spiritual above the natural.

â€¢ ••Oriental Religions••
	• The Oriental religions of substance (Chinese, Hindu, and Buddhist/Lamaist) are distinguished from nature religion and analyzed with a more appropriate concept.
	• These religions are characterized by a cleavage or rupture of consciousness.

â€¢ ••Religions of Substance••
	• The religions of substance are characterized by a cleavage or rupture of consciousness, which is overcome through a reconciliation of consciousness and its object.
	• This reconciliation occurs at a higher, mediated level, where freedom becomes actual for the first time.

â€¢ ••Religions of Finite Freedom••
	• The religions of finite freedom run through three phases: the religion of the good, the religion of anguish, and the religion of ferment.
	• These religions are characterized by a struggle of subjectivity to establish itself over against impersonal substance.

â€¢ ••Religion of Freedom Proper••
	• The religion of freedom proper, or Greek religion, is the second phase of the religions of finite freedom.
	• In this phase, the subject knows itself to be free in relation to the divine object.

â€¢ ••Infinite Antithesis••
	• The subject has not yet passed through the infinite antithesis, which is necessary for the full development of the concept of freedom.
	• This antithesis is overcome in the Christian religion, which is the culmination of the dialectical process.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Hegel's View of Egyptian Religion••
	• Derived from classical mythology, not a historical religion
	• Represented by the sphinx and pyramids, symbolizing the unexpressed and obscure
	• Based on classical sources, with some knowledge of recent archaeological discoveries
• ••Greek Religion••
	• The religion of beauty, linked to symbolic representations of free human spirituality
	• Human beings are free individuals responsible for their actions, but subject to fate
	• Gods are not yet infinite spirit, reconciliation is not complete
• ••Roman Religion••
	• Issues in infinite unhappiness and anguish, a precursor to a new religion
	• Undoes the relative reconciliation and freedom gained through previous religions
• ••The Progress of the Consciousness of Freedom••
	• The history of religion is aligned with the history of the world
	• The progress of the consciousness of freedom is a key theme in Hegel's philosophy
• ••Judaism and the Oriental Religions••
	• Judaism is on the way to freedom, but has not achieved its ethical actualization
	• Persian, Jewish, Phoenician, and Egyptian religion are lumped together as transitional
• ••The Movement of Spirit••
	• Spirit emerges in a diversity of places and times, not in a simple chronological sequence
	• The movement is a struggle for justice, bringing ethical powers into collision
• ••The Relationship of Religion to the State••
	• Hegel's treatment of this topic is discussed in Chap. 9
	• The relationship is complex and changing, with different patterns emerging across the earth
• ••The Consummate Religion••
	• The religion of infinite freedom is the ultimate goal of the movement of spirit
	• This religion is not yet achieved, but is the direction of the movement
• ••The Geographical Movement of Spirit••
	• Spirit moves from East to West, from China to India to the Middle East
	• The movement is not yet complete, with many possibilities for future development
• ••The Future of the Movement of Spirit••
	• The movement will continue to unfold in complex and changing patterns
	• The ultimate goal is the consummate religion of infinite freedom

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's unfinished work on the geography of world religions••
	• Hegel's time had run out, leaving his work on the geography of world religions incomplete.
	• His struggles with this material demonstrate that he was not a pontificating philosopher who reduced historical actuality to abstract categories.

â€¢ ••Hegel's conceptual play••
	• Hegel's philosophy is a kind of conceptual play based on imaginative variation to arrive at new insights.
	• He was willing to incorporate new data and experiment with new schemes.

â€¢ ••Hegel's use of philosophical categories••
	• Hegel used philosophical categories to interpret religions, connected to his understanding of the concept of religion.
	• These categories were shaped and varied in accord with the concrete materials that needed interpretation.

â€¢ ••Hegel's experimental approach••
	• Hegel's treatment of the history of religion formed an experimental field where virtually everything was tried out.
	• He was reluctant to commit anything to print as long as the process of thinking remained open and fluid.

â€¢ ••The medium of oral lectures••
	• The medium of oral lectures was ideally suited for Hegel's experimental approach.
	• He was not a prisoner of his own system, but rather a way and discipline of thinking.

â€¢ ••Hegel's typology of shapes••
	• Hegel's work on the history of religion is better described as a typology of shapes in which spirit appears in religious history.
	• These shapes need not, and cannot, be linked into a unitary history directed to a common goal.

â€¢ ••The diversity and plurality of religions••
	• Hegel demonstrated the necessity of a diversity and plurality of religions.
	• Spirit comes to itself only through movement and distinction.

â€¢ ••The historicality of human spirit••
	• Religion is fundamentally historical, but its historicality follows from the historicality of human spirit.
	• There is no single history of human spirit, so there cannot be a single, unified history of religion.

â€¢ ••The impossibility of a single, unified history of religion••
	• Hegel's claim to be able to construct a single, unified history of religion is falsified by his actual achievement.
	• The objective of a logical construction of the history of religion cannot be attained.

â€¢ ••Hegel's achievement••
	• Hegel's work on the history of religion is a history of religions, or better, histories of religions.
	• This diversity of histories cannot be organized under a single, encompassing philosophical conceptuality.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Universal History of Religion••
	• Hegel's concept of a universal history of religion is not supported by the geography of religions
	• The shapes of religions appear as dispersed geographically in distinctive cultural trajectories

â€¢ ••Hegel's Recognition of Multiple Trajectories••
	• Hegel recognizes that each religio-cultural trajectory retrogresses and forms a knot, leading to multiple lines in history
	• Spirit moves to another trajectory, resulting in many lines that cannot be amalgamated

â€¢ ••Christianity as the Consummate Religion••
	• Hegel does not establish the consummate or absolute character of Christianity directly from the course of determinate religions
	• Instead, he pictures a break occurring and a new religion emerging out of crisis

â€¢ ••The Concept of Religion••
	• The concept of religion is established on the basis of definition, with Christianity being the fullest instantiation of it
	• However, the geography of religions allows for a more pluralistic approach, with multiple ways to complete the concept of religion

â€¢ ••Absoluteness and Concreteness••
	• Absoluteness requires concreteness (individuality) but not singularity (once-and-for-allness)
	• Hegel's geography allows for a diversity of forms in which God is known

â€¢ ••God as Absolute Spirit••
	• God is known in the mode of dispersal rather than finality
	• Recognition of this fact increases as religions become acquainted with each other across their differences

â€¢ ••Hegel's Affinity with Asian Religions••
	• Hegel's definition of the concept of religion is close to the core insight of Hinduism and Buddhism
	• Hinduism recognizes only one true self for all reality, with self and highest reality (Brahman) mirroring each other

â€¢ ••The Unity of Selfhood or Spirit••
	• Spirit is advaitic (not-two) but also not simply one
	• Spiritual substance is one mediated by many through a spiralling process

â€¢ ••The Process of Division and Reunion••
	• The world becomes more diverse, and creativity is an ongoing struggle
	• The process of division and reunion occurs at a higher level of complexity

â€¢ ••The Possibility of Multiple Forms of Knowing God••
	• God as absolute spirit disperses godself into the world absolvingly
	• God is known in a diversity of forms, rather than in a single, perfect religion

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Hegel's View of Pluralism••
	• Requires acknowledgment of plurality and a wish for unity
	• Goes beyond mere recognition of different religions
• ••Hegel's Treatment of Chinese Religion••
	• Initially views Chinese religion as part of the religion of magic
	• Later interprets it as the religion of heaven or Tian
• ••Hegel's Understanding of Daoism••
	• Sees Daoism as a sect of Chinese religion
	• Emphasizes the way of nature is also the way of reason
• ••The Concept of the Dao••
	• Is seen as rational and concrete
	• Is associated with the number three and the universe
• ••The Indeterminate One••
	• Is considered an impoverished and empty abstraction
	• Is not sufficient to generate the determinateness of the universe
• ••The Symbolism of the Dao••
	• Is associated with the triangle and three horizontal lines
	• Represents the unity of the three
• ••The Religion of Measure••
	• Is defined as the third category of the doctrine of being
	• Unites quality and quantity, or substance and accident
• ••The Measures in Daoism••
	• Take the form of abstract categories (e.g. one and two, being and nonbeing)
	• Derive all other categorial distinctions (e.g. geographical, physical, musical)
• ••The Importance of Measures••
	• Are necessary for the moral and religious life
	• Require finding the mean between opposing categories
• ••The Limitations of Chinese Religion••
	• Is considered an abstract and incomplete religion
	• Does not yet have a higher level of understanding

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Hinduism••
	• In the 1824 and 1827 lectures, Hegel calls Hinduism "Fanciful Imagination" (Phantasie)
	• In the 1831 lectures, he calls it the "religion of abstract unity"

â€¢ ••Hinduism's Conceptual Advance••
	• Provides the conceptual advance from natural powers to unitary substance
	• Resumes the multiplicity of Chinese measures into unity (Brahman)

â€¢ ••Brahman's Limitations••
	• Its thinking remains locked into self-containment
	• The actual laboration of difference falls outside it in a wild infinity

â€¢ ••The Trimurti••
	• Corresponds to the three moments of Brahman: inward self-containment, outward multiplicity, and spiritless resumption
	• Represents the three figures of the Hindu Trimurti (Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva)

â€¢ ••Hindu Cosmogonies and Epics••
	• Hegel attends to the Hindu cosmogonies, epics, and especially the Ra  ma yana
	• Despite this attention, the interpretation is no more favorable than in earlier lectures

â€¢ ••Negative Assessment••
	• The mismatch between content and form accounts for the ugliness of the mythological figures
	• Imagination passes back and forth between external existence and divinity without a rational nexus of thought categories

â€¢ ••Hegel's Familiarity with Hindu Texts••
	• He had some familiarity with primary texts (Upanishads, Laws of Manu, Maha  bha rata, Bhagavad-G  ta, and Ra  ma yana)

â€¢ ••Secondary Sources and Prejudice••
	• His secondary sources reflected the prejudice of the East India Company, which represented Indian culture and society as corrupt
	• This prejudice influenced Hegel's interpretation of Hinduism

â€¢ ••Hinduism's Abstract Unity••
	• The unity of Brahman is abstract and lacks spiritual subjectivity
	• This abstract unity is taken back into itself in a spiritless fashion

â€¢ ••Comparison to Christianity••
	• Hegel notes that the trinity in unity is the most striking and greatest feature in Hindu mythology
	• This principle is similar to the Christian religion, but lacks spiritual subjectivity.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Critique of Hinduism••
	• He was suspicious of the German Romantics' idealization of Hinduism
	• He believed Hinduism's cultic and ethical practices were destructive of human freedom and flourishing

â€¢ ••Key Points from the 1824 Lectures••
	• Brahman, the universal substance, is not personal and is worshipped as a neuter One (das Eine)
	• Brahman is abstract and unknowable, requiring human consciousness to achieve subjectivity
	• The human relationship to Brahman requires renouncing living and concrete things, leading to dehumanizing ascetic practices

â€¢ ••Consequences of Brahman's Abstractness••
	• The caste system deifies the Brahmin caste and regards others as less worthy or worthless
	• The religion is devoid of spirit, with an isolated universal and a region of the particular characterized by irrationality and unfreedom

â€¢ ••The Problem of Brahman's Impersonality••
	• Brahman is impersonal substance, but spiritual and not material
	• It must have within itself the potential for personification, but this is not achieved through itself

â€¢ ••Hinduism's Oscillation between Extremes••
	• It finds expression in beautiful poetry, but also in irrationality and nonsense
	• It attracts and repels at the same time, with a confusing and contradictory nature

â€¢ ••Additional Criticisms from the 1827 Lectures••
	• Ascetic rigors and self-immolation are degrading to human life
	• Superstition and lack of freedom lead to an unethical life with no determinate form of rational freedom

â€¢ ••Hegel's Recognition of Hinduism's Significance••
	• It is the earliest religion to affirm that divinity is the one substance or essence in which all things have their being
	• It transcends monotheism and is also polytheistic and tritheistic

â€¢ ••Comparison to Jewish Monotheism••
	• Jewish monotheism is preferable to Hinduism because it has a personal God
