	• Hinduism's Brahman is substance but not yet subject, lacking the subjectivity of a personal God

â€¢ ••The Trimurti and the Christian Trinity••
	• The Trimurti prefigures the Christian Trinity, but with a different third divine figure
	• Shiva, the creative-destructive energy of life, brings about sheer change instead of the return of the One to itself in the Spirit

â€¢ ••Hinduism's Lack of Reconciliation••
	• It lacks the reconciliation of the elements of being-within-self and self-differentiation in the constitution of divinity
	• It is characterized by restless diversity and a lack of determinate form of rational freedom.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Hinduism••
	• Hegel was fascinated by Hinduism's poetic creations and grand-scale mythologies
	• He recognized the imaginative power and intellectual depth of the Vedas and Upanishads
	• However, he was repulsed by what he saw as Hinduism's ethical failures

â€¢ ••Hinduism's Divine Incarnations••
	• Hegel noted that Hinduism's second divine figure, Vishnu or Krishna, produces multiple and transitory incarnations
	• This is in contrast to a single definitive incarnation

â€¢ ••Hegel's Limited Understanding of Hinduism••
	• Hegel did not appreciate the philosophical subtlety, spiritual wisdom, and internal diversity of Hinduism
	• His understanding was limited by his sources and cultural perspective

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Buddhism••
	• Hegel placed the life of Buddha around 1000 BCE, which led him to assume Buddhism was an older religion than Hinduism
	• He confused the date of Siddhartha Gautama with the introduction of Buddhism into China

â€¢ ••Buddhism's Spread and Adherents••
	• Hegel noted that Buddhism was the most widespread religion in the world, with more adherents than Islam
	• He was likely mistaken about the numbers, as Christianity is now the most populous religion

â€¢ ••Hegel's Depiction of Buddhism••
	• Hegel described Buddhism as the "religion of being-within-self" (Insichsein)
	• He later referred to it as the "religion of annihilation" (Vernichtung)

â€¢ ••Buddhism and Lamaism••
	• Hegel saw Buddhism and Lamaism as similar to Hinduism, but with key differences
	• In Lamaism, the individual is worshipped as absolute power, whereas in Buddhism, the goal is to be united with a dead teacher, Buddha

â€¢ ••Hegel's Sources and Understanding••
	• Hegel's understanding of Buddhism was based on reports from English travellers to the Far East
	• He was not familiar with the main schools of Buddhism and confused Chinese Buddhism and Tibetan Lamaism

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Christianity••
	• Christianity was not a major focus of Hegel's lectures on world religions
	• He did not present a triumphalist scenario, but rather saw the consummation of Christianity as a conceptual, not historical, event

â€¢ ••Hegel's Cultural and Historical Context••
	• Hegel's views on world religions were shaped by his cultural and historical context
	• His understanding of the numbers of adherents and the spread of religions was likely mistaken, reflecting the limited knowledge of his time.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Buddhism as a religion••
	• Hegel initially treats Buddhism as a religion of magic
	• However, he recognizes that this designation is inaccurate
	• Buddhism represents a more advanced stage of religious consciousness

â€¢ ••The unity of finite and infinite••
	• Religion is the unity of finite and infinite, concept and reality
	• This unity is achieved through the recognition of essential being (Wesen)
	• Essential being is independent, self-relating, and eternal

â€¢ ••The emergence of divinity••
	• The place of divinity emerges in the being-within-self
	• This being-within-self is the essence of all existence
	• It is pure, simple, and undifferentiated, eternally at rest

â€¢ ••The state of nirvana••
	• Nirvana is the state of union with God
	• It is characterized by perfect impartiality and absence of concern
	• All determinate distinctions, such as virtue and vice, are transcended

â€¢ ••The depiction of nirvana in 1827 lectures••
	• Nirvana is described as a being-within-self, the one substance
	• This substance has existence in sensible presence, in singular human beings
	• The image of the Buddha in the thinking posture expresses this withdrawal into self

â€¢ ••The ultimate reality••
	• The ultimate reality is nothing or not-being, the negated being of everything particular
	• All other actuality is accidental and dependent
	• Humans must immerse themselves in nirvana to achieve union with God

â€¢ ••Negative theology••
	• The characterization of God as nothing means that God is the indeterminate
	• God is the negation of everything particular
	• This negative theology is a necessary stage of religious representation

â€¢ ••The foundation for further claims••
	• Negative theology lays the foundation for the claim that God is also the absolute fullness or substance
	• This substance encompasses all determinacies within itself but is not identical with any of them
	• This substance is similar to the concept of nirvana

â€¢ ••The nature of God••
	• God is the empty, and this emptiness is God
	• God is not identical with any determinate being or attribute
	• God is the absolute, the one substance that encompasses all determinacies

â€¢ ••The significance of nirvana••
	• Nirvana represents the ultimate goal of human existence
	• It is the state of union with God, characterized by perfect impartiality and absence of concern
	• Nirvana transcends all determinate distinctions and achieves eternal rest.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's understanding of nous••
	• Nous is the ancient Greek concept of unconscious rationality
	• Hegel conflates Buddhist nirvana with Hindu Brahman
	• He sees substantiality as pantheism, where all things have their essential being in God

â€¢ ••Pantheism and the All••
	• The All (pan) is not a collective totality (Allesheit), but universality (Allgemeinheit)
	• This is a key passage for the claim that Hegel's holism is non-totalizing

â€¢ ••God as substance and subject••
	• In higher religions, including Christianity, God is both substance and subject
	• God's subjectivity and personality are a higher determination than mere substance

â€¢ ••Tension between nirvana and human beings••
	• The emptiness of nirvana is contrasted with the notion that specific human beings are God
	• This tension remains unresolved in Hegel's discussion

â€¢ ••Buddhism and pantheism••
	• Hegel's evaluation of pantheism is found in the context of his conflated treatment of Buddhism/Hinduism
	• He recognizes the spiritual insight of Asian religions and seeks to protect them from crude misinterpretations

â€¢ ••Shared conviction among religions••
	• Judaism, Buddhism, Hinduism, and Christianity share the conviction that God is the one and absolute substance
	• Judaism and Christianity grasp the further truth that the absolute substance is also absolute subject

â€¢ ••Hegel's evolving treatment of Judaism••
	• His interpretation of Judaism undergoes a striking metamorphosis between 1821 and 1827
	• The first lectures share the perspective of the Early Theological Writings

â€¢ ••Judaism as alienation and The Phenomenology of Spirit••
	• In the Early Theological Writings, the spirit of Judaism is represented as alienation (masterservant relationship)
	• This is also reflected in The Phenomenology of Spirit

â€¢ ••Nachman Krochmal's Aufhebung of Hegel's views on Judaism••
	• The 19th-century Jewish scholar Nachman Krochmal performed an Aufhebung of Hegel's views on Judaism
	• This Aufhebung is within the framework of a Hegelian philosophy of history

â€¢ ••Hegel's recognition of Jewish spirituality••
	• Hegel recognizes the universality and spirituality of the idea of monotheism in Judaism
	• The Jewish people are seen as the true bearers of absolute spirit and able to transcend temporality

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Hegel's Interpretation of Judaism••
	• Associated with the concept of a luminous being (Lichtwesen)
	• Viewed as a religion of sublimity (Erhabenheit)
• ••Sources of Hegel's Knowledge••
	• Primarily based on his reading of the Hebrew scriptures
	• Limited to the Pentateuch, Job, and the Psalms in the first three lectures
	• Later lectures include references to the universalism of the later prophets
• ••Key Concepts in Jewish Religion••
	• God is a personal One (der Eine), not a neuter One (das Eine)
	• God is all-powerful and expressed through pure light
	• The world is contingent and dependent on God's power
• ••Relationship between Judaism and Christianity••
	• Hegel struggles with the question of the relationship between the two religions
	• Christianity is seen as a liberation from the servile consciousness of Judaism
• ••The Jewish Cultus••
	• A fundamental expression of the servile consciousness and masterservant relationship
	• Humanity's relationship to God is one of onerous service
• ••Fear of the Lord••
	• The absolute religious duty in Judaism
	• Involves regarding oneself as nothing and knowing oneself as absolutely dependent
• ••Comparison to Christianity••
	• Christianity is seen as a liberation from the servile consciousness of Judaism
	• The Trinity is viewed as a more developed and inwardly concrete concept of God
• ••Hegel's View of Jewish Survival••
	• Recognizes the fact of Jewish survival, but cannot account for it
	• Attributes it to the legendary phoenix-like rebirth of Judaism
• ••Influence of Other Thinkers••
	• Hegel's views on Judaism are influenced by Schleiermacher's Glaubenslehre
	• He also engages with the ideas of Krochmal and Rosenkranz
• ••Recurring Themes in Hegel's Thought••
	• Jewish existence keeps reappearing in Hegel's thought, despite its supposed anachronism
	• This is seen as a flaw in the Hegelian mediation between Judaism and Christianity

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Judaism••
	• Initially, Hegel views Judaism as an expression of Jewish piety, rather than Christian piety.
	• He sees Judaism as a national or ethnic religion, with a unique relationship between God and the Jewish people.

â€¢ ••Jewish Obstinacy and Particularity••
	• Hegel believes that Jewish obstinacy and particularity arise from the conviction that the Jewish people alone are God's people.
	• This conviction is rooted in the idea that the land of Israel is a gift from God, which can be taken away and restored at God's will.

â€¢ ••The Beginning of Wisdom (1824)••
	• In 1824, Hegel presents a more balanced and developed assessment of Judaism.
	• He recognizes the importance of the concept of God in Judaism, as the infinitely important recognition that God is simply and solely the One.

â€¢ ••God's Self-Determination••
	• Hegel sees God's self-determination as expressed representatively in Judaism.
	• He discovers that God's fundamental self-determination is external, through the act of creation and preservation.

â€¢ ••Creation and Preservation••
	• God's creation and preservation of the world show forth God's goodness and justice.
	• This is an absolute creation, ex nihilo, an inner, intuitive, eternal activity on God's part.

â€¢ ••The Relationship between God and the World••
	• In Jewish religion, the relationship between God and the world is understood in terms of God's sublimity.
	• God is exalted above the reality in which God appears, and the reality itself is negated or totally subjected to God's power.

â€¢ ••The Representation of God's Purpose••
	• God's theoretical purpose is to be recognized and glorified by all humanity.
	• God's practical, worldly purpose is an ethical purpose, having its soil in human self-consciousness and freedom.

â€¢ ••The Family as the Ethical Form••
	• The family is the ethical form in which the divine purpose is realized in Jewish religion.
	• This is the one family, the Jewish people, to the exclusion of all others.

â€¢ ••The Contradiction in Jewish Religion••
	• There is a striking contradiction in Jewish religion between God's universality and God's limited purposes and operations.
	• God is universal, but God's purposes and operations are confined to just one people, defined by birth and race.

â€¢ ••Jewish Particularity and Fanaticism••
	• Jewish particularity is not polemical, as there is no obligation to convert other peoples to the God of Israel.
	• Judaism has become fanatical only when attacked or when its existence has been threatened.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Fear of the Lord••
	• In 1824, Hegel views fear of the Lord as the beginning of wisdom
	• This fear is not of an earthly lord, but of the absolute
	• It involves recognizing the relativity of all finite things

â€¢ ••Liberation from Bondage••
	• Fear of the Lord leads to a fundamental liberation from earthly forms of bondage
	• It involves letting go of all finite things and immersing oneself in the Lord
	• This is a far cry from a feeling of dependence

â€¢ ••Judaism as a Religion of Freedom••
	• In 1824, Hegel views Judaism as the first religion of freedom
	• This is a reversal of his earlier view in 1821, where Judaism was seen as an embodiment of dependence
	• He now sees Schleiermacher's definition of religious piety as applying to the Romans, not Judaism

â€¢ ••Affirmative Side of Jewish Worship••
	• The fear of the Lord yields an absolute trust and infinite faith
	• This trust is preserved through great victories and is the foundation of Jewish existence
	• It is this trust that causes the history of the Jewish people to continue

â€¢ ••Shift in Hegel's View of Abraham••
	• In 1824, Hegel's view of Abraham and his people is more positive than in The Spirit of Christianity and Its Fate
	• He now sees Abraham's faith as a model for Jewish existence

â€¢ ••Narrowing the Distance between Judaism and Christianity••
	• The 1824 lectures narrow the distance between Judaism and Christianity
	• The profound aspects of Jewish piety are absorbed into Christian faith
	• Christianity extends the universalism implicit in Judaism and introduces the process of divine self-determination

â€¢ ••The Infinite Subjectivity of God••
	• In the 1827 lectures, Hegel views the God of Israel as infinite subjectivity
	• This subjectivity is without shape and subsists independently of the natural world
	• It is this distinction that lies behind the elevation of the religion of beauty into the religion of sublimity

â€¢ ••Judaism as a Religion of Sublimity••
	• The 1827 lectures continue the favorable reassessment of Judaism established in 1824
	• Judaism is seen as a religion of sublimity, which elevates the spiritual above the natural
	• It shares this characteristic with Greek religion, but in a more profound way

â€¢ ••The Great Contribution of Israel••
	• The great contribution of Israel to the history of religion is its comprehension of the spiritually subjective unity of God
	• This unity is not mere substance, but absolute power, wisdom, and purpose
	• It is holy and merits the name God for the first time

â€¢ ••Infinite Subjectivity as the Essence of God••
	• Infinite subjectivity is the essence of God in the Jewish idea
	• It is the source of the divine life and the foundation of all creation
	• It is the key to understanding the relationship between God and the world

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's Nature••
	• God subsists without sensible shape, only for thought
	• Thinking is the essential soil for this object
	• God's wisdom contains the process of divine particularization

â€¢ ••God's Process••
	• God's wisdom contains the process of divine self-determining, judging, and creating
	• This process is not yet posited within God concretely, but remains abstract and external
	• It is not yet an immanent Trinity

â€¢ ••Creation••
	• God is the creator of the world
	• The world is created ex nihilo, not emanating from God
	• The subjectivity of the One remains what is absolutely first

â€¢ ••God's Relations to the World••
	• God's relations to the world are definitive of God's own being
	• We know God in knowing God's relations
	• The categories of goodness and justice are now defined more fully

â€¢ ••God's Attributes••
	• As good, God releases and sets free the created world
	• As just, God maintains the world in relation to Godself
	• God does not abandon the world to radical autonomy

â€¢ ••The World's Nature••
	• The world is rendered profane, prosaic
	• Nature is divested of divinity
	• There is no cheap identity of finite and infinite

â€¢ ••God's Purpose••
	• God's purpose is made manifest in the natural and human world
	• The purpose is that the whole earth and all peoples should proclaim the glory of God
	• This glorification of God is the inner aspect of all human ethical activity

â€¢ ••Limitations of Judaism••
	• The self-determining wisdom of God is not yet an inward self-development
	• The God of Judaism remains a national God, rather than the God of the whole human family
	• The divine purposes are abstract and immutable

â€¢ ••Overcoming Limitations in Christianity••
	• Christianity overcomes the limitations of Judaism
	• The concept of God as triune is a key aspect of this overcoming
	• The non-provincial character of the community of the Spirit is also significant

â€¢ ••Judaism as Foundation for Christianity••
	• The soil in which Christianity emerged could only be that of Judaism
	• Judaism's doctrines of God and creation are the foundation of the new religion

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's creation and goodness••
	• God creates out of God's own being, not from primordial matter
	• This creation is an act of goodness

â€¢ ••The source of evil••
	• Evil is not due to cosmic dualism (e.g. Persian religion)
	• Evil is not due to the flawed character of absolute substance (e.g. Greek theology of necessity or fate)
	• Evil is due to the free fall of finite spirit (the Adamic myth)

â€¢ ••The story of the fall••
	• This story is a profoundly speculative feature of the narrative
	• It is transferred from the context of Christian theology to Judaism in 1831
	• The story of the fall is essential to Jewish religion, but it remains unresolved in Judaism

â€¢ ••Judaism's limitations••
	• Judaism's depth of anguish is known only to the individual, not as an eternal moment of spirit
	• Judaism's tension between individual and universal is unresolved
	• Judaism's laws are not laws of freedom, but are prescribed by God

â€¢ ••Judaism's laws and authority••
	• Jewish laws are given by the Lord, not by reason
	• These laws are positive commandments, with formal and absolute authority
	• The particular aspects of the Jewish political constitution are not developed from the universal purpose

â€¢ ••The Jewish people's vocation••
	• The Jewish people's vocation is to serve the Lord, which accounts for their steadfastness
	• However, this means there is no freedom vis-a-vis this firm bond

â€¢ ••The Lord's role••
	• The Lord does not enter into human combat with evil, but punishes evil
	• The finite subject engages in an unresolved struggle between good and evil

â€¢ ••Liberation from evil••
	• Liberation from evil can only occur if the struggle and anguish are taken into the divine life itself
	• This is a theme that Hegel returns to in the 1831 lectures

â€¢ ••Hegel's concerns••
	• Hegel is concerned with the relationship between religion and state
	• He is concerned with creating and preserving free political institutions

â€¢ ••Judaism's limitations as a foundation for a free state••
	• Judaism is on the way to freedom, but it is not a free religion
	• Only a free religion can serve as the foundation of a free state

• ••Hegel's View of Greek and Jewish Religions••
	• Hegel sees Greek and Jewish religions as one-sided and incomplete
	• Greek religion is higher from the point of view of the mediation of divinity and humanity
	• Jewish religion is higher from the point of view of the idea of God, monotheism, and spiritual unity
• ••The Relationship Between Greek and Jewish Religions••
	• Christianity is a new religion that emerges from the mix of Greek and Jewish influences
	• Christianity is not a simple development of Judaism, but a genuinely new religion
	• The contrasting emphases of the 1827 and 1831 lectures highlight the complexity of this relationship
• ••The One-Sidedness of Christianity••
	• Christianity has its own one-sidednesses, cultural compromises, and ethical failings
	• These one-sidednesses become apparent when Christianity is compared with other living religions
• ••Beyond Inclusivism to Pluralism••
	• Christianity cannot claim to be the consummate religion in a pluralistic framework
	• Instead, Christianity is one of the determinate religions that share in each other's wisdom
• ••The Concept of Consummation••
	• Consummation occurs in each of the post-Axial religious trajectories in distinctive ways
	• Consummation is a richer, dialogical process that involves the unification of religions in the Spirit
• ••The Eschatological Nature of Consummation••
	• Consummation is a strictly eschatological possibility, never achieved on earth
	• On earth, God's Spirit is always concretely manifested and cannot be domesticated by any determinate religion
• ••Hegel's Philosophy of Religion and Pluralism••
	• Hegel's dialectical thinking fits better with the situation of religious pluralism than Christian inclusivism
	• Hegel's geography of religions points to a pluralism that cannot be ordered into a historical development or hierarchical sequence
• ••The Typological Nature of Transitions••
	• Transitions between types of religions involve breaks or ruptures in which new possibilities emerge
	• Spirit comes to itself through movement and distinction, resulting in a diversity of determinate forms
• ••The Diversity of Religio-Cultural Trajectories••
	• Empirically, we find distinctive religio-cultural trajectories that have gone their separate ways for most of history
	• These trajectories are not necessarily ordered into a historical development or hierarchical sequence
• ••The Importance of Recognizing the Novelty and Limits of Christianity••
	• Recognizing the novelty and limits of Christianity allows for the continued existence of Judaism as an independent and equally valid religion
	• This recognition also allows for a more nuanced understanding of the relationship between Christianity and other living religions.

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Religion••
	• Hegel sees religion as a way for absolute spirit to come to itself
	• This process involves distinctions and directions, and is the path by which spirit comes to itself

â€¢ ••The Goal of Spirit••
	• The absolute goal is for spirit to recognize itself and be for itself
	• This is achieved through the movement of spirit coming to itself

â€¢ ••Determinate Religions••

	• Christianity is one of these determinate religions, but it is not the final goal

â€¢ ••The Theme of Freedom••
	• Hegel believes that the common theme marking the path of religion is freedom
	• This freedom is not just human freedom, but the freedom of God as absolute/absolving spirit

â€¢ ••Freedom as Presence to Self••
	• Freedom means presence to self, mediated in and through relationships with others
	• This freedom is grounded in the freedom of God

â€¢ ••The Reich der Freiheit••
	• An objective realm of freedom, or Reich der Freiheit, must be created for subjective freedom to flourish
	• Religions' vocation is to contribute to this freedom project

â€¢ ••Religion's Role••
	• Religion must be liberated from inadequate representational forms and rise to critical and speculative thinking
	• This thinking recognizes the limits of all metaphors, symbols, and practices

â€¢ ••Religious Pluralism••

	• This shakenness is a common experience across different religions

â€¢ ••Freedom in World Religions••

	• In Asian religions, freedom is achieved through liberation from illusion, suffering, and desire
	• In Judaism, freedom is achieved through worship of the one true Lord
	• In Greek religion, freedom is achieved through the fashioning of free political and social institutions

â€¢ ••The Future of Religion••
	• Religion itself will not be superseded, but will be liberated from inadequate forms and rise to critical and speculative thinking
	• This thinking will keep us focused on the affairs of the world.

	• William Desmond argues that the God portrayed in Hegel's philosophy is a counterfeit, rather than the true God of Christian faith.
	• Desmond's critique is anticipated by SÃ¸ren Kierkegaard and Ludwig Feuerbach, who also read Hegel as a pantheist.

â€¢ ••Hegel's philosophy of spirit••
	• Hegel's philosophy of spirit is seen as a tension between heterodoxy and orthodoxy (ontotheology).
	• This tension is a key aspect of Hegel's thought, and is relevant to contemporary theological debates.

â€¢ ••Beyond totality and infinity••
	• Hegel's thought offers a possibility of wholeness, which goes beyond the alternative of totality and infinity.
	• This wholeness is a key aspect of Hegel's philosophy, and is relevant to contemporary debates about the nature of God.

â€¢ ••Trinitarian metanarrative••
	• Hegel's thought offers a trinitarian metanarrative, which emerges from the interplay of language and history, logic and time.
	• This metanarrative is a key aspect of Hegel's philosophy, and is relevant to contemporary debates about the nature of God.

â€¢ ••Redemption through tragedy••
	• Hegel's thought offers a redemption that incorporates suffering and death into the life of God.
	• This redemption is mediated by the crucified and risen Christ, and is a key aspect of Hegel's philosophy.

â€¢ ••Community and the autonomous self••
	• Hegel's thought offers a community in which each individual exists for the sake of and from the gift of others.
	• This community is a key aspect of Hegel's philosophy, and is relevant to contemporary debates about the nature of human relationships.

â€¢ ••Religious pluralism••
	• Hegel's thought offers a religious unity that arises from an inexhaustible diversity.
	• This unity is a key aspect of Hegel's philosophy, and is relevant to contemporary debates about the nature of religious pluralism.

â€¢ ••Hegel's Philosophy of Religion••
	• Introduces becoming into logic and excludes the possibility of the offense of reason
	• Views God as an absolute spirit that determines itself in terms of finite consciousness

	• Proclaims the infinite qualitative difference between God and man
	• Views God as an unmoved mover who radically transcends history
	• Criticizes Hegel's system as a monistic pantheism that loses the absolute otherness of God

â€¢ ••Desmond's Representation of Kierkegaard's Critique••

â€¢ ••Feuerbach's Pantheism••
	• Materialistic, not idealistic
	• Converts into an atheistic humanism that views God as a projection of the human species-essence

â€¢ ••Feuerbach's Critique of Hegel••
	• Aligns with Hegel in understanding religion as a project of human self-consciousness
	• Criticizes Hegel's view of religious self-consciousness as only the subjective side of absolute spirit

â€¢ ••Feuerbach's View of Truth••
	• Resides only in the sensual and actual, not in the ideal or divine
	• Collapses Hegel's mediation of objective and subjective into a single, material element

â€¢ ••The Breakdown of Mediation••
	• Characterizes the post-Hegelian period
	• Raises the question of whether Hegelian mediation is possible without damaging the mediated elements

â€¢ ••Alternatives to Mediation••
	• Theological supernaturalism
	• Secular naturalism

â€¢ ••Contemporary Scholarship on Hegel••
	• Focuses on aspects of Hegel's thought other than philosophy of religion
	• Does not directly criticize the religious dimension of Hegel's philosophy

â€¢ ••Implications for the Philosophy of Religion••
	• The breakdown of mediation raises questions about the nature of God and the world
	• The ongoing discussion of Hegel's philosophy continues to shape the philosophy of religion.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Interpreting Hegel's philosophy••
	• Some scholars interpret Hegel's philosophy as antimetaphysical and antireligious, fitting into postmodern paradigms.
	• They view Hegel's system as mythological or representational, but retain fragments that treat this-worldly realities.

â€¢ ••Feuerbach and Baur's influence••
	• Scholars like Feuerbach and Baur have influenced the interpretation of Hegel's philosophy.
	• They see Hegel's philosophy as a critique of modern secularism and atheism.

â€¢ ••Desmond's critique of Hegel••
	• Desmond critiques Hegel's philosophy from a Christian orthodox perspective.
	• He argues that Hegel's philosophy is not bothered by orthodoxy and instead invents its own truth.

â€¢ ••Desmond's charges against Hegel••
	• Desmond charges that Hegel's philosophy is not truly Christian, but rather mimics orthodoxy.
	• He argues that Hegel's philosophy is too Eleatic and not Jewish enough.

â€¢ ••Desmond's narrow reading of orthodoxy••
	• Desmond gives a narrow reading of orthodoxy, devaluing the Greek, mystical, and humanistic strands of the Christian tradition.
	• He emphasizes the infinite qualitative difference between eternity and time, God and humanity.

â€¢ ••Hegel's philosophy of immanence••
	• Desmond sees Hegel's philosophy as a philosophy of immanence based on human self-transcendence.
	• He argues that Hegel's philosophy critiques the God who is beyond, and instead posits God as an absolutely self-determining being.

â€¢ ••Desmond's critique of Hegel's transcendence••
	• Desmond argues that Hegel's transcendence is not ultimate, but rather a self-completing immanence or holism.
	• He claims that Hegel's philosophy does not find an other, but rather a self-completing immanence.

â€¢ ••Hegel's anticipation of Desmond's criticisms••
	• Hegel anticipates many of Desmond's criticisms in his own work, particularly in the Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences.
	• He refutes the charge of pantheism in his philosophy of religion lectures.

â€¢ ••Desmond's unclear stance on Hegel's self-completing immanence••
	• It is unclear from Desmond's exposition whether he regards Hegel's self-completing immanence as divine in nature or mere human self-transcendence.

â€¢ ••The significance of Hegel's philosophy today••
	• Hegel's philosophy remains significant today, despite criticisms from scholars like Desmond.
	• His philosophy continues to be debated and interpreted in various theological and philosophical traditions.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Hegel's God and Reality••
	• Desmond argues that Hegel's God is either pantheistic or atheistic
	• Hegel's God is universal humanity writ large
	• Pantheism and atheism/humanism are the same
• ••Hegel's Holism••
	• Desmond interprets Hegel's holism as a philosophy of immanence or mystical monism
	• This is a gross caricature, according to the author
	• Hegel's holism is actually a whole that comprises parts, with genuine difference and otherness
• ••God as the Whole••
	• God is the whole in which everything finite comes into being and passes away
	• God is the whole in which time and history transpire
	• God becomes concretely self-determined in this whole
• ••Advaitic Holism••
	• Hegel's holism offers an alternative to monism and dualism
	• It is advaitic, not two and not one
	• This alternative is lost in Desmond's analysis
• ••Desmond's Interpretation of Hegel's Logic••
	• Desmond interprets Hegel's logical formula as S(SO), meaning the subject is the relation of subject and object
	• This is not accurate, according to the author
	• Hegel's logical formula is actually UPI, meaning a universal principle is mediated with an individual subject by a particular quality
• ••Hegel's Logic as Syllogism••
	• Hegel's logic is a syllogism that preserves identity and difference
	• The third term in the syllogism is not the same as the first
	• It is something new, emerging out of the moment of particularity or difference
• ••Hegel's Logic as Ontological Process••
	• Hegel's logic describes an ontological process in which ideality and reality interact
	• Spirit emerges from this process
	• The triple mediation of Hegel's logic is lost in Desmond's analysis
• ••Desmond's Analysis of Hegel's Trinity••
	• Desmond regards Hegel's Trinity as counterfeit
	• This is because the third term in the Trinity is simply the self-recognition of the first in the second
	• The first is the human self projecting itself into infinity
• ••Desmond's Resistance to Hegelian Dialectic••
	• Desmond's analysis represents a resistance to Hegelian dialectic and the logic of mediation
	• This resistance is similar to charges brought against Hegel in his own time
• ••Implications of Desmond's Analysis••
	• Desmond's analysis reduces panentheism to pantheism, which reduces to atheism
	• This is a misinterpretation of Hegel's philosophy, according to the author

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Trinity is not orthodox••
	• Based on philosophical and heterodox sources, not traditional Christian doctrine
	• Comprises God and the world together, not just three divine Persons

â€¢ ••Immanent Trinity vs. Inclusive Trinity••
	• Desmond argues that Hegel's primary focus is on the inclusive Trinity, which includes God and the world
	• This is distinct from the immanent Trinity, which is God's ideal self-relations

â€¢ ••God-world relation is asymmetrical••
	• Desmond argues that God's relation to the world cannot be made symmetrical with the world's relation to God
	• This means that God's relation to the world has a character of hyperbolic goodness, or gratuitous descent into finitude

â€¢ ••Kenosis and agapeic character••
	• Desmond believes that Hegel's kenosis (self-emptying) is not genuinely agapeic (self-giving)
	• Hegel's view of the God-world relation is not based on a gratuitous, non-necessary self-giving

â€¢ ••Infinite and finite relations••
	• The relationship between the infinite and finite is irreversible, with the infinite having primacy
	• The finite can only be related to the infinite negatively, and affirmatively to itself

â€¢ ••Religious relationship and self-negation••
	• The religious relationship involves a negation of self as finite and an elevation of self into the divine milieu
	• This is not based on human self-projection, but on the divine descent into finitude

â€¢ ••God's self-manifestation••
	• God gives or manifests Godself, and does not withdraw into inaccessible mystery
	• Religion is a begetting of the divine spirit, not an invention of human beings

â€¢ ••Speculative theology and logical articulation••
	• Desmond believes that Hegel's speculative theology grasps the truth of God's self-manifestation
	• This truth is not something that lies hyperbolically beyond logical articulation

â€¢ ••Asymmetry in interhuman relations••
	• Asymmetry is an essential aspect of reciprocal recognition in interhuman relations
	• This is similar to the asymmetry present in the God-world relation

â€¢ ••Hegel's view of the God-world relation••
	• Hegel's view of the God-world relation is not based on a symmetrical, homogenous relationship
	• Instead, it is based on an irreversible primacy of the infinite over the finite.

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of God••
	• God is a spiritual process, not a static entity
	• God is a coming-to-be and a bringing-into-being
	• God's nature is creative, both erotic and agapeic

â€¢ ••Desmond's Critique of Hegel••
	• Desmond dismisses Hegel's use of "entlassen" (release) as insignificant
	• Desmond believes Hegel's thought is too focused on self-return and lacks robust otherness

â€¢ ••Hegel's Concept of Entlassen••
	• "Entlassen" means to loosen or let go, from which Hegel's concept of the absolute as utterly relational derives
	• The other that is released as other is not cut off from the divine oikonomia

â€¢ ••The Nature of Creatio Ex Nihilo••
	• Creatio ex nihilo means creatio ex Deo (creation from God)
	• The nihil from which finite things are created is the pure possibility of being that subsists within God

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Creator and Creation••
	• The difference between creator and creation does not arise from ontological dualism
	• The nihil is the inexhaustible source out of which God releases otherness to become something free and independent

â€¢ ••The Problem of Evil••
	• Desmond believes evil remains external to God
	• Hegel's view introduces tragedy into the divine life

â€¢ ••The Real and Counterfeit God••
	• The God who is totally other, the sovereign Lord beyond all thought, is the counterfeit
	• The real God is the revelatory, self-giving God, knowing godself in our knowing

â€¢ ••Interpretation of Christian Theology••
	• Desmond's interpretation of Christian theology is a valid option
	• There are many who share his criticisms and suspicions of Hegel

â€¢ ••The Dispute Between Desmond and Hegel••
	• Two fundamentally different paradigms are at play in the dispute
	• Both offer true insight into the reality of God and religion, but both have limitations

â€¢ ••The Importance of Openness and Broad Interpretation••
	• The texts are difficult and can be read in different ways
	• Rather than narrowing the range of acceptable readings, we should open it up and affirm the broad stream that has flowed from the beginning

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Theology••
	• Hegel's account of reconciliation in the world is selective and speculative
	• He attempts to construct a unitary history of religion
	• His theology is suffocatingly tied to philosophy

â€¢ ••Challenges and Deficits••
	• Hegel's emphasis on unity may obscure difference
	• His theology may be beholden to philosophy, particularly Hegel's own philosophy

â€¢ ••Resources for Theology••
	• Hegel's philosophy offers valuable resources for theology
	• The focus of this book is on exploring these resources

â€¢ ••Desmond's Critique••
	• Desmond, a critic of Hegel, also draws on Hegel's thought
	• Desmond's concept of "hyperbolic goodness" bridges the gap between infinite and finite

â€¢ ••Metaxology vs. Dialectics••
	• Desmond's metaxology thinks from the "between" of difference, not the "through" of mediation
	• This approach may require a "through" to be effective

â€¢ ••Hegel's Stream of Interpretation••
	• Hegel's theology is part of a broader stream of interpretation that privileges unity over difference
	• This stream includes both orthodoxy and heterodoxy

â€¢ ••Heterodoxy and Ontotheology••
	• Hegel's speculative theology has been criticized as heterodoxy and ontotheology
	• However, Hegel's heterodox conception of spirit (Geist) can overcome the negative aspects of metaphysical orthodoxy

â€¢ ••Orthodoxy and Heterodoxy••
	• Hegel's project is orthodox in that it reestablishes the conditions of possibility of knowing God
	• However, his heterodox conception of spirit retrieves the truth of orthodoxy

â€¢ ••The Challenge of Theological Agnosticism••
	• Hegel's philosophy of religion responds to the challenge of theological agnosticism
	• He argues that God makes godself known, and human cognition can grasp the idea of God

â€¢ ••The Goal of Philosophy of Religion••
	• Hegel's goal is to reintegrate religion, rationality, and life under the conditions of modernity
	• He believes that more of dogmatics has been preserved in philosophy than in theology itself

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Orthodoxy••
	• Hegel declares his orthodoxy in his philosophy
	• He critiques the theologies of his time, which he sees as abandoning the project of knowing God
	• He argues that philosophy now retains more of the teachings of the church than theology does

â€¢ ••Critique of Theological Rationalism••
	• Theological rationalism has relinquished the project of knowing God
	• Its exegesis twists scripture to fit its own preconceptions
	• Hegel sees this as a failure of theology to engage with the divine

â€¢ ••Critique of Theological Historicism••
	• Theological historicism is only interested in what was once believed
	• It lacks assets of its own and relies on the past
	• Hegel sees this as a failure of theology to engage with the present

â€¢ ••Critique of Theological Agnosticism••
	• Theological agnosticism abandons dogmatics for ethics
	• Hegel argues that this view corrupts religion by reducing it to moral duties
	• He sees faith as properly located in the objective truth of God, not subjective feeling

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Faith••
	• Hegel argues that faith is not properly located in subjective feeling
	• He sees faith as a rational and objective understanding of God
	• He rejects the idea that faith is a projection of subjective needs and desires

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of the Trinity••
	• Hegel discusses the doctrine of the Trinity in relation to Valentinian Gnosticism
	• He argues that the apparent distinction between the divine One and the Logos is no distinction at all
	• He sees the Logos as remaining within the bosom of God, not entering into the materiality of the world

â€¢ ••Hegel's Rejection of Gnosticism••
	• Hegel rejects the spirit-matter dualism of Gnosticism
	• He argues that spirit is at home with itself in the sensible world
	• He sees Gnosticism as a rejection of the natural world

â€¢ ••Hegel's Influence of Gnosticism••
	• Hegel's metanarrative reflects the influence of a Gnostic-mystical form of apocalyptic
	• The various phases of divine manifestation are constitutive of divine identity and personhood
	• Narrative reaches into the inner divine life, which undergoes a process of perfecting

â€¢ ••Hegel's Experimentation with Christian Doctrine••
	• Hegel does not shrink from experimenting with Christian doctrine
	• He considers the possibility that Christianity will be superseded by a new folk religion or a philosophical religion of the spirit
	• He realizes that religions are not invented by philosophers, but are shaped by historical and cultural forces

â€¢ ••Themes of This Book••
	• The book explores the themes of divine history, process, and suffering in Hegel's theology
	• It examines how Hegel's novelty plays out in a theology of divine history, process, and suffering
	• It considers the implications of Hegel's experimentation with Christian doctrine for our understanding of God and the world.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Approach to Theology••
	• Hegel rejects traditional categories of classical trinitarianism and christology
	• He seeks to understand Christianity in its historical context, rather than imposing abstract categories

â€¢ ••Gnosticism and Hegel••
	• Baur suggested a connection between ancient Gnosticism and modern Protestant philosophies (Boehme, Schelling, Schleiermacher, Hegel)
	• Gnosticism's theory of redemptive knowledge and universal historical process has a legitimate place in Christian theology, according to Baur

â€¢ ••Gnosticism vs. Hegel••
	• Gnosticism is antithetical to Hegel's philosophy, with a metaphysical dualism and a determinism that excludes moral freedom
	• Hegel's philosophy is holistic, not dualistic, and affirms the positivity and spirituality of Christianity

â€¢ ••Hegel's Christology••
	• Hegel's Christology is not docetic, as the redeemer figure is embodied and suffers the degrading death of crucifixion
	• God becomes incarnate as an individual, sensible, human being in Hegel's philosophy

â€¢ ••Theological Significance of Hegel Today••
	• Hegel's philosophy offers a new perspective on Christianity, one that is not bound by traditional categories
	• He recognizes the need for a deconstruction of representational forms and a reconstruction in conceptual language

â€¢ ••Ontotheology••
	• Ontotheology is a name for traditional metaphysical theology, which makes claims about the ontological status of God
	• Hegel accepts Kant's critique of metaphysical theology, but still makes ontological claims about God

â€¢ ••Hegel's Distinctive Construal of Ontotheology••
	• Hegel pushes past the boundaries of orthodoxy on key issues, including ontotheology
	• He does not embrace the term "ontotheology" as such, but makes ontological claims about God

â€¢ ••The Challenge of Reinterpretation••
	• Hegel recognizes the need for reinterpretation of Christianity in the face of changing historical and cultural contexts
	• He is convinced that refusing the challenge of reinterpretation will lead to fossilization of Christianity

â€¢ ••The Courage to Go into the Water••
	• Hegel calls for the courage to go into the water, to take up the challenge of reinterpretation
	• This requires a willingness to push past the boundaries of orthodoxy and to engage with the complexities of human experience

â€¢ ••Hegel's Legacy••
	• Hegel's philosophy continues to be relevant today, offering a new perspective on Christianity and its relationship to the world
	• His emphasis on the importance of reinterpretation and the need for a holistic understanding of spirit and matter remains a powerful challenge to traditional theology.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Hegel's Ontotheology••
	• Hegel affirms the ontological proof as the only valid proof for God's existence.
	• He replaces traditional metaphysics with a postmetaphysical ontotheology that introduces a new category: spirit.
• ••God's Being••
	• God's being is not that of pure immediacy, abstract substance, or the supreme being.
	• God's being is that of spirit, characterized by energy, movement, life, mind, manifestation, and self-revealing.
• ••Spirit as Act••
	• Spirit is an act or narrative of self-revealing and self-realizing that moves through dialectical spirals.
	• This process establishes divine inter-subjectivity and brings all that exists into being.
• ••Hegel's Theology••
	• Hegel offers theology, not theosophy, which involves direct contemplative contact with divinity.
	• His theology is characterized by the hard labor of disciplined thought and the unity of thought and being in spirit.
• ••The Ontological Proof••
	• The true concept contains objectivity within itself and is alive and active.
	• The true concept is spirit, which corresponds to the final category of the logic and the system.
• ••God as Absolute Spirit••
	• God is absolute spirit, which means God assumes the determinate existence of humanity and becomes involved in the brokenness of the world.
	• This represents a sharp break with the metaphysical tradition.
• ••Critique of Ontotheology••
	• Emmanuel Levinas critiques ontotheology for obscuring the difference between being as an event and beings as worldly entities.
	• Heidegger regards ontotheology as the fate of the Western metaphysical tradition.
• ••Levinas' Question••
	• Levinas asks whether ontotheology's mistake consists in taking being for God or taking God for being.
	• Heidegger assumes the former, while Levinas suggests that ontotheology takes God for being.
• ••Hegel's Marginality••
	• Hegel belongs to the heterodox margins of the ontotheological tradition.
	• His marginality does not take him outside the tradition, but rather represents a unique perspective within it.
• ••Implications••
	• Hegel's ontotheology represents a sharp break with the metaphysical tradition.
	• His theology emphasizes the unity of thought and being in spirit, which is characterized by energy, movement, and self-revealing.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's approach to God and being••
	• Differed from Heidegger and Levinas
	• Reconnected being and God, rather than disconnecting them

â€¢ ••Hegel's concept of God••
	• God is absolute/absolving spirit
	• God is both substance and subject, power and person, life and mind
	• God is the universal that embraces all otherness and difference

â€¢ ••Hegel's concept of God's relationships••
	• God has real, reciprocal, mutually affective relations with the world
	• The other that God releases is something genuinely other than God
	• Finitude becomes a moment in the development of spirit

â€¢ ••Hegel's concept of recognition••
	• Recognition preserves both identity and difference
	• Recognition constitutes a community (or kingdom) of freedom

â€¢ ••Hegel's concept of God's inclusivity••
	• God encompasses what is not-God within God
	• God preserves it as not-God
	• God does not exclude space, time, and finitude but includes and saves them

â€¢ ••Levinas' critique of Hegel's holism••
	• Levinas charges that Hegel's holism is totalizing
	• A totality is not simply a blank sameness but the relativity in which beings are co-present

â€¢ ••Hegel's concept of the whole or universal••
	• Subjectivity is not subordinated to but formed by systemic relations
	• Subjectivity is always already intersubjectivity

â€¢ ••Hegel's concept of co-dependent origination••
	• Co-dependent origination means that subjectivity is always already intersubjectivity
	• This is in contrast to Levinas' view that subjectivity is subordinated to structure

â€¢ ••Hegel's concept of the divine externalization••
	• The divine externalization is the logical idea releasing itself into the externality of nature and finite spirit
	• This is not a dualism that externally limits God or makes God one of two

â€¢ ••Hegel's concept of the kingdom of God••
	• The kingdom of God is a community of recognition and freedom
	• It is a place where God's infinite subjectivity is realized

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Levinas' Subjectivity••
	• Depends on exteriority, disconnection, and mutual transcendence
	• Truth is a form of contact without participation
	• One person is not defined by the recognition of another

â€¢ ••Contrast with Hegel••
	• Levinas and Hegel have different views on the absoluteness of the infinite God
	• Levinas believes the absolute absolves itself from relation, preserving its transcendence
	• Hegel believes the absolute releases itself into real relations with the other

â€¢ ••Levinas' Atomism••
	• Individuals stand out in themselves, breaking through and rending their forms
	• This is symbolized by the nudity of the face
	• Levinas' atomism is evident in his rejection of the privileging of unity

â€¢ ••Hegel's Social Ontology••
	• Hegel's system is holistic and social, rather than atomistic
	• He believes the absolute is realized in communion with the other
	• This is in contrast to Levinas' emphasis on exteriority and disconnection

â€¢ ••Charges against Hegel••
	• Levinas accuses Hegel of removing all multiplicity from the infinite
	• He believes Hegel's absolute excludes every other that might limit it
	• Levinas questions whether Hegel allows for mystery and transcendence in God

â€¢ ••Hegel's Defence••
	• Others have construed his system more rigidly than he himself does
	• Hegel engages in conceptual play and recognizes the need for an apophatic element in theology
	• His dialectic is a form of negative thinking that keeps knowledge open and fluid

â€¢ ••Hegel's Relationship with Reality••
	• Empirical reality stands over against thought for Hegel
	• Reality is not totally sublated by thought, but rather corresponds to it

â€¢ ••The Hegelian System••
	• The system comprises a correspondence, not an identity, between logic and the philosophies of the real
	• The system is closed in respect to its categorical structure, but open to the real

â€¢ ••Levinas' Critique of Hegel••
	• Levinas believes Hegel's system is too rigid and dogmatic
	• He accuses Hegel of failing to recognize the need for mystery and transcendence in God

â€¢ ••Implications for Theology••
	• The contrast between Levinas and Hegel has significant implications for theology
	• It raises questions about the nature of the absolute, the role of the other, and the relationship between thought and reality.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Concept of Otherness••
	• Distinguishes between logical and empirical otherness
	• Logical otherness refers to God's self-othering (immanent Trinity)
	• Empirical otherness refers to the broken free other (economic Trinity)

â€¢ ••The Relationship between God and the World••
	• For Hegel, the economic Trinity subsumes the immanent Trinity
	• History is constitutive of the divine life, not a side-show
	• God's self-othering is a condition for empirical relations

â€¢ ••The Hegelian Totality••
	• Is the antithesis of sameness and totalitarian hegemony
	• Is a non-foundationalist threefold mutual mediation
	• Each element assumes the middle position and is mediated by the others

â€¢ ••The Syllogism and Rationality••
	• The syllogism is the key to rationality for Hegel
	• The three elements of a syllogism are U (universal principle), P (particular quality), and I (individual subject)
	• The three figures of a syllogism are UPI, UIP, and PUI

â€¢ ••The Triple Syllogism••
	• Applies the syllogism to the three branches of philosophy: logic, nature, and spirit
	• Formulates the triple syllogism as UPI, UIP, and PUI
	• Each premise becomes a conclusion and is thus proved

â€¢ ••The Natural Mediation••
	• The logical idea is the universal principle of nature
	• Spirit is embedded in nature as pre-self or other-than-self
	• The logical idea is the principle of spirit

â€¢ ••The Spiritual Mediation••
	• The logical idea is the principle of spirit
	• Nature has its telos in spirit
	• Nature is raised to its essence in the logical idea

â€¢ ••The Logical Mediation••
	• Nature has its principle in the logical idea
	• Spirit has its principle in the logical idea
	• Nature and spirit are independent but connected

â€¢ ••The Relationship between God, Nature, and Humanity••
	• God is the universal rational principle (logical idea)
	• God is the ground of nature and finite spirit
	• God mediates between nature and finite spirit, preventing their collapse

â€¢ ••The Implications of the Triple Syllogism••
	• There is no single master syllogism and no single order among the members
	• The triple syllogism shows that God is the ground of nature and finite spirit, but also mediates between them.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God as Totality••
	• God is the whole totality, not a point of indifference
	• God is the luminosity in which colors are discriminated
	• God is the inclusive or holistic Trinity, a spiral of concentric relations

â€¢ ••God's Relationship to Nature and Finite Spirit••
	• God is beginning, middle, and end
	• God is God only in relation to what is not-God, nature and finite spirit
	• God does not cease to be God, but is God only in relation to a multifaceted totality

â€¢ ••The Syllogism and Rationality••
	• The three members of the totality cannot collapse into sameness
	• Without the syllogism, there is no rationality, no living process

â€¢ ••Hegel's Concept of Totality••
	• Hegel's totality is to be construed holistically, as a Ganzheit
	• This is in contrast to totalizing philosophies, such as Schelling's philosophy of identity

â€¢ ••God as Substance and Spirit••
	• God is the substance or essence upon which everything depends for its existence
	• God is an abiding unity in which distinctions are already potentially present

â€¢ ••Abundant Universality vs. Collective Totality••
	• God is not a mere soil out of which distinctions grow subsequently
	• God is an abundant, overflowing universal, not a collective totality

â€¢ ••Critique of Pantheism••
	• Hegel rejects crude pantheism, which identifies God with the empirical world
	• God is not everything, but the All that remains utterly one

â€¢ ••Absolute Knowledge••
	• Absolute knowledge is not definitive cosmology or metaphysics
	• It is an unending quest through countless determinate negations

â€¢ ••The Nature of Absolute Spirit••
	• Absolute spirit is the determinacy that encompasses all determinations
	• It is the deepest and last of the determinations of unity

â€¢ ••The Chief Interest of Philosophy••
	• The chief interest of philosophy is the manner in which unity becomes determinate
	• The deepest and last of the determinations of unity is that of absolute spirit

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's concept of the infinite••
	• Must include the finite within itself
	• Does not exclude that which limits or stands over against it
	• Includes the finite within its own being

â€¢ ••Levinas' critique of Hegel's infinite••
	• Levinas sees Hegel's infinite as just another finite, limited by its own exteriority
	• Levinas' infinite is a radical exteriority, with God as wholly other

â€¢ ••The relationship between infinite and finite••
	• Must be conceived as one of radical exteriority (Levinas)
	• Can be seen as a dynamic, self-transformative relationship (Hegel)

â€¢ ••Hegel's metaphors for creation••
	• Linguistic-juridical act: God speaks or posits a division in empirical reality
	• Physical image: releasing or letting go, allowing the world to exist as something free and independent

â€¢ ••The nature of absolute knowledge••
	• Is like philosophy's wistful love of wisdom (eros, not stasis)
	• Is dynamic, self-transformative, and never locked into any single unchanging form

â€¢ ••The divine play of love••
	• Becomes serious in the sense of undergoing separation and rupture
	• Requires an inner differentiation within the divine life

â€¢ ••The doctrine of creation••
	• God creates absolutely, out of nothing
	• Human production requires a material with which to work, but divine production is sheer inward vitality

â€¢ ••The natural world••
	• Is de-divinized, rendered prosaic in contrast to the divine sublimity
	• Is good because it is created out of God's own potency rather than out of primordial matter

â€¢ ••Hegel's concept of creation ex nihilo••
	• Means that God creates out of nothing other than God
	• Is a non-emanationist concept, where God is the subject and is simply intuitive, infinite activity

â€¢ ••The implications of Hegel's concept of creation••
	• Challenges traditional notions of the relationship between God and the world
	• Offers a dynamic, self-transformative understanding of the divine and the world

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Creation••
	• God is an inexhaustible creative fecundity
	• Releases potential divisions and distinctions within divinity
	• Actualizes itself in the world

â€¢ ••Contrast with Levinas••
	• Levinas emphasizes divine contraction or withdrawal
	• Leaves space for a created order other than God
	• Levinas' view is influenced by the Kabbalah

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Infinity••
	• Infinity does not close in on itself, but expands and enacts itself
	• Expands in an open spiral, rather than a closed circle

â€¢ ••Society and Difference••
	• Hegel's view of society is primary and constitutive
	• Genuine difference contributes to a diverse totality
	• Levinas prioritizes the asymmetry of the ethical relationship over society

â€¢ ••The World and Its Truth••
	• The world is real, but its truth is its ideality
	• The world's destiny is to take itself back into the divine idea
	• This is not a Gnostic denigration of the material world, but a classical Christian theme

â€¢ ••God's Relationship to the World••
	• God releases the world, but also holds on to it and preserves it
	• This is the work of divine wisdom
	• God encompasses what is not-God within God

â€¢ ••Hegel's Holism••
	• Offers a vision of God as the encompassing, inexhaustible sphere of all that is
	• Includes the intellectual and material, eternal and temporal, infinite and finite
	• Nothing is outside of God, not even the most trivial or despicable

â€¢ ••Postmodern Critique of Holism••
	• Holism obscures real differences, power struggles, and conflicts
	• Postmodern thinkers like Derrida and Foucault reject holism and find no possibility of achieving justice or redemption

â€¢ ••Religious Orthodoxy's Critique of Holism••
	• Holism compromises the transcendence and omnipotence of God
	• This is the view of thinkers like Desmond

â€¢ ••The Significance of Hegel's View••
	• Offers a vision of God as the encompassing, inexhaustible sphere of all that is
	• Provides a framework for understanding the relationship between God and the world
	• Remains relevant today, despite postmodern and religious critiques

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Holism and the Spirit••
	• Holism is rejected when done for the sake of God or the world
	• A holism of the spirit honors the generative power of God and the wealth of the world
	• Keeps God and the world connected, preventing isolation and decay

â€¢ ••Language and History••
	• Language expresses relations synchronically (not dependent on time)
	• History expresses relations diachronically (through a sequence of events)
	• Hegel resolves the tension between language and history through narrative

â€¢ ••Narrative and Representation••
	• Representation is an ontological category, pointing to a divine action
	• God is both the subject and object of representation
	• Representation has two forms: sensible and nonsensible

â€¢ ••Sensible and Nonsensible Representation••
	• Sensible representation expresses contents in historical narratives (mythical or factual)
	• Elements exist alongside each other as independent entities in narrative
	• Representation is not yet able to hold disparate elements together

â€¢ ••Thought and Conceptual Grasp••
	• Thought is the highest form of language, ideal and synchronous
	• Thought draws upon materials furnished by representation
	• Thought loses contact with life experience if it cuts itself off from representation

â€¢ ••Theological Significance of Hegel••
	• An aspect of narrative is always present in thought, especially in religious thought
	• Hegel's philosophy is relevant to postmodern theology and philosophy

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of History••
	• Hegel rejects historicism's focus on the past (Historie)
	• God as absolute spirit is geschichtlich, an eternal process of self-distinguishing and self-reintegrating

â€¢ ••Eternal History and Becoming••
	• The eternal history is an eternal movement, a process of becoming
	• Becoming requires temporality, movement through time
	• Time is introduced into the logic and the immanent divine life

â€¢ ••Hegel's Eternity••
	• Hegel's eternity is not antihistorical, but rather a primordial and infinite process
	• Eternity is a more fundamental sense of time, not a rejection of it

â€¢ ••Influence and Relevance••
	• Hegel's philosophy has influenced postmodern theology and philosophy
	• His ideas on narrative, representation, and eternity remain relevant today

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Eternity and Time••
	• In eternity, time modes coexist and complete each other without collapsing into sameness.
	• God's eternal presence comprises a tense interplay of past and future.

â€¢ ••God's Nature••
	• God is eternally becoming what God already is.
	• God incorporates novelty that occurs in nature and history.

â€¢ ••Narrative and Time••
	• A true narrative expresses ideal, logical, or spiritual relations in terms of a story.
	• The story aspect is not extrinsic but reveals something about the ideal relations themselves.

â€¢ ••Hegel's Speculative Redescription••
	• Hegel offers a speculative redescription of the Christian metanarrative.
	• The inclusive Trinity encompasses God and the world together, overcoming classical dualism.

â€¢ ••Narrativity in the Divine Life••
	• The Trinity introduces narrativity into the divine life, overcoming static conceptions of eternity.
	• Narrative is a discursive projection of logical space.

â€¢ ••Demystification of Narrative••
	• Narrative must be demystified to grasp the primal logical structure expressing itself in it.
	• Sequential, spatially-temporally diffused metaphors and symbols must be thought through.

â€¢ ••Hegel's View of Narrative••
	• Narrative is logicized, and logic is narratized.
	• Narrative space is a discursive projection of logical space.

â€¢ ••Denarratizing Operators••
	• Hegel uses denarratizing operators to remove temporal sequence, contingency, and voluntarism from divine activity.
	• These operators break the narrative order of the master syllogism.

â€¢ ••The Triple Syllogism••
	• The triple syllogism means that the metanarrative comprises a genuine interplay of God, nature, and humanity.
	• God, the absolute idea, remains the ultimate subject of becoming.

â€¢ ••The Perdurance of Narrative••
	• Narrative perdures conceptually, transcending popular religious imagination and overcoming rationalism/atheism.
	• Logico-conceptual space is a kind of contracted narrative, while narrative is an expanded syllogism.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Revisionist View of Knowledge••
	• Challenges traditional rationalism and empiricism
	• Has more in common with mystical and esoteric traditions
	• Sees knowledge as a complex and nuanced process

â€¢ ••Postmodernity and the Loss of Metanarrative••
	• Assumes the loss of any legitimate metanarrative
	• Reality has no intelligibility
	• Hegel sees postmodernity as indulging in hypercritical dogmatism

â€¢ ••Hegel's Reconstruction of Narrative••
	• Includes the terror of tragedy along with the triumph of redemption
	• Sees tragedy and redemption as intertwined
	• Challenges the idea of a simple, optimistic narrative

â€¢ ••Tragedy and Redemption in Hegel's Worldview••
	• Sees tragedy as a necessary part of human existence
	• Affirms the crucified God as a symbol of tragic suffering
	• Identifies divine love with death and reconciliation

â€¢ ••Human Nature and the Condition of Evil••
	• Sees human being as an internally unresolved contradiction
	• Evil requires the ability to decide and choose
	• Humans must undergo a cleavage or separation to realize their spiritual potential

â€¢ ••The Role of Knowledge and Cognition••
	• Knowledge both makes and wounds a human being
	• Cognition posits the antithesis in which evil is found
	• Estrangement and reconciliation have the same source: consciousness

â€¢ ••Social and Religious History as Tragic••
	• Sees history as a tragic process of human development
	• Critiques the reduction of everything to subjectivity and self-satisfaction
	• Identifies modern decadence with profanization, privatization, and loss of common life

â€¢ ••The Problem of Evil and Violence••
	• Evil assumes a dynamism that outstrips the capacity of reason
	• Absurdity and chaos point to a dimension of the irrational and uncontrollable
	• Challenges the idea that spirit can undo evil

â€¢ ••The Power of Spirit••
	• Affirms that spirit has the power to undo evil
	• Sees spirit as a power greater than evil and greater than death
	• Challenges the tendency to totalize death and cynicism

â€¢ ••The Complexity of Things••
	• Recognizes the actual complexity of things
	• Challenges comfortable cynicism and the tendency to oversimplify
	• Encourages a nuanced and open-ended understanding of human existence

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Evil can be undone••
	• Its effects can be reversed
	• Deeds remain as part of an inexpungeable legacy
	• Remembering and honoring victims leads to transcendence

â€¢ ••Spirit is the power of rebirth••
	• Inexhaustible movement that reconciles opposing forces
	• Establishes new connections
	• Power of God

â€¢ ••Reconciliation and redemption are central themes of religion••
	• Overcome prior estrangement
	• Liberate from attachment to idols and bondage
	• Bring forth unity of God and humanity

â€¢ ••God's involvement in history is focused on the figure of Christ••
	• Christian religion emphasizes Christ's role
	• Hegel's theology of spirit provides a different perspective on christology

â€¢ ••Incarnation is the actualized unity of divine and human nature••
	• Must appear in history
	• Must come forth from the godhead into the anguish of history
	• Spirit is the absolute power to endure this anguish

â€¢ ••Freedom can be understood in two ways••
	• Finding true identity in the divine identity (Hegel's understanding)
	• Being utterly claimed by the divine difference (e.g. Buddhist, Daoist, Nietzschean perspectives)

â€¢ ••Hegel's christology is orthodox in some respects••
	• Universal is an abstraction apart from its concrete instantiations
	• Normative instantiation must be singular

â€¢ ••Hegel's construal of incarnation is not orthodox••
	• Incarnation as appearance or revelation is not traditional
	• Having a literal divine nature is not what makes Jesus to be

â€¢ ••The principle of positivity is central to Hegel's thought••
	• Universal is an abstraction apart from its concrete instantiations
	• Normative instantiation must be singular

â€¢ ••Hegel's theology of freedom is situated and relativized by other perspectives••
	• Comparative study of Hegel and Daoism highlights similarities and differences between Hegel's understanding of freedom and other philosophical perspectives.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's focus on Christ••
	• Emphasizes Christ's role as revealer of divinity and mediator of reconciliation
	• Focuses on Christ's teaching and death as key aspects of his message

â€¢ ••Christ's teaching••
	• Not just moral maxims, but a proof of the truth of the divine idea
	• Centers on radical love and revolutionary reversal of established orders

â€¢ ••God's presence in Christ••
	• Only faith can see God present in Christ
	• Christ's story, sayings, and deeds confirm what faith sees

â€¢ ••Christ's death••
	• From a human perspective, a shameful death of a criminal
	• From a faith perspective, the death of God, a divine tragedy

â€¢ ••Resurrection••
	• Not a physical miracle, but a raising up, exaltation, and return to God
	• A communal event, extending the divine divestment into history

â€¢ ••Community of the Spirit••
	• Embodies infinite love arising from infinite anguish
	• Creates a new kind of human bond, renouncing distinctions based on power, position, sex, and wealth

â€¢ ••Intersubjectivity and social existence••
	• The shape of the cross becomes the pattern of community
	• Self and other are redefined in a relationship of compassion and suffering

â€¢ ••Sacramental communion••
	• Symbolizes the mystical union of the community
	• Enacts the sacrificial death of Christ, giving the pattern of communal intersubjectivity

â€¢ ••Levinas vs. Hegel••
	• Levinas sees the self as responding to an infinitely exterior other
	• Hegel sees self-othering as intrinsic to subjectivity-in-process

â€¢ ••Theological significance••
	• Hegel's thought offers a rich description of the community of the Spirit
	• Levinas's thought emphasizes the transcendence of God, while Hegel's thought emphasizes the incarnation and death of God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's view of community••
	• Community does not come into being through mutual recognition and self-determination
	• Instead, community is formed through the asymmetry and absolute demand of the ethical relationship

â€¢ ••Hegel's focus on the ethical relationship••
	• The ethical relationship is the central focus of Hegel's philosophy
	• This relationship is characterized by an absolute demand that transcends sociality

â€¢ ••The movement from heart to church to ethical life••
	• Hegel traces a movement from individual spirituality to community and finally to ethical life
	• This movement is driven by the desire for freedom and the redemption of the world

â€¢ ••The relationship between freedom and community••
	• The freedom of the basileia community passes into social and political freedom
	• This freedom is the telos of world history

â€¢ ••Hegel's views on secular institutions••
	• Hegel has little confidence in the secular institutions of his time
	• He is aware of a pervasive cultural decadence

â€¢ ••Hegel's views on social injustices••
	• Hegel notes the incompatibility of slavery with Christianity
	• However, he does not attend to other social injustices in his lectures

â€¢ ••Hegel's views on the role of religion••
	• Religion is mostly a thing of the past for Hegel
	• He does not glimpse the possibility of a religious renewal through shared ethical struggles

â€¢ ••Andrew Shanks' interpretation of Hegel's philosophy••
	• Shanks argues that Hegel's philosophy is driven by a hegemonic impulse
	• This impulse is aimed at creating an elite community of scholars and civil servants who would build a new social order

â€¢ ••Shanks' views on Hegel's legacy••
	• Shanks believes that Hegel would be a keen advocate of contemporary dissident civil society if he were alive today
	• This would include movements such as environmentalism, feminism, and human rights activism

â€¢ ••The relationship between Hegel's philosophy and pluralism••
	• Hegel's philosophy is characterized by a symphony version of pluralism
	• However, it does not include the jazz version of pluralism, which involves discord and debate.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hegel's Theology of the Spirit••
	• Hegel's philosophy of religion is complex and open to multiple interpretations
	• He emphasizes the unity of God, Christ, and Spirit, but also acknowledges the diversity of historical religions

â€¢ ••Pluralism in Hegel's Thought••
	• Hegel's philosophical history of religion implies a kind of logical deduction of the necessity of religious pluralism
	• He argues that the diversity of historical religions is a necessary consequence of the movement and distinction of the Spirit

â€¢ ••The Consummation of Spirit••
	• Hegel's concept of the consummation of spirit is teleological, but not necessarily hierarchical
	• He argues that the process of the Spirit's self-realization need not culminate in a single perfect exemplification

â€¢ ••The Absolute and Pluralism••
	• The Hegelian absolute can be read as both monolithic and pluralistic
	• The author interprets the absolute as one-in-many and many-in-one, affirming both oneness and manyness

â€¢ ••Inclusivism vs. Pluralism••
	• Hegel's philosophy of religion fits the inclusivist model of interreligious dialogue, but its depth logic is closer to pluralism
	• The author argues that Hegel's dialectical thinking prevents closure on any cultural synthesis

â€¢ ••The Divine Spirit in History••
	• The divine Spirit is always concrete and does not reach its goal in history
	• Christianity is just one of many determinate religions on the path to consummation

â€¢ ••A Theology of Religious Pluralism••
	• The author believes that Hegel would avow a theology of religious pluralism today
	• This theology would emphasize the diversity of cultural trajectories and the work in progress of the religion of freedom

â€¢ ••Hegel's Legacy••
	• Hegel's philosophy contributes to a critically liberal revisioning of theology
	• His legacy includes resources for a theology of community, freedom, Christ, narrativity, and the fractured/mended wholeness of God and world

â€¢ ••Needed Theologies••
	• A theology of nature and a theology of social transformation are needed to complement Hegel's legacy
	• These theologies would build on Hegel's philosophy of nature and his ethics

â€¢ ••Hegel's Relevance Today••
	• Hegel's philosophy remains relevant for contemporary theological debates
	• His ideas on the absolute, the consummation of spirit, and the divine Spirit in history continue to inspire and challenge theologians today

	• Hegel's philosophy of religion offers an alternative to philosophical agnosticism and religious fundamentalism.
	• His thought continues to be relevant in the context of modernity and postmodernity.

	• Can Hegel's view of the rationality of the actual account for radical, irrational evil?

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Hegel's Philosophy of Religion••
	• Explores the relationship between philosophy and religion
	• Examines the concept of religion as a system of thought
	• Discusses the role of reason and faith in understanding religion
• ••Key Concepts••
	• ••Freedom••: a central concept in Hegel's philosophy of religion
	• ••Sublimity••: a characteristic of the divine in Hegel's thought
	• ••Particularity••: the unique aspects of individual religions
• ••Influences and Interactions••
	• ••Kant••: a significant influence on Hegel's philosophy of religion
	• ••Kierkegaard••: a critic of Hegel's philosophy of religion
	• ••Levinas••: a philosopher who engages with Hegel's thought on ethics and religion
• ••Religious Traditions••
	• ••Christianity••: a central focus of Hegel's philosophy of religion
	• ••Judaism••: Hegel's philosophy engages with Jewish thought and tradition
	• ••Asian Religions••: Hegel's philosophy interacts with Asian religious traditions
• ••Philosophical Themes••
	• ••Knowledge and Being••: Hegel's philosophy explores the relationship between knowledge and being
	• ••Mediation••: a key concept in Hegel's system, referring to the process of reconciling opposites
	• ••Necessity••: a central theme in Hegel's philosophy, referring to the rational necessity of historical events
• ••Critiques and Challenges••
	• ••Pantheism••: Hegel is accused of pantheism, but argues that his philosophy is distinct from this position
	• ••Materialism••: Hegel's philosophy is criticized for being materialistic, but he argues that his thought is spiritual and idealistic
	• ••Orthodoxy••: Hegel's philosophy is seen as a challenge to traditional orthodoxy in Christianity
• ••Methodology••
	• ••Phenomenology••: Hegel's philosophy employs a phenomenological approach to understanding religion
	• ••Speculative Reason••: Hegel's philosophy uses speculative reason to understand the nature of reality and the divine
	• ••Dialectical Method••: Hegel's philosophy employs a dialectical method to understand the development of ideas and historical events
• ••Key Works••
	• ••Lectures on the Philosophy of Religion••: a key work in which Hegel develops his philosophy of religion
	• ••Phenomenology of Spirit••: a central work in which Hegel develops his phenomenological approach to understanding consciousness and the divine
	• ••Science of Logic••: a key work in which Hegel develops his logic and metaphysics
• ••Influences and Legacy••
	• ••Feuerbach••: a philosopher who engages with Hegel's thought on the nature of God and religion
	• ••Marx••: a philosopher who critiques Hegel's philosophy and develops his own thought on the relationship between philosophy and politics
	• ••Levinas••: a philosopher who engages with Hegel's thought on ethics and religion
• ••Key Figures••
	• ••Kant••: a significant influence on Hegel's philosophy of religion
	• ••Kierkegaard••: a critic of Hegel's philosophy of religion
	• ••Levinas••: a philosopher who engages with Hegel's thought on ethics and religion

• ••Spirit••
	• Defined as the activity of self-manifesting
	• Can be finite and infinite
	• Evolves through Hegel's philosophy
	• Related to community, love, and nature
	• Can be revelatory and sensible

	• Can be agnostic, apophatic, or kataphatic
	• Can be speculative, rational, or revealed
	• Can be historical or metaphysical
	• Can be natural or cognitive

	• Can know itself and reality
	• Can be mediated through representation
	• Can be related to knowledge of God
• ••Subjectivity and Substance••
	• Can be communal and intersubjective
	• Can be related to exteriority and God
	• Can be a substance or a subject

	• Can be a philosophical triad or a Christian doctrine
	• Can be economic, immanent, or inclusive
	• Can be related to numbers, persons, and the philosophical triad
